Brasilien: Lula als Präsident wiedergewählt

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Brasiliens Präsident Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva hat nach vorläufigen Zahlen die Stichwahl gegen seinen rechten Herausforderer Flávio Bolsonaro gewonnen und sich damit eine weitere Amtszeit gesichert. Der 80-Jährige lag nach Auszählung fast aller Stimmen mit 51,75 Prozent zu 48,25 Prozent uneinholbar vorn, wie das Oberste Wahlgericht mitteilte.

Der unterlegene Bolsonaro erkannte das Wahlergebnis hingegen nicht an. Wegen einer um zwei Stunden verlängerten Öffnungszeit der Wahllokale will er den Urnengang annullieren lassen. Er könne das Wahlergebnis unmöglich anerkennen, sagte der rechte Senator und Sohn des früheren Staatschefs Jair Bolsonaro vor Medien.

Die Führung wechselte im Verlauf der Auszählung mehrfach. Flávio Bolsonaro konnte einen zwischenzeitlichen Rückstand aufholen, fiel anschließend aber wieder hinter Lula zurück. Im Süden der Millionenmetropole Rio de Janeiro waren bei beiden Wendungen laute Jubelrufe, Musik und Feuerwerk zu hören, wie ein Reporter der Deutschen Presse-Agentur berichtete.

Der 80-jährige Lula von der linken Arbeiterpartei PT bewarb sich um eine vierte Amtszeit. Er setzt auf die Fortsetzung seines Regierungskurses mit Sozialprogramme und staatlichen Investitionen. Flávio Bolsonaro, der Sohn des früheren Staatschefs Jair Bolsonaro, warb mit konservativen Positionen und insbesondere einem härteren Vorgehen gegen Kriminalität um Stimmen.

Paramount and Warner Bros merge into one Hollywood giant

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Paramount has completed an $81 billion purchase of Warner Bros. Discovery, as its owner Skydance creates a new entertainment behemoth. Billionaire David Ellison's hostile takeover bid defeated rival efforts from Netflix.

Paramount on Tuesday finalized its takeover of Warner Bros. Discovery, completing a hostile acquisition that was months in the making and almost faltered at several hurdles.

The takeover cost a total of around $81 billion (roughly €72 billion), or almost $111 billion including the Warner Bros. debts passed on as part of the deal.

The merger brings together two of the oldest US filmmaking studios and will form a new giant called Skydance Corporation.

The new name Skydance hails from billionaire investor David Ellison, who first bought out Paramount and its subsidiaries and then moved on to target the much larger fish that is Warner Bros.

The bid faced stiff competition in a bidding war with Netflix, and later scrutiny from US regulators and politicians, but ultimately went through.

"Today is a historic day, not just for Skydance but for our entire industry," said Ellison, whose family has ties to US President Donald Trump.

Skydance shares began trading on the New York Stock Exchange on Tuesday under the ticker SKYD, while Warner Bros. Discovery shareholders will receive about $31 per share.

The deal closed less than a week after a federal judge in California approved a settlement between Paramount and 12 US states that had sued to block the transaction on antitrust grounds.

As well as the two giant film studios, Paramount Pictures and Warner Bros. Pictures, the deal also brings together the CBS News and CNN news networks, and the HBO and CBS television brands. Ellison says he plans to merge the subscription streaming platforms Paramount+ and HBO Max.

Ellison will serve as chairman and CEO of the new company. Former Mattel boss Ynon Kreiz is operating as co-CEO in charge of day-to-day operations.

Skydance Corp. looks set to have a total revenue in the region of $70 billion, based on the performance to date of its component parts. It says it's targeting cost savings of around $6 billion within three years as it takes advantage of overlaps created by the merger.

Nobel Prize in literature goes to Canada's Anne Carson

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Carson won for her "bold and inventive oeuvre." The decorated poet and essayist is known for her reverence for Latin and Greek classics.

The Swedish Academy awarded the 2026 Nobel Prize in literature to Anne Carson on Thursday, praising her "bold and inventive oeuvre."

Her work has "in playful dialogue with the classical tradition...created new forms for contemporary literature," said Ingrid Carlberg, permanent secretary of the academy.

The 76-year-old writer is a longtime professor of classics, creative writing, and comparative literature at prestigious institutions like McGill and Princeton universities. She is only the 19th woman of 123 laureates to win the award.

Born in Toronto in 1950, Carson moved with her family among several small towns in Canada during her youth. She has credited a beloved Latin teacher for sparking her interest in language and classics.

Her first book of criticism, 1986’s Eros the Bittersweet, was published to great acclaim and has had significant influence on other classicists, poets, and even film and television. Carson has also published her own poetry such as the 2001 collection The Beauty of the Husband as well as highly regarded translation of ancient Greek poets such as Sappho and Sophocles.

Carson is also heavily influenced by the King James Bible and more modern writers including Emily Dickinson and Virginia Woolf. She has received numerous awards such as the T.S. Eliot Prize for Poetry, a MacArthur fellowship, and the PEN/Nabokov Award.

Deeply private, Carson often declines to speak about her personal life in interviews and discourages autobiographical interpretations of her work. However, she did reveal in 2024 that she has Parkinson's disease.

The winner of the Nobel Prize in literature receives prize money of 12 million Swedish kronor, equivalent to about $1.2 million. The winner also receives a medal and a diploma at an official ceremony in December, and is expected to deliver a speech known as a Nobel lecture.

On Friday, the winner of the Nobel Peace Prize will be announced, followed by the Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel on Monday.

Elizabeth Holmes doc You Can See Everything rivals Clayface at box office with huge takings

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Clayface, a new horror movie about the Batman villain of the same name, sat comfortably at No. 1 on its debut weekend – but the Nathan Fielder documentary wasn’t far behind

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The Elizabeth Holmes documentary You Can See Everything gave DC Universe’s Clayface a run for its money at the box office this weekend.

Clayface, a horror movie about the Batman villain of the same name, sat comfortably at No. 1 on its debut weekend, taking in $24 million from 3,458 North American theaters. But You Can See Everything wasn’t far behind – again defying box office expectations.

The documentary about the disgraced Theranos founder, by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, collected an impressive $19.5 million while expanding to 2,655 venues on its second weekend, according to Rentrak, which compiles box office data. So far, the movie has made $45 million in total domestically.

The film begins 34 days before Holmes surrenders herself at the Federal Prison Camp in Bryan, Texas, where she’s serving an 11-year sentence after being convicted on four counts of fraud and conspiracy for lying about the capabilities of her biotech company’s blood testing technology.

The movie has been such a success that A24 expanded the film to 2,655 theaters, which makes it an exceptionally wide release for a documentary and more than triple its opening-weekend footprint.

You Can See Everything is the highest-grossing nonmusical documentary since Michael Moore’s Fahrenheit 9/11 in 2004, and reviews have been overwhelmingly positive across the board.

Some commentators are predicting that it could become the first documentary to ever score a best picture nomination at the Oscars.

Clayface was a modest opening for a comic-book adaptation, but it was probably a relief for DC, since the studio has struggled with success at the box office this year.

The studio’s Supergirl was a box office flop in July, taking in $126 million worldwide against production marketing costs of at least $270 million.

ADVERTISEMENT. If you sign up to this service we will earn commission. This revenue helps to fund journalism across The Independent.

In third place in the weekend box office rankings was Wildwood, a stop-motion animation about a teenage girl who searches an enchanted forest for her kidnapped brother. The movie debuted at $11 million this weekend and received rave reviews, but was expensive to make at about $100 million.

Following behind was the romantic fantasy movie Wicker, starring Olivia Colman as a lonely fisherwoman who commissions a basket maker to create her a husband (Alexander Skarsgard), taking in a solid $6 million.

Klara and the Sun, the adaptation of the sci-fi novel of the same name, didn’t have much success on its opening weekend, collecting just $725,000 from 880 theaters. It stars Jenna Ortega and Amy Adams.

Lula wins 4th term as Brazil's president, but Bolsonaro rejects results

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Updated on: October 26, 2026 / 1:36 AM EDT / AP

Brazil's President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, a vocal critic of President Trump, was reelected Sunday in a result that provides relief to critics of Mr. Trump and keeps some distance between Latin America's biggest economy and Washington.

With 100% of the vote counted, Brazil's electoral court announced that the soon to be 81-year-old Lula had a lead of nearly 4.4 million votes over opponent Flávio Bolsonaro, giving him 51.8% of the total vote. Bolsonaro quickly refused to concede defeat and asked for the election results to be annulled after an evening of chaos at some polling places that followed a last-minute decision to extend voting by two hours. That decision by a Supreme Court justice may trigger legal action by Bolsonaro.

Bolsonaro is the son of former President Jair Bolsonaro, whom Lula defeated to take the presidency in 2022.

Lula's triumph bucks a trend in Latin America, where increasing numbers of right-of-center presidential candidates allied with Mr. Trump have been winning, further extending the United States' influence in the region.

Lula's victory leaves Mexico and Brazil, Latin America's two most populous nations, with left-of-center leaders.  It also means the nation with Latin America's largest economy, with a GDP of $2.6 trillion per year, will likely continue defying Mr. Trump's demands, despite the pressure of increased tariffs he imposed.

The announcement kicked off a celebration of supporters in and out of the Sao Paulo hotel where Lula watched the count with first lady Rosângela da Silva, allies, and friends.

Lula's victory, if confirmed by electoral authorities, will give him his non-consecutive fourth term as president. The next term begins on Jan. 5, 2027.

It is the first time since Brazil's military dictatorship ended in 1985 that the second-highest vote-getter in the first-round elections won the presidency. Bolsonaro had 47% of the vote in the first round to Lula's 45%, a difference of 2.2 million votes. However, more than 33 million voters out of nearly 160 million did not turn out in the first round.

A video published by Lula's campaign shows the veteran leader celebrating with first lady Rosângela da Silva and allies after his runoff victory was confirmed by Brazil's electoral court. Wearing star-shaped red glasses, he kissed his wife. Hundreds of his supporters began gathering on São Paulo's main artery to celebrate the victory.

In addressing supporters, Lula expressed his gratitude to the Brazilian people, and pledged to respect all Brazilians, regardless of gender or religion. He also attacked Bolsonaro."

"This was a defeat for denialism and lies. I must confess that every time I attended a debate, I felt embarrassed," he said.

He also doubled down on his commitment to Brazil's sovereignty.

"Either the homeland remains free, or we die for Brazil," he said. "This country will not accept being anyone's backyard."

In Rio de Janiero, cars beeped their horns and a woman dressed in red — the color of Lula supporters — shouted "You lost! Cry harder!" to four men in yellow and green sipping beers, Bolsonaro's signature colors. A huge roar echoed in the street as the result was confirmed.

Rodrigo Valentim, a 51-year-old businessman and supporter of Bolsonaro, said that another Lula government would harm the economy. He pointed to financial markets' positive reaction to the result of the first round, when Bolsonaro bested Lula.

"I don't understand how people can vote for Lula," he said. "The country is collapsing."

Sunday's victory will likely be the last of the veteran leader's political career. Lula began his political career in the 1970s as a union leader. He then helped found the Workers' Party, which he represented as a presidential candidate three times until he finally won it in 2002. He was reelected four years later and was succeeded by one of his cabinet ministers, Dilma Rousseff, in 2010. She was reelected in 2014.

Rousseff was impeached for accounting maneuvering in 2016 amid a sprawling corruption probe, which also ensnared Lula. He tried to run for president again in 2018 but was jailed in March after being convicted.

Lula was released from prison after 580 days and saw Brazil's Supreme Court annul his sentence after police found messages that showed the judge who convicted him was biased. He quickly became one of former President Jair Bolsonaro's biggest critics and narrowly beat him in 2022.

Lula admitted in 2024, two years into the job, that his administration had done little to earn the support of his voters.

Since he lost the first round, Lula reorganized his campaign, appealed to young voters, put millions of Brazilians on the streets to defend democratic values and his legacy, raised fears of another Bolsonaro administration and boosted several government initiatives.

Lula also defended Brazil's sovereignty and insisted that a Bolsonaro presidency would allow the U.S. to exert control over Brazil.

He joins Mr. Trump and Nicaragua's president, Daniel Ortega — all age 80 — as the oldest presidents in the Americas.

The electoral court's announcement ended an evening of confusion at polling places after Brazilian Supreme Court Justice Cristiano Zanin ruled three minutes before the scheduled end of voting to keep polls open for two more hours, following requests by both candidates. Lula's request came after issues with buses that provide transportation to polls in Sao Paulo and Salvador, two cities that tend to favor the incumbent. Bolsonaro's request was due to heavy rains in Brazil's south, where he was favored.

In Brasilia, volunteers who oversaw the vote count at a school debated whether they needed to stay after the announcement to extend voting, but most left 15 to 20 minutes after polls closed and they had given count information to electoral authorities. At a polling station in Rio de Janeiro, the voting machines had already been switched off minutes after polls were set to close and couldn't be turned back on.

The decision delayed tallying the votes by two hours. It was unclear how many polling stations stayed open after the scheduled closing time.

Bolsonaro ally Nikolas Ferreira noted that Zanin, who made the decision, is Lula's former lawyer.

Lula, who first governed Brazil between 2003 and 2010, faced a tough reelection battle because of concerns about the cost of living and security. Bolsonaro made a tough-on-crime approach the cornerstone of his campaign and pledged to bring down food costs.

Both had been hit by corruption suspicions involving family members and allies, with Bolsonaro implicated in a sprawling scandal rocking Brazil. Lula previously spent more than a year in prison on corruption convictions that were eventually annulled.

Bolsonaro's admission that he accepted about $12 million from disgraced former banker Daniel Vorcaro to produce a film on his father's life has cast a shadow over his campaign. He is under investigation for corruption, money laundering and tax evasion in connection with the case.

As Bolsonaro voted at a school in Rio de Janeiro, his supporters shouted "Lula, thief, your place is in prison" while Lula's supporters chanted in his support.

"The time of real change has arrived," Bolsonaro said. "Brazil can't go on for another four years on its current trajectory."

In the lead-up to the vote, Lula frequently portrayed Bolsonaro, whose father is under house arrest for attempting a coup, as a threat to democracy. Bolsonaro has promised to seek an amnesty for his father if elected.

Lula, trying to lure undecided voters, has invested in costly incentives. His government endorsed a debt relief program for small businesses and promised free public transportation on weekends. A major win then came when the Senate backed a popular proposal to end the six-day workweek.

Lula thanked his voters for an outpouring of support while casting his ballot in the city of Sao Bernardo do Campo, where he began his political career as a union leader in the 1960s. "It is a lot of generosity, a lot of care, a lot of love. I will be forever grateful," Lula said.

Lula voters turned out Sunday driven by his history of supporting marginalized people and working to lift Brazilians out of poverty.

"Lula is a man of the people and he knows what it's like to be poor," Luzia da Souza, who runs a bar in Rio de Janeiro, said. "I find it impossible not to vote for him."

Anderson Falcão, a 43-year-old journalist in Brasilia who sported a red shirt in support of Lula, said he worried about Bolsonaro's long-term political vision, which he called "exclusionary."

"It's a project that harms the less fortunate, harms the worker, women, the forest, the Amazon," he said.

Bolsonaro voters, many of whom wore yellow and green Brazil jerseys in support of the senator, said they wanted change.

"Things are really expensive and taxes are too high," Fabianne Borges Santoro, a 45-year-old physiotherapist in Rio de Janeiro, said. She added that she once voted for Lula's party but now associates it with corruption.

In Bahia state, a stronghold for Lula, whose voters tend to live in areas that depend more heavily on buses, at least 75 public buses were prevented from leaving municipal garages during the presidential runoff vote, according to a state electoral judge. He ordered the arrest of the mobility secretary of the capital, Salvador, Pablo Souza, in relation to the case.

Mayor Bruno Reis, a Bolsonaro ally, blamed trade unionists for illegally sticking Workers' Party materials onto transit vehicles for the measure.

President of Brazil's Superior Electoral Court, Kassio Nunes Marques, confirmed that electoral authorities received a report on problems with buses in Bahia that is being analyzed. He did not confirm if voting would be extended, which Lula requested in Salvador and Sao Paulo, where issues with public transportation were also reported in the city with a mayor allied with Bolsonaro.

"We will not be robbed by mayors, governors or businessmen," Lula told the press. "These people need to respect democracy."

Bolsonaro did not immediately comment on the issue.

International observers earlier in the day said that voting was occurring peacefully and with transparency and did not immediately comment on the incident.

Lula criticized the U.S. president before Brazil's election, speaking about the risks of U.S. intervention in Brazilian elections after the Trump administration imposed high tariffs against Brazilian exports and classified drug-trafficking groups as foreign terrorist organizations. Experts say the moves may have been intended to make Lula look soft on crime.

Bolsonaro has touted his proximity to the White House and insisted that Brazil needs to strike better deals with the Trump administration, including in the rare minerals economy. Brazil estimates that its deposits hold up to 21 million tons, second only to China. He denies he's being helped by the U.S. government.

Mr. Trump said Sunday that he was watching the election but declined to endorse a candidate.

Salir de la pena máxima

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Las grandes historias giran en torno a pequeñeces. Eso es la vida: una pequeñez. Entró o no la pelota. Entró o no el penalti del padre al que perdiste de niño

En la quietud de la medianoche le escribo: Ahora termino El cielo en casa. Es de las cosas más bellas, originales, poéticas, íntimas, humildes y a la vez memorables que recuerdo haber leído en mucho tiempo. Mando el mensaje y me voy a la cama. Ahí comienza a reverberar en mi cabeza la historia de este chico de 20 años que encuentra una vieja fotografía en un cajón.

La foto muestra a un futbolista lanzando un penalti en un campo de tierra, uno de esos campos inscritos en alguna comisura olvidada de miles de sonrisas ya un tanto apagadas. El balón está detenido en el aire. Se dirige a la derecha, quizá a la escuadra. El portero, a contrapié, mira el vuelo de la pelota. No se ve la portería, solo la red. No puede adivinarse qué pasará con el balón. Si entrará, si no. El chico de veinte años mira la foto. El lanzador lleva bigote pero no es Antonín Panenka. Su leyenda es mucho mayor. Se llama Manuel Martínez Bragagnolo y es su padre. Murió cuando él, Manuel Marsol, no era más que un niño.

¿Cómo saber ahora si aquel penalti se convirtió en gol?

Las grandes historias giran en torno a pequeñeces. Eso es la vida: una pequeñez. Entró o no la pelota. Entró o no el penalti del padre al que perdiste de niño. Pobre de aquel que piense que algo así no importa.

A partir de esa duda, el ilustrador Manuel Marsol, que dibuja y escribe esta asombrosa novela gráfica sin ficción, busca estadísticas regionales de aquella temporada 70-71, llama a un compañero del equipo de barrio en el que jugaba su padre —el Ríos Rosas—, se enmaraña con sus recuerdos infantiles, revisita películas y canciones para tratar de entender el misterio que ha marcado su vida entera y, finalmente, viaja a aquellos dulces días veraniegos de playa, risas y canciones italianas en el coche. Así es como llega hasta el penalti de Roberto Baggio, no hace falta decir cuál.

Todos sabemos que Baggio quedó atrapado en aquel punto de penalti y que en adelante sería un fantasma encadenado a su yo muerto. Mil veces hemos visto la secuencia. La carrerilla de pasos cortos. Ese miedo al que llaman tensión para hacerlo soportable. El balón enviado al cielo más infernal. La vista clavada en el suelo. Un genio ligado por siempre a su fracaso. Todo parece un drama de hechuras clásicas hasta que Manuel Marsol nos cuenta la trastienda desconocida de aquel penalti.

No la habitación de hotel en la que Baggio se encerró solo aquella noche americana del 94. Era el 17 de julio. Esa noche, el niño Marsol celebraba con su padre que los italianos, malditos italianos que habían eliminado a España, perdían el Mundial. Ese mismo 17 de julio del año siguiente moría el padre de Marsol tras una súbita enfermedad. Moría el protagonista del otro penalti. El lanzador de la pena máxima de Marsol.

Uno imagina a Roberto Baggio perderse en el laberinto del 94 —como un poco antes había hecho Miroslav Djuckic y un poco más tarde haría Gareth Southgate— y entonces descubre que nunca Baggio imaginará al niño Marsol, en cada aniversario de su penalti, pensar en el padre perdido, en aquel penalti que no sabe cómo acabó.

Puede que Baggio empezara su noche eterna en Pasadena. Marsol comenzó entonces su tarde infinita. Una tarde de y sis en bucle y porqués sin respuesta.

Escribe en el libro: Aunque el jardín de mi infancia se había cerrado hacía mucho tiempo, de golpe y para siempre, el balón de la foto había abierto una mirilla, un agujerito, a fuerza de vibrar, de latir en la puerta.

Tal vez haya escrito la enésima variación Goldberg de lo que es el fútbol.

A la mañana siguiente de quedar impresionado con este libro, que tanto habla del fútbol y de la vida, le hago una pregunta corta: Qué significa el fútbol para ti. Él me responde con algo que no esperaba. Me cuenta que nada más tener impreso el libro se lo leyó a su madre, Maripaz. Ella le dio las gracias. Lloraron los dos. Y entonces ella le contó algo que no sabía. Que antes de morir, su padre le había pedido que no dejaran de ir al fútbol. Dos años antes los había hecho socios del Madrid al niño, a la niña y a su mujer. Iban los cuatro juntos al campo. Su madre, ya viuda, cumplió aquella promesa. Han pasado más de treinta años. Todavía van los tres juntos al Bernabéu. En realidad, supongo, van los cuatro juntos. Los imagino gritando gol, riendo y abrazándose en la grada y me entran ganas de llorar. Ya nunca me entristecerá una victoria del Madrid.

Escribió Julio Ramón Ribeyro: Quien no conoce las tristezas deportivas no conoce nada de la tristeza.

Marcó el penalti o no. Claro que importa. Querer saberlo es la vida.

Stichwahl in Brasilien – Lula gewinnt – der Streit um die Wahl geht weiter

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Einige Elemente auf SRF.ch funktionieren nur mit aktiviertem JavaScript.

Während das Lula-Lager feierte, wurde bei Flávio Bolsonaro im Wahlbunker die Musik heruntergedreht. Nach dem gewonnenen ersten Wahlgang waren er und sein Team siegesgewiss. Doch Lula holte auf. Er gewann im zweiten Wahlgang rund 10.4 Millionen Stimmen hinzu, Flávio nur 3.7 Millionen. Der amtierende und neue Präsident legte im digitalen Wahlkampf zu. Der Whatsapp-Chat «Die Armee Lulas» gewann immer mehr Follower, am Ende waren es mehr als zehn Millionen.

Bolsonaros Wahlkampfchef warnt gar vor einem möglichen «Justizputsch». Er kritisiert insbesondere Richter Zanin, der wegen Problemen im öffentlichen Nahverkehr eine zweistündige Verlängerung der Abstimmung anordnete. Die Entscheidung kam allerdings zu spät, um sie überall umzusetzen.

Umgekehrt wirft das Lula-Lager dem Präsidenten des Wahlgerichts, Kassio Nunes Marques, vor, zu lange untätig geblieben zu sein. Ihm lag schon gegen Mittag der Antrag auf eine Verlängerung der Abstimmung vor, doch er traf keine Entscheidung. Auch Bolsonaros Wahlkampfteam hatte wegen Unwettern in bestimmten Regionen eine Verlängerung beantragt.

Lula hat zwar die Präsidentschaft verteidigt, seine politischen Gegner aber bleiben stark. Bolsonaro erhielt beinahe die Hälfte der Stimmen, seine Partei PL hat bei den Parlamentswahlen an Einfluss gewonnen. Für Lula dürfte das Regieren noch schwieriger werden als bisher.

Nach der Niederlage könnte sich im Bolsonaro-Lager die Führungsfrage stellen: Flávio hat es nicht geschafft, seinen Vater an der Staatsspitze zu beerben. Sein Senatsmandat läuft im Januar aus und es laufen Ermittlungsverfahren gegen ihn wegen mutmasslicher Geldwäsche. Sein Bruder Eduardo, der in den USA wohnt, hat ihm bereits öffentlich angeraten, Brasilien zu verlassen.

International ist Lulas Wiederwahl ein wichtiges politisches Signal – und ein Rückschlag für Donald Trump. Er hätte mit Flávio Bolsonaro einen engen politischen Verbündeten an der Spitze der grössten Volkswirtschaft Lateinamerikas gewonnen. Stattdessen dürfte Brasilien unter Lula seinen eigenständigen aussenpolitischen Kurs fortsetzen, mit engen Wirtschaftsbeziehungen zu China. In seiner ersten Ansprache nach der Wahl kündigte Lula zudem eine strengere Regulierung der grossen Technologieplattformen an.

Für Lula beginnt eine schwierige Mission. Er muss ein tief gespaltenes Land regieren – mit einem Parlament, in dem seine Gegner stärker geworden sind. Parallel dürften die Diskussionen um die Justizentscheidungen am Wahltag die nächsten Wochen und Monate prägen. Bolsonaronahe Politiker sprechen bereits von Impeachment-Plänen gegen Oberste Richter.

Heikel ist der knappe Wahlsieg auch in Hinblick auf die jüngste Geschichte Brasiliens. Die Erinnerungen an die letzte Wahlniederlage eines Bolsonaro sind noch frisch: Als Flávios Vater Jair 2022 die Wahlen verlor, folgten Putschpläne, einschliesslich gerichtlich festgestellter Mordpläne gegen Lula, und ein Sturm auf die Regierungsgebäude in Brasília.

Die Wahl ist ein wichtiger Etappensieg für Lula. Nun beginnt die Arbeit.

Karen Naundorf ist SRF-Korrespondentin in Südamerika, Standort Buenos Aires. Sie hat in Berlin und Barcelona Kommunikation studiert, die Henri-Nannen-Journalistenschule in Hamburg absolviert und ist Fellow des Pulitzer Center on Crisis Reporting.

President's Challenge Night 2026 raises over S$11 million

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The annual fundraiser brought together international acts Johnny Hates Jazz and Avantgardey with local artistes and community groups, raising S$11,045,752 as of Sunday (Oct 25) night. Donations remain open until Nov 1.

President's Challenge Night was held on Sunday, Oct 25, 2026, at The Theatre at Mediacorp. (Photo: Mediacorp)

President Tharman Shanmugaratnam took to the stage at The Theatre at Mediacorp on Sunday (Oct 25) to play the kompang, a traditional Malay hand drum, during President's Challenge Night 2026.

He joined performers from Singapore Polytechnic for a segment combining kompang drumming, dikir barat and Malay dance, alongside actress Zoe Tay and other local performers.

It was one of the highlights of Mediacorp's annual televised fundraiser in support of the President's Challenge, which raised S$11,045,752 (US$8.6 million) as of Sunday night.

This year's show, themed Uplift and Inspire, was hosted by actress Rebecca Lim and comedian Rishi Budhrani, bringing together international acts, Singapore artistes, young performers and community groups.

President Tharman's kompang performance was part of a larger segment celebrating Malay music and dance.

Actress Zoe Tay joined Seri Thera, a dikir barat group from Singapore Polytechnic, for a performance of the traditional Malay art form, which combines rhythmic chanting, poetry and coordinated movements.

Meanwhile, singer Iman Fandi and violinist Celine Ng joined dancers from Sri Warisan in a performance choreographed by Cultural Medallion recipient Som Said, bringing together traditional and contemporary elements.

The evening also featured performances by pop band Johnny Hates Jazz – who performed their hits Shattered Dreams, Turn Back The Clock and I Don't Want To Be A Hero – and Japanese dance troupe Avantgardey.

Singer-actor Nathan Hartono also shared the stage with 14-year-old Anisah and community choir The Joyful Voices for a rendition of Can You Feel The Love Tonight.

Other participants that evening included singers Amni Musfirah, Rebekah Dorai and Gareth Fernandez, as well as Mediacorp artistes Herman Keh, Ezam Ernady, Tasha Low, Cheryl Chou and Jaynesh Isuran.

Those who missed the broadcast can catch the show on demand on mewatch or watch the repeat telecast on Channel 5 on Nov 1 at 4.40pm.

The donation link will remain open until Nov 1, 2026, 11.59pm, through the official President's Challenge Night website.

The President's Challenge Night is Mediacorp's annual televised fundraiser in support of the President's Challenge.

All proceeds raised through President's Challenge will support 120 programmes, empowering the lives of over 100,000 individuals and their families across Singapore.

Adultery review – The chemistry in this steamy ITV drama is electric

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Romola Garai and Dominic Cooper add star power to this familiar tale of infidelity

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

“There’s a psychological equation. It says that attraction, plus an obstacle, equals excitement. But what happens if the attraction is too strong; the obstacles are too great?”

If the name alone hadn’t tipped you off, this question, posed by a northern-accented Romola Garai in the opening of ITV’s new sexy six-part drama, Adultery, gives a pretty good idea of what’s to come.

Garai plays Beth, a harried, married mum of two and part-time zumba teacher who’s going through a rocky patch after the death of a friend. Minutes into episode one, her world literally collides with Tom (Dominic Cooper), a charmed, middle-class GP who’s also married with kids, when she knocks him off his bike with her car. One thing, as they say, leads to another…

Infidelity is hardly unfamiliar telly terrain. This series follows in the multitude of footsteps that went before it: The Affair, Scandal, Doctor Foster, The Good Wife, The Betrayal, to skim just a few off the top of the considerable iceberg. Any drama worth its salt these days will likely throw in an affair at some point, even if it’s a B-plot device helping chivvy the story along rather than being dished up as the main event.

Perhaps it’s indicative of the fact that we’re a sex-obsessed species of dreadful gossips. Whatever the reason behind the fascination, this kind of show, covering nothing in the way of new ground, lives and dies by the chemistry of its leads. Fortunately, here the casting team absolutely understood the assignment.

Garai and Cooper bring far more than simply good looks and star power to illicit proceedings (though these certainly help); they manage to create an instant, natural warmth that’s both believable and familiar to anyone who’s ever felt that punch-to-the-solar-plexus connection with another person within seconds of meeting.

This palpable, mutual attraction has more substance than merely sexual, with an innate understanding and easy affection behind all that tortured yearning. It’s this depth of feeling that makes the speed at which both parties decide to press the f***-it button and risk blowing up their entire lives somewhat plausible.

To be honest, I would happily watch Garai do just about anything – there’s something so inherently lovely about her, isn’t there? – but she’s in her element when pining, those big, doe eyes following Tom around like a lovestruck mooncalf. Cooper, meanwhile, hits Aidan Turner-levels of smouldering intensity, barely able to rip his eyes away from Beth whenever they’re in the same room.

ADVERTISEMENT. If you sign up to this service we will earn commission. This revenue helps to fund journalism across The Independent.

The attraction, then, is clear – and so are the obstacles. Namely, the fact that Tom and Beth are married to other people, with the added complication that their teenage kids are in a romantic relationship themselves.

The show is more sophisticated and nuanced than many that have gone before it in terms of presenting the humanity of everyone involved, thanks to smart writing from Bafta winner Danny Brocklehurst and a stellar ensemble cast. (Mercifully, the former has eschewed the “jump the shark” style of his many Harlan Coben adaptations here in favour of a more naturalistic style.)

Neither Tom nor Beth are in loveless marriages, per se, just ones that have hit their own respective tricky patches; neither his wife, Hannah (Shelley Conn), nor her husband, Neil (Matthew McNulty), are cartoon villains, cardboard cut-out collateral damage or blameless saints, but rather complex, flawed, living, breathing human beings.

It makes the pathos feel all the more real as the drama unfolds, holding the tension of the uncomfortable truth that cheating doesn’t automatically make someone a monster without shying away from its potentially devastating consequences.

“We’re all one moment away from our lives disintegrating,” says Hannah to her therapist at one point, in what could either be viewed as a self-fulfilling prophecy, given the extreme anxiety and intrusive thoughts that have strained her marriage in recent years, or a burst of pure prescience.

Like with all tales of forbidden love, it seems inevitable that Tom and Beth will, at some point, get found out. The only question is when and how the wheels will come off. But safe to say, attraction, plus an obstacle, is unlikely to equal happily ever after...

Young Sheldon star Emily Osment asked for intimacy coordinator on set because of ‘concern’

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Director admits that he and others ‘totally dropped the ball’

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Emily Osment asked for an intimacy coordinator on the set of Young Sheldon because of the age gap between herself and co-star Montana Jordan.

Osment, who played Mandy McAllister on the CBS sitcom, was 30 when the pair shot their initial episodes. Jordan, meanwhile, was just 18.

According to Jessica Radloff's new book, Young Sheldon: The (Juicier-Than-Meemaw's-Brisket) Inside Story of the Hit Comedy, Osment expressed “concern” around shooting kissing scenes with Jordan.

“That made me uncomfortable. He was a baby! A baby with a mullet,” she said.

“So I spoke to [producer] Tim Marx, and obviously they were not able to get one that late in the day, but for the next few episodes they had one there, since the relationship between them escalates.

“It made everything easier having one there. I think it’s something that’s really important.”

She said she wasn’t sure if Jordan knew he could ask for an intimacy coordinator, but wanted to “give him the option” and “make him comfortable”.

She added that he is “such a sweet kid” that he would have probably gone ahead with the scenes without rocking the boat.

She was thankful to the showrunners for getting an intimacy coordinator on set, she said.

“I love those guys, and I’m really happy it got to the point where they were able to do that.”

Alex Reid, the director of the show – a prequel to The Big Bang Theory – said that he and others “totally dropped the ball” while shooting the kissing scenes.

“I have a photo between takes of them sitting in that car when we shot that, and they’re looking off in other directions. I showed it to Emily and she said, ‘Yeah, that was so uncomfortable for both of us, because we didn’t know each other.’

ADVERTISEMENT. If you sign up to this service we will earn commission. This revenue helps to fund journalism across The Independent.

“It wasn’t even about the lack of an intimacy coordinator. It’s just you’re having to make out with somebody you just kind of met. Obviously they worked that out, and they have a great chemistry now, but that was kind of embarrassing.”

Co-creator Steve Molaro added that Osment had “every right to ask” for an intimacy coordinator on set.

I saw a ghost on the baby monitor. For a year after my home was haunted by sudden chills, drawers torn open and a dark presence that focused on my sons. It was so terrifying our babysitter refused to come back. Here's how I banished it: CAPRICE BOURRET

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

As an American, I’ve always enjoyed Halloween. And, having spent half my life in the UK, I’ve watched it grow in popularity here too.

It falls very close to my birthday so I’ve often combined the celebrations. But it’s the dressing up and having fun aspect I like, not the spookiness! There’s a good reason for that – a very unwelcome spook that invaded my home and terrified me.

I’d recently given birth to twin sons back in 2013 and, like any new mum, my overwhelming instinct was to protect my precious babies.

So when, one evening, my husband Ty turned to me on the sofa and said: ‘Someone’s in the house… there’s an intruder, they’re in the boys’ room,’ my blood ran cold.

Ty and I were in the living room of our six-bedroom west London home watching TV while our sons Jax and Jett, then six months old, were upstairs in their cribs.

We had a video baby monitor and on it Ty and I could clearly see a female figure. This person was dressed in black and bending over and staring down at the children as they slept. In that instant I felt sheer panic, thinking my babies were about to be harmed or kidnapped.

We both leapt to our feet and sprinted to the nursery, but there was nobody there and the boys were fine. There was no sign of a break-in either and nothing was missing, and yet we both knew what we had seen.

Little did I know then that this was the beginning of a nightmare that was to take over my life for the best part of a year.

Supermodel Caprice Bourret saw a ghost on her baby monitor, which marked the beginning of a nightmare that took over her life for the best part of a year

We’d just moved into our house following a huge renovation project. It’s a large Victorian building that used to be a very well-known nightclub, somewhere The Rolling Stones, David Bowie and a host of other rock stars had played.

I love knowing that, at one stage, some of the biggest names in music had performed in what became our lounge.

Before that it was a working men’s club, so it has a fascinating history.

We were able to buy the house partly thanks to success I’d had in the UK. I hit the headlines for wearing a daring dress to the 1996 National Television Awards. And over the next decade I appeared on more than 350 magazine covers as well as being voted the World’s Sexiest Woman.

The building is Grade II-listed and it took a long time to get all the necessary permissions to turn it into a residential home.

But property renovation and interior design is a passion of mine, so it was worth the wait.

By the time we moved in we had created this amazing space over four floors with beautiful light and we were so excited to make it our family home. However, within months I became so terrified by the ghostly presence that I didn’t think I’d be able to stay. Even though I never saw the figure in black again, I knew she was still around as I constantly sensed her.

On the baby monitor she was wearing a long, black dress and had a slim, feminine figure but I never saw a face, because it was covered by a veil.

Then other things started to happen, always at night and only ever in two areas – the boys’ bedroom and a nearby guest room, both on the top floor.

In these rooms I’d suddenly experience an intense chill, the kind that goes to your bones and makes you shiver, yet just a few feet away the room would feel warm.

'This person was dressed in black and bending over and staring down at the children as they slept. In that instant I felt sheer panic', writes Caprice (stock image)

We had underfloor heating and I called out three different engineers in case it had gone wrong, but they all said it was working just fine. The odd thing was that the babies’ cribs were always warm and toasty.

At night I’d hear movement upstairs, such as doors slamming, and sometimes I’d go up there to find drawers open that I knew had been left shut.

When I went into the two affected rooms everything felt ‘off’. There was a strange and heavy atmosphere.

Not long after the baby monitor incident, Ty and I went out to an event and got a babysitter in.

We weren’t gone for very long but arrived home to find the babysitter, a young woman, in the downstairs playroom crying and in real distress.

She looked terrified and told me there was ‘something’ in the house.

She said she could feel it on her, watching her.

She told me I needed to go and check the boys’ bedroom because she was too scared to go up there. Of course I raced up the stairs and they were fine and sound asleep but suffice to say that she refused to babysit for us again.

After that, I moved a bed into my sons’ room and slept there because I was so frightened for their safety.

I was imagining all these awful scenarios. What if this ghost started to flip things over and chuck objects around and injured the kids?

People will ask why I didn’t move the children into our room but by then I had a strong sense that the babies were her focus and if I moved them into our bedroom, where we never experienced anything strange, then trouble would follow.

Because I was scared and in this permanent state of heightened alert I wasn’t sleeping well and was constantly exhausted and on edge.

Caprice with her husband Ty Comfort. The couple have two children, sons Jax and Jett

My husband, like a lot of men, is a logical thinker and can be a bit unemotional. He’d been the first one to see the ghost on the monitor but once he knew it wasn’t a real intruder he wasn’t concerned whereas I was quite the hysterical drama queen about it all.

He’d tell me: ‘Cap, whatever it is doesn’t seem to be hurting anyone, so just ignore it.’

I’d say: ‘Are you joking? OK, she hasn’t hurt us so far, but that could change!’

I’m a very spiritual person. I believe in fate and destiny. I’ve had psychic readings before and have been given messages from loved ones that have passed away, which I absolutely believe to be real.

I do believe in ghosts too – even though they terrify me – because I’ve encountered one before.

A friend of mine has a huge, old house in the country and one weekend 20 of us were staying there. We did a seance, where we tried to communicate with spirits – so I guess you could say we were asking for trouble.

The lights went out and the whole house was plunged into blackness and the room went cold. That was enough for me.

I said, ‘see you guys later, I’m out of here,’ and checked myself into a hotel. If it wasn’t for my children, I think I’d have moved into a hotel in London too but it would have been too unsettling for them.

The thing was, even though the ghost seemed obsessed with them, they were totally oblivious to what was happening – they slept well and were always happy and content.

After months of this – the cold rooms, slamming doors and this constant sense of something watching us – I decided to call in the ghostbusters.

Where do you start with something like that? In the end I just Googled it and took it from there. The first one came and she had this pendulum instrument that she walked around the house with. I didn’t tell her which rooms were affected because I wanted to see how good she was.

'I do believe in ghosts too – even though they terrify me – because I’ve encountered one before'

She walked around downstairs and all was calm and then she went into the guest room and I swear the pendulum started swinging manically.

It was alarming but I didn’t expect the ghostbuster to be scared. She freaked out and said ‘I’m leaving,’ and literally fled. I remember calling after her ‘hang on a minute! I paid you up front, you can’t just walk out!’

The second one came and wasn’t much better. She said she could feel a very aggressive presence. A rabbi also came and agreed there was a spirit living with us. Both claimed to have healed the house. But they hadn’t, our ghost was still there watching us and causing mischief. I imagined her laughing at them and me too, probably, for wasting my money.

Then, one night, after nearly a year of this, I had had enough.

I was sleep-deprived and at my wits’ end. I was in my sons’ room on my makeshift bed when that icy chill came over my part of the room again. I just sat up and pleaded out loud: ‘I cannot do this any more, you have to leave. You’re scaring me. I can’t sleep, I can’t cope, please leave us alone.

‘This isn’t your house, it’s ours and I don’t want you here. Just go.’

And yes, I know it sounds mad and people reading this will think I’m a crackpot, but it worked. There was a shift in energy almost immediately and the temperature went back to normal. The room suddenly felt as tranquil as the rest of the house. I was on tenterhooks for weeks after that, wondering if she’d reappear, but she never bothered us again.

I have no idea who she was or what she really wanted. Maybe she wanted to protect the boys and watch over them, but that was my job.

In the end, for all my messing around with ghostbusters, all it took was for her to be asked nicely to leave. My sons know all about it but, at 13 years old, they just think I’m their crazy mum telling stories and laugh at me.

Our London house is for sale now, nothing to do with the ghost – although I’m so relieved we got rid of her – but these days we mostly live in Ibiza and it’s where the boys go to school. I love England but I don’t see myself living in the UK full time again. London feels less safe, whereas in Ibiza we leave the doors open, the kids walk into town by themselves and scooter everywhere, it’s a wonderful carefree life for them.

Also, although I love Halloween, my professional career is now based around a jollier seasonal event: Christmas.

At 54, I’m focused on my movies. I’ve made six of them via my own production company JJLove Productions and we have a new film, An English Christmas Wish, which I star in and which will be on Channel 5 next month.

All my films are Christmas- and romance-themed and, yes, they stick to a formula and everyone knows the ending, but people love them.

They’re comforting, unlike scary ghost stories – real or otherwise.

I look back and think about my ‘haunting’ experience a lot. I’ve never doubted myself though, never wondered if I imagined it all because I was an exhausted new mother.

It was very real, very frightening and I hope nothing like that ever happens again.

Emma Loveheart has been a house healer for 15 years, helping to restore peace to more than 1,000 haunted properties. Here she reveals the six biggest signs you may not be alone in your home...

Smoke is the most common. When people first move in they assume any smoke/tobacco smells are left over from the previous owner. But some find that the smell continues and even gets stronger, despite changing carpets and curtains.

It’s likely the spirit of a former occupant, a smoker, is trapped there and the smell will very often be in one particular area of the room where they used to sit and smoke. The smell of lavender, something associated with older people, is another frequently occurring one.

We all experience runs of bad luck but if everything is going wrong with no let-up: job, relationships, finances –the key things in life – and you can trace it back to when you started living in your house, then there could be something else going on. It often starts with something small and then just keeps ramping up.

Feeling despondent and having no energy at home, yet your mood and energy lifts as soon as you step outside. This may indicate a spirit is trapped in your house and is influencing how you feel.

Dreading being at home and struggling to sleep – but not when you are away – could be a sign something is amiss and there’s a presence that needs to be helped to leave.

Spirits are beings that get ‘stuck’ between this life and the next – very often they don’t even know they are stuck.

They have a connection to the house or land and need to be persuaded that it’s time to leave.

Unexplained cold spots in an otherwise warm house are something I hear a lot from clients. It’s mostly in one particular area. Very often it’s a living area where somebody used to sit.

I recently encountered the spirit of a woman who would sit in a chair in the main sitting room of an old house and stare out of the window, waiting for her son who had been away fighting in a war to come home.

Slamming doors, footsteps, banging and tapping are all noises that people report hearing and very often these noises occur at a specific time of the day or night, ruling out things such as pipes or floorboards. Some people feel a physical presence too, someone brushing past them or a weight on them at night.

Electrical interference happens a lot as well. Electricity is an energy and the spirits can tap into that and communicate through it.

Your lights might flicker or you’ll notice that the bulbs keep going even after they’ve just been changed.

Radios, TVs and other appliances suddenly coming on is another occurrence.

Pets, especially dogs, and small children are much more sensitive and intuitive than adults. Clients will tell me that their dog will suddenly start barking at a wall or a corner of the room or they’ll just refuse to go into the room at all.

Children will often say they can see something and they’ll describe it in such a detailed way they couldn’t be making it up. Young children are far more connected and intuitive as they’re purer and less likely to try to rationalise.

What happened to the cast of Once Upon A Time? From a real-life fairytale romance to a scandalous affair and a tragic death; a look back at the stars 15 years after the show first aired

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Published: 00:01 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 00:15 GMT, 26 October 2026

It's hard to believe it's been 15 years since fairy tale fantasy drama Once Upon A Time first hit screens back in October 2011.

Spanning across seven seasons, the ABC series centered around two alternate worlds, featuring characters who lived in the real-world town of Storybrooke, Maine and their fairy tale counterparts who resided in the Enchanted Forest.

The main premise saw The Evil Queen aka Mayor Regina Mills (Lara Parrilla) cast a dark curse which results in the fairy tale characters losing their memories of who they really are and becoming trapped as their alter-egos in Storybrooke.

Their saviour comes in the form of bail bonds collector Emma Swan (Jennifer Morrison), who is actually the long-lost daughter of Snow White (Ginnifer Goodwin) and Prince Charming (Josh Dallas), who was sent away as a baby before the curse took hold so that she could one day return and break it.

Blending a mix of fantasy, drama, adventure and romance, the age-old conflict between good and evil and a huge helping of nostalgia thanks to the plethora of classic Disney, fairy tale and folklore characters; Once Upon A Time was a major hit when it first premiered, attracting a whopping 12.93 million viewers for its pilot episode.

It's hard to believe it's been 15 years since fairy tale fantasy drama Once Upon A Time first hit screens back in October 2011

The ABC series centered around two alternate worlds, featuring characters who lived in the real-world town of Storybrooke, Maine and their fairy tale counterparts who resided in the Enchanted Forest

While much of the cast were already established actors, the show undoubtedly significantly boosted their popularity, with the stars building a solid fanbase who have continued to support them throughout their careers.

However as the years went on, the show suffered a mass decline in viewership, going on to be cancelled following the seventh season, with the last episode airing in May 2018.

Despite no longer producing new episodes, the fantasy series has gained a whole new following as teens who watched the original run are now watching the show with their own children, while the series continues to spark debate amid its online fandom.

And as Once Upon a Time marks 15 years since it first aired, we take a look back at the varied fates of the show's main cast....

Lana Parrilla (Regina Mills / The Evil Queen)

Lana excelled in her standout role as the villainous Evil Queen / Storybrooke Mayor Regina, garnering a massive fans base and numerous awards and nominations.

Since leaving the show, she went on to make appearances in 2021's The Tax Collector, anthology series Why Women Kill (pictured) and 2023's The Lincoln Lawyer

Lana embarked on a romance with The Gentlemen star Benjamin Clementine, following the collapse of his marriage earlier this year

Lana, now 49, excelled in her standout role as the villainous Evil Queen / Storybrooke Mayor Regina, garnering a massive fan base and numerous awards and nominations.

Since leaving the show, she went on to make appearances in 2021's The Tax Collector, anthology series Why Women Kill (which also featured co-star Ginnifer Goodwin) and 2023's The Lincoln Lawyer.

As well as TV and film, Lana also branched into music, releasing a house song called It's Over Now in 2021 alongside Italian DJ Glovibes.

She once again teamed up with Glovibes, along with singer Luciana, for the 2024 dance track, You.

But while she was successful on screen, Lana has faced bad luck in her life, with the star seeing her LA home targeted by trespassers back in August 2024.

TMZ reported that when security approached the person, he ventured further onto the property grounds, leaving them to call the Los Angeles Police Department.

After law enforcement surveyed the property, the suspect – a 40-year-old homeless man – emerged from the house and surrendered.

Lana, who herself had suffered homelessness in her early career, was out of town when the incident took place.

In her personal life, Lana secretly married fiancé Fred Di Blasio in 2014, however, five years later she announced on her Instagram that she was 'no longer married' and was instead starting a 'new life path'.

This new path has since led to a romance with The Gentlemen star Benjamin Clementine, following the collapse of his marriage to 'City Superwoman' Dame Helena Morrissey's musician daughter Flo earlier this year.

The couple made their red-carpet debut last month at the series two premiere of The Gentlemen, in which Benjamin plays a former soldier called Nanny.

Ginnifer Goodwin (Mary Margaret Blanchard / Snow White)

Ginnifer, now 48, had a major career breakthrough with her lead role as Snow White and Mary, the school teacher of young Henry and the mother of long-lost daughter Emma Swan

She went on to make television appearances in anthology series The Twilight Zone and Heartstring, before landing the lead role of Beth Ann Stanton in dark comedy series Why Women Kill [pictured]

Meanwhile, during her time on the ABC show, Ginnifer met and fell in love with co-star Josh Dallas, who plays Prince Charming

Ginnifer, now 48, had a major career breakthrough with her lead role as Snow White and Mary; the schoolteacher of young Henry and the mother of long-lost daughter Emma Swan.

Following her success in the series, which she left at the end of season six, Ginnifer went on to have big triumphs with her voiceover work, including starring as Judy Hopps in Disney's Zootopia franchise – of which she won a selection of awards for.

She went on to make television appearances in anthology series The Twilight Zone and Heartstrings, before landing the lead role of Beth Ann Stanton in dark comedy series Why Women Kill.

Meanwhile, during her time on the ABC show, Ginnifer met and fell in love with co-star Josh Dallas, who plays Prince Charming.

Their fairytale ending saw them tie the knot in California in 2014, with the couple going on to welcome two sons; Oliver, 12, and Hugo, 10.

During an appearance on the Today show in 2020, Josh revealed that he fell in love with Ginnifer at first sight.

'I knew right away. I knew right away, it was that thing,' he said. 'It was that thing - I turned around and I saw her and I knew I was in trouble.'

Josh shared that 'understanding' has helped their relationship as well as 'willing to be flexible, and just love. You gotta love and be willing to grow with that person, or let that person grow too and give them space.'

Josh Dallas (David Nolan / Prince Charming)

Josh, 47, left thanks swooning thanks to his role as the handsome Prince Charming and alter-ego David Nolan

Following his years on the show, Josh jumped into another long-term TV project, starring as Ben Stone in the NBC/Netflix supernatural mystery drama Manifest, which he appeared on from 2018 to 2023

Like his wife, Josh also briefly reprised his Once Upon A Time role to appear in the series finale in 2018. Back in 2023, Josh said he has 'nothing but gratitude' for 'Once Upon a Time'

Josh, 47, left fans swooning thanks to his role as the handsome Prince Charming and alter-ego David Nolan.

Following his years on the show, Josh jumped into another long-term TV project, starring as Ben Stone in the NBC/Netflix supernatural mystery drama Manifest, which he appeared on from 2018 to 2023.

Josh also reunited with his love Ginnifer Goodwin for his voice role as Frantic Pig in 2016's Zootopia, later reprising his part in 2025 for the sequel.

Like his wife, Josh also briefly reprised his Once Upon A Time role to appear in the series finale in 2018.

Back in 2023, Josh said he has 'nothing but gratitude' for Once Upon a Time.

He told how after years since the series finale, it is 'humbling' to know that the fantasy drama still has an audience.

He told People: 'It's still finding an audience. People come up to me all the time talking about 'Once' and saying they just started watching it and they're loving it.

'Or I've had people come up to me — which makes me feel old, but that's okay — they'll say, 'You were my childhood. You got me through college, all through high school' and that show changed their life and it's super humbling.

'It's just nothing but gratitude to be part of a show that was such a big part of some people's fabric of their lives.'

Elsewhere, in 2026, Josh made minor appearances in TV series The Hunting Party and We Were Liars, while he's set to lend his voice for animated film Hexed, which is due for release in November.

Jennifer portrayed Emma Swan, a Boston bail-bond agent who discovers she's Snow White and Prince Charming's daughter

Jennifer's directing career also saw her direct feature film Sun Dogs, an episode of Euphoria and the first four episodes of the second season of Peacock anthology series Dr. Death

Even the criminals don't like Labour's early release scheme in this dark saga: CHRISTOPHER STEVENS reviews This City Is Ours

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Published: 22:09 GMT, 25 October 2026 | Updated: 22:13 GMT, 25 October 2026

What a relief to find a thick drug dealer. Most of them are urbane toffs like Theo James in The Gentlemen or ultra-cool masterminds like Peaky Blinders kingpin Tommy Shelby.

Henchman Ando (Theo Nate), in This City Is Ours, is more true to life. If his brain were made of cocaine, he wouldn’t have enough on him to get arrested. Even sniffer dogs would ignore him.

As he swaggered into the house of rival gangster Banksey (Mike Noble) and started giving him lip, Ando found himself looking down the barrel of a silenced pistol.

‘Is that vest bulletproof?’ Banksey asked casually, the way you might wonder if his pullover came from Marks and Sparks.

‘Yeah, it is,’ said Ando, too dumb to stay shtum. So Banksey shot him, and then did it again for good measure.

It was a rare moment of dark comedy in an episode that, as the show returned, was often violent but seldom upbeat.

What a relief to find a thick drug dealer. Most of them are urbane toffs like Theo James in The Gentlemen or ultra-cool masterminds like Peaky Blinders kingpin Tommy Shelby

Ozzie hasn’t bumped into the head of Banksey’s gang, the brooding Michael Kavanagh (James Nelson-Joyce), but a war between the two of them promises to be the mainspring of this eight-part season

It began with murder, with Banksey running over a Colombian cartel boss who didn’t take the basic precaution of looking both ways before stepping out into a Liverpool road.

The next 30 minutes slowed to a crawl, the main theme being the grief of women widowed by gang feuds.

Julie Graham, as Elaine, was missing dead husband Ronnie (Sean Bean) so much that she drank red wine until she could see his ghost sitting at the kitchen counter.

Grief-stricken Cheryl (Saoirse-Monica Jackson) was also knocking back the claret, though she’d admittedly had a tough day – finding her dead husband’s rotting corpse in a body bag in the Spanish hinterland, playing Ave Maria on her phone over his shallow grave, and then getting kidnapped by the Colombian drug lord’s brother.

All this grimness became oppressive, until the drama began to feel as entertaining as a migraine.

But the pressure found a release when Banksey and his mate went to collect a drugs shipment at a carwash. As his car was smothered in foam, it seemed to close in on him like a coffin.

When the jet hoses hit the windscreen, he and I both jumped out of our skins.

Shaun Evans joins the cast as an ex-jailbird called Ozzie, with an air of amiable menace.

First, though, Michael has to appease the Colombians, currently holding his pregnant girlfriend Diana (Hannah Onslow) hostage

Everyone seems to know him and no one was expecting to see him, which suggests they all thought he was still inside. Labour’s early release scheme is upsetting everyone, even the criminals.

Ozzie hasn’t bumped into the head of Banksey’s gang, the brooding Michael Kavanagh (James Nelson-Joyce), but a war between the two of them promises to be the mainspring of this eight-part season.

First, though, Michael has to appease the Colombians, currently holding his pregnant girlfriend Diana (Hannah Onslow) hostage.

In a bold move, the show has adopted Matt Monro’s gorgeous hit On Days Like These as its title music. I thought that song was copyrighted to The Italian Job.

BBC must 'pay £400,000 to Oscar-winning Jewish producer after she became victim of anti-Semitism and was bullied out of the broadcaster'

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Published: 21:52 GMT, 25 October 2026 | Updated: 02:41 GMT, 26 October 2026

A Jewish documentary maker has reportedly been awarded a £400,000 payout from the BBC following allegations she was bullied out of the broadcaster and the victim of anti-Semitism.

Oscar-winning filmmaker Lucie Kon said she was mistreated by BBC colleagues who said Zionists like her were 'evil' in the wake of the October 7 Hamas terrorist attacks on Israel.

Ms Kon – who has family living in Israel – alleges she was instructed by coworkers to consider 'what drove Hamas to do what they did,' and told she should 'call Israel terrorists, too' if she wished to label Hamas as such.

She also said her colleagues told her a documentary about the Nova music festival massacre should 'contextualise' the terrorist attack, and it was 'disgusting' that former Prime Minister Rishi Sunak visited a Jewish school.

After making a formal complaint about her treatment, Ms Kon claimed the BBC asked her to stay at home for months before isolating her and eventually ousting her from the workplace.

It was then she began unfair dismissal proceedings against the broadcaster, who without any admission of liability, paid her compensation which was funded in part by the licence fee.

Oscar-winning filmmaker Lucie Kon has been awarded a £400,000 payout from the BBC following allegations she was bullied out of the broadcaster and the victim of anti-Semitism

Ms Kon claimed she was mistreated by BBC colleagues who said Zionists like her were 'evil'. Mc Kon (left) is pictured in February at the BAFTA Film Awards

The broadcaster has not issued a formal apology to Ms Kon - who worked on shows such as Panorama and BBC Breakfast - and refused to acknowledge she was the victim of anti-Semitism while in their employment.

Ms Kon, who told the Telegraph the BBC attempted to 'stop' her doing her job because of her religion, is the first person the broadcaster is known to have paid a settlement to as a consequence of anti-Semitism.

'It was like having Jew stamped on my forehead,' Ms Kon described feeling in the wake of the October 7th terrorist attacks.

'I think I made too much noise about being a Jew inside the BBC,' she said. 'I stood up for my community. I was open about being a Zionist. The BBC says, bring your authentic self to work. And I did. And I lost my job as a result.'

Her payout comes just one year following the resignation of the BBC's director-general, Tim Davie, whose premiership saw the broadcaster being accused of institutional bias, including claims of anti-Semitism.

Under his tenure, more than 200 Jewish staff accused the corporation's Board of 'ignoring' their pleas for an investigation into alleged anti-Semitism at the broadcaster.

In July 2024, more than 200 Jewish staff, contractors, suppliers and contributors wrote to BBC chairman Samir Shah asking for an urgent formal investigation by the BBC Board into 'systemic problems of anti-Semitism and bias at the BBC, alongside senior management's demonstrable failure to properly address the issue'.

The premiership of former BBC director general Tim Davie (pictured) saw the broadcaster being accused of institutional bias, including claims of anti-Semitism

The BBC has not issued a formal apology to Ms Kon and has refused to acknowledge she was the victim of anti-Semitism while in their employment (Pictured: BBC Broadcasting House in London)

Attached to the letter was a report titled 'Being Jewish and working at the BBC' which included testimony from staff who argued the corporation was no longer 'a safe space to be Jewish'.

The same staff members wrote again to the BBC Board last December, accusing it of ignoring their pleas and offering 'words not action' after a gap of more than a year.

Matt Brittin - Mr Davie's successor – has since pledged to eradicate anti-Semitism at the company, however, Ms Kon said she informed him that she and fellow Jewish BBC employees continue to be bullied and abused.

She alleged other Jewish employees have left the broadcaster because of how they were treated, adding she has friends who are 'still struggling' and 'still suffering' because they are a Zionist or Jew.

A BBC spokesman said: 'While we do not comment on individual staffing matters, anti-Semitism in any form is not tolerated at the BBC.

'If allegations or concerns are raised, they are taken seriously and investigated in accordance with our policies. We have robust procedures in place to deal with any behaviour that is contrary to our values.'

Scott Walker’s The Drift will get under your skin

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Walker’s 2006 album is unsettling, bizarre, and the perfect way to kill a party.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Walker’s 2006 album is unsettling, bizarre, and the perfect way to kill a party.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In my mind, Spooky Season music comes in two distinct flavors. There are the fun, occasionally goofy songs like “The Monster Mash” and John Carpenter’s “Halloween Theme.” Then there is the genuinely terrifying: Throbbing Gristle, The Body, or Scott Walker’s The Drift.

This 2006 album from the singer-songwriter is truly upsetting. On it, Walker explores death, disease, and tragedy. But not just from some abstract perspective. There are visceral descriptions of the hanging of Benito Mussolini and his mistress Clara Petacci. He sings about 9/11, his stillborn twin brother, and the Srebrenica genocide. Lyrically, it’s about as dark as an album gets.

But it’s the experimental backdrop that drives it all home.

At times, the record sounds more like the score to an Ari Aster film than anything that could be called pop music under even the most generous definitions. Seasick guitars in the verses of “Jesse” give way to dissonant strings that drone oppressively over the chorus. You can feel the low-bitrate MP3 noise that opens “Clara” in your jaw, which also allegedly features the sound of his percussionist punching a pig carcass as part of the rhythm track.

Songs stop on a dime, linger in uncomfortable silence, then erupt in bursts of mechanical noise. Animal bleating, foley work, and atonal orchestra sections grind against Walker’s warble. Nothing is ever quite in tune, and it can be challenging to get through the record in a single sitting if you’re not in a very particular mood. But sticking with it can be quite rewarding.

Some songs and performances are so beautiful they send shivers down your spine or give you goosebumps. Scott Walker’s The Drift is the opposite of that. You will still have an involuntary physical reaction, but it’s more shudders than shivers. It will make your skin crawl.

Scott Walker’s The Drift is available on Bandcamp and most major streaming services, including YouTube Music, Apple Music, Qobuz, Deezer, and Spotify.

Thomass-Einstand im "Tatort": Gott sei Dank für diesen Garten

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In ihrem ersten Franken-"Tatort" bekommt Rosalie Thomass einen Fall mit echtem Gewicht: Missbrauch, Rache und die Frage, ob ein Freispruch für immer gelten muss. Erzählt wird er allerdings mit Figuren aus dem Holzschnitt und Dialogen, die zuverlässig Fremdscham auslösen. Die beste Rolle in "Gottesgarten" spielt die Landschaft.

"Man soll Gott für alles danken, sogar für die (Ober-)Franken." Mit diesem Satz stellt sich Kommissarin Emmy Rathgeber (Rosalie Thomass) ihren neuen Nürnberger Kollegen vor. Wer dabei nicht zusammenzuckt, kommt vermutlich ohne Schaden durch den Rest des Abends. Allen anderen bleibt ein Trost, und der ist kein ganz kleiner: Selten war Oberfranken im Ersten so schön anzuschauen.

Es ist ein guter Felsen, von dem die drei Jugendlichen ihre Beine baumeln und den Blick in die Ferne schweifen lassen. Der Autor dieser Kritik weiß das aus erster Hand: Er macht das auch jedes Mal, wenn er zurück in seine alte Heimat kommt. Mit großer Kunstfertigkeit hat Autor Constantin Lieb die Drehorte im "Gottesgarten", wie der Landstrich zwischen Bad Staffelstein und Lichtenfels marketingtauglich genannt wird, ausgewählt. Und dass er im gleichnamigen Film bewusst die wilde Schönheit des Staffelbergs bevorzugt und die majestätischen Wahrzeichen Kloster Banz und Vierzehnheiligen außen vor lässt, lässt zu Beginn auch auf den Inhalt hoffen. Allerdings in weiten Teilen zu Unrecht, zwischen den Zeilen passiert in diesem "Tatort" zu wenig.

Aber der Reihe nach, sprich zum Einstand von Rosalie Thomass als "Tatort"-Kommissarin. Den Rahmen steckt Polizeipräsident (Stefan Merki) gleich zu Beginn ab: "Wir machen das mal ganz pragmatisch." Gemeint ist die Nachfolge von Paula Ringelhahn. Dagmar Manzel hatte die Rolle nach zehn Fällen abgegeben, Felix Voss (Fabian Hinrichs) ermittelte zuletzt in "Ich sehe dich" allein. Nun steht Kriminalhauptkommissarin Rathgeber im Büro: eine Oberbayerin, die am Tag nach Voss' Geburtstag mit Blumen anrückt und gegen dessen Brummschädel gleich eine Tablette aus der Handtasche zieht. Für Thomass, gebürtige Münchnerin und Grimme-Preisträgerin ("Eine unerhörte Frau"), ist es der erste Einsatz als "Tatort"-Ermittlerin. Ihren Durchbruch hatte sie passenderweise bereits 2005 in einem BR-"Polizeiruf" von Dominik Graf.

Das gute alte Kompetenzgerangel bei der Einführung neuer Kommissare löst Drehbuchautor Constantin Lieb nicht ungeschickt als angedeuteten Spagat. "Ich glaube, ein Kompetenzgerangel fand niemand interessant", sagt Thomass. Ganz verzichten mag das Drehbuch trotzdem nicht darauf: Eine Weile hängt eine passiv-aggressive Stimmung in der guten fränkischen Luft. Bis Rathgeber klarstellt, dass sie nicht als Assistentin neben Voss herläuft und ihren (Mit)Führungsanspruch formuliert: "Versprochen?" Große Versprechen lägen ihm nicht, entgegnet Voss. "Aber ich kann Ihnen mein Wort geben, wenn Ihnen das wichtig ist." Welches Wort das denn wäre, fragt Rathgeber. "Zwetschgenquarkknödel."

Später werden die Knödel zum Aufhänger für eine vorsichtige private Annäherung der beiden: Voss erklärt seine heftige Wut auf die Reaktion des Vaters des Toten mit seiner eigenen Familiengeschichte, Rathgeber hört zu und reagiert einfühlsam - spätestens hier ist der Konflikt zwischen den beiden Geschichte. Die Geschichte der Kommissarin deutet das Drehbuch dagegen nur an: ein verlorener kleiner Bruder, ein Blick, der in der Pathologie dem toten Elias ausweicht. Auserzählt wird das wohl erst in den kommenden Fällen.

Sicherlich auch, weil sich Drehbuchautor Lieb ohnehin eine Menge vorgenommen hat. Es geht in "Gottesgarten" um Missbrauch und Rache, um junge Menschen, die sich von traditionellen Geschlechterbildern lösen, und um Eltern, die damit nicht umgehen können. Und es geht um den Grundsatz, dass niemand nach einem Freispruch wegen derselben Tat erneut angeklagt werden darf - ein Prinzip, über das in Deutschland ernsthaft gestritten wird: 2021 beschloss der Bundestag eine Ausnahme für Mord und andere unverjährbare Verbrechen, 2023 erklärte das Bundesverfassungsgericht sie für verfassungswidrig. Stoff genug für einen großen Film.

Vielleicht ein bisschen zu groß für einen "Tatort", denn erzählt wird er bevorzugt als Scherenschnitt. Elias' Vater (Horst Kummeth) etwa handelt den Tod seines Sohnes mit einem "Selber schuld. Oder net?" ab - mehr Zwischentöne gönnt ihm das Drehbuch nicht. Auch Voss' oben schon angerissener Vater-Monolog an der Tankstelle kommt arg simpel daher und sorgt bei sensibleren Zuschauern für einen kleinen, harten Fremdschämknoten unterhalb des Zwerchfells. Aber immerhin: Der Sprit kostet dort gerade mal 1,53 Euro. Feels like gute, alte Zeit, man kann sich also gut für einen Moment ablenken.

"Besonders schön finde ich, dass der Regisseur Dustin Loose und das fabelhafte Schauspielensemble auch den Humor und die Leichtigkeit des Fränkischen hervorragend getroffen haben, ohne es zu sehr auszustellen", sagt Lieb im BR-Pressedossier. Vielleicht bezieht sich der Autor auf eine frühere Schnittfassung, man weiß es nicht. Denn im fertigen Film taugen viele der Szenen, die dem Publikum die fränkische Seele näherbringen sollen, vor allem als Wegzapp-Moment.

Bleibt die Landschaft. Die Wege durchs Obermaintal, hinauf zum Staffelberg und rund um Bad Staffelstein ist Lieb nach eigenen Worten schon als Kind und Jugendlicher gegangen, und Kameramann Clemens Baumeister macht daraus Bilder, die bei jedem Exil-Franken - den Autor dieser Zeilen eingeschlossen - zuverlässig Heimweh auslösen. Nur: Wenn die Kulisse die überzeugendste Darstellerin eines Krimis ist, hat der Krimi ein Problem. Der "Gottesgarten" hätte eine feinere Umsetzung verdient. Für einen Einstand, der mit Rosalie Thomass gut besetzt ist, ist das eine verschenkte Chance. Vielleicht wird es ja was mit dem nächsten Fall: "Totholz" ist bereits abgedreht und soll voraussichtlich 2027 laufen.
