Der Tod hat eine Melodie: „Dies irae“ ist überall

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Von Liszt bis „Star Wars“, von Berlioz bis zur „Eiskönigin“: Wenn diese vier Töne erklingen, ist der Tod nicht weit. „Dies Irae“ zieht sich als fixe Idee durch die gesamte Musikgeschichte.

Vier Töne genügen, und alle denken an den Tod. Ob man die Melodie von „Dies irae“ bewusst kennt oder nicht, ob man durch die Musik der Romantik, durch Hollywood oder tatsächlich durch die Kirche mit ihr in Berührung kam: Die Todesbotschaft dieser Töne ist unmissverständlich. Warum eigentlich, fragen sich da Katrin Nussmayr und Wilhelm Sinkovicz in ihrer jüngsten Folge von „Klassik für Taktlose“.

Die Töne „sind nur so gruselig und unheimlich, weil man den Jüngsten Tag damit assoziiert“, meint Sinkovicz. Seit dem frühen Mittelalter gehört das „Dies irae“ zur katholischen Totenmesse, in gregorianischen Chorälen wurden darin die „Tage des Zorns“ beschworen. Dann verselbstständigten sich die Töne: Hector Berlioz ließ dazu die Hexen tanzen, Franz Liszt und Rachmaninow zitierten sie ausgiebig – und als dann noch die Filmmusik im großen Stil aufsprang, wurde „Dies irae“ endgültig zum Leitmotiv des Todes. Luke Skywalker braucht nichts mehr dazu zu sagen, wenn hinter ihm diese ominösen Töne aufragen, in „König der Löwen“ kann man sie gleich mehrfach entdecken – und sogar in der „Eiskönigin“! Nur die Kirche, die hinter der Todesmelodie steckt, hat ihren vielleicht größten Hit mittlerweile aus dem Programm genommen …

In „Klassik für Taktlose“ ergründen Feuilleton-Redakteurin Katrin Nussmayr und Klassikkritiker Wilhelm Sinkovicz gemeinsam die Welt der klassischen Musik: Braucht jedes Orchester einen Dirigenten? Warum verstört Richard Wagner so? Was war an Mozart eigentlich so toll? Wie viel Klassik steckt in „Bohemian Rhapsody“ oder Taylor Swift? Für musikalische Einsteiger und Klassik-Freunde, die’s ein wenig genauer wissen wollen.

Der Podcast erscheint jeden zweiten Samstag auf der Webseite der „Presse“ und überall, wo es Podcasts gibt.

Stevie Wonder is taking legal action against a reported ex-fiancée, filing a lawsuit this week to evict her after she allegedly lived rent-free at his property in Studio City for nearly four decades.

The prolific musician, whose legal name is Stevland Morris, filed a legal complaint in Los Angeles County Superior Court on Thursday against Carmen Efferson, whom he says has been living for free at a home on Oak Glen Drive since July 1988, according to the complaint obtained by The Times. Wonder seeks to evict Efferson after she allegedly refused to allow inspectors into the property earlier in the summer and failed to vacate the home in August, despite receiving a 30-day notice in late July.

Efferson, whom TMZ reported was once betrothed to the Grammy-winning “Songs in the Key of Life” musician, did not immediately respond to The Times’ multiple requests for response on Friday. A legal representative for Wonder, 76, did not comment and did not clarify the singer’s reported relationship with Efferson. Wonder has been married Tomeeka Bracy since 2017.

A former studio engineer for Eminem pleads guilty to a charge of criminal copyright infringement. Prosecutors accuse the man of stealing and selling the rapper’s unreleased music.

According to the musician’s complaint, Wonder and Efferson reached an oral agreement in 1988 that she can occupy the property without paying monthly rent. Tensions began in July this year when representatives for Wonder attempted to access the property multiple times “for purposes of updating and retaining property insurance,” according to an email between Wonder’s legal representatives and Efferson included in the complaint.

One attorney for Wonder notified Efferson on July 23 of a property inspection the following week. Efferson had choice words for the request, emailing another attorney for Wonder on July 25 to “please refrain from your rascist [sic] coldblooded ‘white male privilege’ tone.”

“I’m feeling harassed,” Efferson said, according to screenshots of her email. “Regarding your ORDERS of entering into the place where I am living, I do not consent.”

She ended her email with an explicit statement. An attorney for Wonder responded two days later, informing Efferson that the lawyer who issued the inspection notice “is neither racist, coldblooded, white or male.”

In an email dated July 27, Efferson also threatened to “immediately contact law enforcement” upon further attempts by Wonder’s representatives to enter the property. The next day, attorneys notified Efferson she had 30 days to vacate the home.

“You were given the opportunity to reconsider your decision to deny access and declined,” the notice said, according to the complaint. Efferson was ordered to vacate the property by Aug. 27

Wonder’s legal representatives also claimed they gave Efferson “three additional opportunities to permit entry” for an inspection, but she failed to respond.

The attorneys also notified Efferson that Wonder would file a complaint should she refuse access again.

Wonder — celebrating the 50th anniversary of his seminal “Songs in the Key of Life” this year — is seeking forfeiture of the lease agreement with Efferson, legal fees and statutory damages up to $600.

Ralph Lauren, el sueño americano de la moda

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sus polos, corbatas y chaquetas le han convertido en el diseñador más rico del mundo.

"Nunca he querido estar a la moda; porque, si estás a la moda, acabarás pasado de moda", dijo hace años Ralph Lauren. Lo cierto es que, para triunfar en la industria textil, lo importante no es ir de la mano de las últimas tendencias, sino anticiparlas o, como ha hecho este icono del sector durante décadas, marcarlas.

En la década de 1970, sus prendas triunfaron porque se convirtieron en un símbolo de estatus y pertenencia social. Lauren entendió mejor que nadie que los consumidores no buscaban únicamente polos o chaquetas de calidad, también pretendían proyectar una imagen específica de sí mismos.

Sus creaciones acercaron el lujo a un público más amplio sin renunciar al carácter aspiracional. Un ejemplo de ello fue su reinterpretación de la estética preppy, asociada a los estudiantes de las universidades más elitistas de Estados Unidos, para convertirla en un modo de vestir elegante, atemporal y reconocible en todo el mundo.

Este y el resto de sus creaciones lo convirtieron en el diseñador más relevante de su generación. Al menos, en lo que a números se refiere. Su empresa, la única de la lista Fortune 500 junto a Charles Schwab cuyo nombre es simplemente el nombre y apellido de un fundador vivo, cuenta actualmente con una capitalización superior a los 20.000 millones de dólares, mientras que su patrimonio, de 15.500 millones de dólares, es superior al que jamás han amasado iconos como Giorgio Armani, Calvin Klein y Tommy Hilfiger.

"Ralph ha resistido el paso del tiempo en las altas esferas de nuestra cultura en constante cambio y se ha mantenido innovador, moderno y relevante", destacaba su buen amigo Steven Spielberg en 2023.

Ralph Lauren creció lejos de grandes templos de la moda como París y Milán. Sus padres, inmigrantes judíos de origen bielorruso, cruzaron el Atlántico en busca de una vida mejor que la que tenían en Europa y se instalaron en el neoyorquino barrio del Bronx.

Por entonces, su apellido era Lifshitz, pero él y sus hermanos se lo cambiaron siendo ya adolescentes cansados de las burlas que recibían -la segunda sílaba se pronuncia como mierda en inglés-.

El temprano interés de Lauren por la moda le llevó a trabajar en una tienda de guantes para hombre mientras cursaba Educación Secundaria y así conseguir ingresos extra. Su ambición por destacar en este mundo se vio en el anuario del instituto, donde escribió que aspiraba a ser millonario. Tiempo después dijo que fue una broma, pero uno de sus hermanos aseguró que desde joven "siempre estaba tratando de conseguir más".

Tras servir en el ejército entre 1962 y 1964, trabajó brevemente como vendedor de corbatas y luego pasó a diseñarlas y venderlas él mismo en el Empire State Building. Eran algo más anchas de lo habitual en EEUU, con un estilo europeo que hacía pensar a parte de la clientela que estaban pasadas de moda.

El punto de inflexión en su carrera llegó cuando la cadena de grandes almacenes de lujo Bloomingdale's aceptó vender sus prendas. En un primer momento, le pidieron hacerlas más estrechas y retirar la marca Polo de ellas, pero Lauren se negó. Gracias a ello, su reputación se disparó, pudo lanzar su primera colección completa -cuyo principal éxito fueron las camisas de piqué- y encargarse del vestuario del film El gran Gatsby (1974).

Veintitrés años después, su compañía protagonizó la salida a Bolsa más exitosa de una empresa estadounidense hasta el debut de Michael Kors en 2011. Lauren estuvo al frente del grupo hasta 2015, cuando renunció al cargo de CEO para disfrutar de su rancho de Colorado, Double RL (llamado así por él y su esposa, Ricky).

Michael Gray, former teen idol and 'Shazam!' series star, dies at 75

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

See more from the L.A. Times in Google Search. Set us as preferred

Michael Gray, who played the teenager who could turn into a superhero in the 1970s live-action Saturday morning children’s TV series “Shazam!,” has died. He was 75.

Gray died Oct. 16 from natural causes in Palm Desert, a representative for the actor announced Thursday. No other details were provided.

Based on the comic book superhero Captain Marvel, who first appeared in an issue of Whiz Comics in 1939, “Shazam!” aired for three seasons on CBS from 1974 to 1976. Gray starred as Billy Batson, a teenager who could transform into a grown-up superhero by saying the magic word “Shazam!” In his enhanced form, Captain Marvel possesses powers such as superhuman strength and speed, heightened intelligence and the ability to wield lightning. The character has since been renamed to Shazam.

Richard Riehle, an actor whose credits include ‘Office Space,’ ‘Bridesmaids,’ ‘The Fugitive,’ ‘Star Trek’ and more, has died at age 78.

“It was a lot of fun,” Gray said of the role in a 2012 interview with Fanboy Planet. “I didn’t know how much I would like it until years later when I found out how much of an influence my character was, and the show was, in people’s lives.

“There were such good moral values to the show,” he added. “I meet people [now] who say, ‘You have no idea what you did for me as a child. You helped me get through life.’ ... I didn’t know the show had that much strength and power back then.”

Born Sept. 2, 1951, in Chicago, Gray grew up in Miami Beach before making his way out west to pursue a career in Hollywood. He would frequently grace the covers of teen pop magazines, including Tiger Beat, thanks at least in part to his manager Charles Laufer, the magazine’s publisher.

“The teen idol angle was orchestrated to some degree,” Gray said during a Q&A event in 2012, according to Comic Book Resources. “I was interviewing for ‘The Brian Keith Show’ with Warner Brothers, and they also owned a news company that produced a lot of magazines. They wanted me to be the next David Cassidy. I was on the front cover of ‘Tiger Beat’ and it worked.”

Emmy-winning actor Tom Skerritt, whose decades-long career includes roles in ‘Alien,’ ‘Top Gun’ and TV series ‘Picket Fences,’ died Wednesday. He was 93.

Among Gray’s earliest screen credits were appearances on shows such as “Room 222” (1969), “The Flying Nun” (1970) and “Marcus Welby, M.D.” (1970). His first recurring role was on “The Little People,” also known as “The Brian Keith Show,” where he played a student doctor named Ronnie Collins. Gray also appeared in an episode of “The Brady Bunch” as Marcia’s boyfriend, Jeff.

Gray left acting not long after his run on “Shazam!” after feeling typecast in the roles that were offered to him. His later pursuits included work in children’s theater education and men’s fashion and owning a West Hollywood flower shop.

More recently, Gray’s legacy from “Shazam!” pulled him back into Hollywood. He voiced a version of himself as Sterling Archer’s childhood hero in four episodes of FX’s adult animated series “Archer” from 2015 to 2019. He also appeared in a cameo in the 2023 film “Shazam! Fury of the Gods” where he interacted with the latest incarnation of the superhero played by Zachary Levi.

Gray is survived by his wife, Stacy Benon Gray; his stepchildren Ryan Cooper and Laurel Cooper; and son-in-law Lee Barker.

Fatherland Is One of the Most Quietly Surprising Films of the Year

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Sandra Hüller and Hanns Zischler in Fatherland

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

The title of Pawel Pawlikowski’s extraordinary, compact drama Fatherland has a double meaning. In this somewhat fictionalized story of Thomas Mann’s brief 1949 visit to Germany, the Nobel Prize-winning author, after having lived in exile in California for 16 years, is challenged with finding his mooring in a country he can no longer call home; if it’s his fatherland, it long ago turned its back on him and his ideals. But fatherland can also be a state of mind where fathers live, a state both more specific and more elusive than fatherhood. In Pawlikowski’s telling of this portion of Mann’s story, it’s during this trip that Mann learns of the death of his son, Klaus—also a writer, though not as successful as his father—by suicide. If the elder Mann feels any emotion when he hears this news, it’s frozen inside him, a bubble of feeling isolated on its own ice floe. Fatherland, so secure in its rigid borders, is a cold, cold place.

Mann’s freedom from this interior island prison happens in the film’s last five minutes, a wordless reckoning that’s as quietly moving as anything you’ll see at the movies this year. But that moment wouldn’t hold so much power if Pawlikowski—with the help of his cowriter, Hendrik Handloegten—weren’t so skilled at sketching in the feathery details leading up to it. Pawlikowski is also the director of the 2013 Ida, as well 2018’s Cold War, pictures that, like this one, deftly balance postwar Eastern Bloc realities with the truth that all humans strive for connection and belonging. These aren’t dour movies, but serious, lustrous ones, less “political” pictures than movies about people trying to sort out what living really means.

At the beginning of Fatherland, no one—not even the aged Mann, played here in a quietly extraordinary performance by Hanns Zischler—has figured that out. The movie opens with a long-distance phone call: Mann’s semi-estranged son Klaus (August Diehl), living in Cannes and clearly in emotional distress, has reached out to his sister Erika—we’ll later realize it’s Sandra Hüller’s voice we hear on the other end of the line. Erika, an actress, writer, and onetime race-car driver, has become her father’s assistant, and she now urges Klaus to join her and their father on the trip they’re about to undertake, from California to the “new” barely postwar Germany. In the year of Goethe’s 200th birthday, Mann will be accepting two awards, one in Frankfurt, in the West, the other in the East, in Weimar: his justification is that his literary idol was born in one city and died in the other, though it’s obvious that Goethe couldn’t have imagined that chasm that would open between the two within 200 years' time.

Both cities are in transition, and in crisis, though neither wants to admit as much. In Frankfurt, a gray city crumbling at the corners and still finding its footing after the war, though somehow doing its best to pretend all is well, Mann and Erika are greeted by the mayor and hustled off to a press conference. There, the esteemed man himself answers the respectful but probing questions of journalists. Once they learn that he’ll also be traveling to the East, it’s not lost on them that doing so could make him a target at home in the States, where anti-Communist storm clouds have long been gathering. And there are other, more spiritual questions for him to consider. An American journalist, a woman who, it turns out, is an old friend of Erika’s and possibly a former lover (she’s played by Anna Madeley), asks him where he feels his true home is, now that he’s an American citizen. His answer is courtly but honest. He may live in the United States, he says, “but where my Heimat is, my home, is a much more difficult question.” He’s a man between states, both literally and figuratively.

In Weimar, the chief way to welcome a returning hero is apparently to greet him with a somber folk song sung by a choir dressed in dark clothing; at one point, he’s serenaded by a group of children singing the then-newly pened East German national anthem. Mann accepts it all gracefully, but there’s also something a bit quizzical and lost in his expression. He’s jarred by one alarming moment: a wild-eyed man appears at the hotel suite where he and Erika are staying, begging him to reveal in his speech that the nearby former concentration camp Buchenwald is now being used as a detention center for political prisoners. The man is whisked way by officials before he can finish his spiel, though it’s doubtful any of it has swayed Mann at all. In both the East and West, when it’s time to address the audiences who have eagerly gathered to hear his thoughts, he gives heartfelt speeches, anchored in the legacy of Goethe, about the power of art to transform and sustain not just individuals, but whole nations. He seems to truly believe, as he says at one point, that art doesn’t recognize borders—or at least, he truly wants to believe it.

Through all of this, Hüller’s Erika serves her father dutifully. When necessary, she translates his responses to journalists into fluid French; she attends cocktail parties in his honor, even when it means suffering the indignity of running into an oily, traitorous, Nazi-loving ex-husband she’d rather forget forever; she drives her father through the countryside, and though we can’t see her face as she does so, we wonder if this particular task gives her some joy, given her car-racing past. But through it all, because Hüller is such a subtly expressive actor, almost gossamer in the ways she shifts moods, we see the ways in which her resentments toward her father can’t help simmering to the surface. When she learns of her beloved brother’s death, her composure doesn’t crumple visibly—only somehow we know her emotional reserves are folding in and flattening like a house of cards. And when she sees how little this event seems to trouble her father, she can barely go through the motions of helping him on his mission, which, as he seems to envision it, is to save his homeland with his lofty views on art.

How these two find their way back to one another, and to a place of acknowledged and shared grief, is a kind of unspoken miracle that unfolds in last 15 minutes or so of this elegant, remarkably economical movie. Artistically and emotionally, Fatherland accomplishes more in its 82-minute runtime than any two-hour-plus movie released this year. Cinematographer Lukasz Zal, who also shot Ida and Cold War, renders Fatherland in the same satiny black and white, as well as the same serenely squarish, or Academy, aspect ratio. Every frame is spare, elegant, intimate, and the film moves along so gracefully that you’re barely prepared for how exhilarating, and how quietly devastating, its final moment is. At one point, Hüller’s Erika tells her father that Klaus had once said he felt Bach made him feel life was worth living. Her father shrugs that information off, but the final moments of Fatherland—incorporating a serendipitous conjuring, like a specter, of “Jesu, Joy of Man’s Desiring”—bring it back to him. Can a person have more than one Heimat? Or can that place of spiritual belonging shift over time? Perhaps Bach had the answer. Perhaps he put it in a piece of music, for us to find when we most need it, in a church or even in a movie.

'Wildwood' Is a Love Letter to All Things Portland, Oregon

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

On Location peels back the curtain on some of your favorite films, television shows, and more. This time, we take a look at Wildwood.

There are several times when Prue, the 13-year-old protagonist of fantasy epic Wildwood, seems just as in disbelief of the magical world she inhabits as us viewers. As someone who grew up in Portland, Oregon, she thinks she's already encountered the limits of weirdness, and yet—as one of the giant animal denizens of the Impassable Wilderness (known by locals as Wildwood) is quick to remind her—there she is, speaking with an owl having tea in a waistcoat. Par for the course for the stop-motion studio, Laika's newest creation is a visual feast with soaring eagles the size of commercial airplanes, ancient trees with roots that sprawl across the whole realm, and even an all-out war between worlds reminiscent of the D-Day Landing in Saving Private Ryan. As much as you could lose yourself in these grand spectacles, the film is also emphatic about remembering the magic that imbues just a single blade of grass. As the first Laika movie explicitly set in Portland, the studio looked for inspiration in their own backyards for how to bring the universe of Colin Meloy's titular novel to life. Condé Nast Traveler spoke with Wildwood director Travis Knight on the inspiration behind this exhilarating, stop-motion saga that takes place not too far from home.

While Dripsters isn't a literal spot in Portland's St. Johns neighborhood, the storefront is a nod to Laika's own in-studio coffee shop.

This movie is such a love letter to Portland, from the Voodoo Doughnuts Prue and Mac eat to the Decembrists posters in her room. This is the first Laika film that’s been explicitly set where the studio calls home. How did that influence the filmmaking process?

It was a joy, unlike anything I've ever experienced. Filmmaking for me is such a privilege to be able to do for a living. To be able to tell a story that was set in the place that I was born, the place that I've lived almost my entire life, the place where our studio is, where so many of us live, was a very special experience because we know that place better than anybody, and we could bring that world to life with real specificity, with real personality. The Portland that you see on screen in Wildwood, while it's a stylized reality because it's animation, very much does represent the truth of what it means to live in that part of the world.

One recurring theme in the film is that magic exists in everyday life, in Portland and in the places we least expect. Is the reverse true as well—in the magical realm of Wildwood, are there fingerprints of Portland and the PNW that the audience can look forward to?

There's magic in real life, and there is danger in the fantasy world—both of those things are true. I think when you look at Wildwood the place, not the movie, there are places from Portland, the Pacific Northwest, and Oregon, specifically all over it. Crater Lake is in there, Haystack Rock is, and there's all these different, beautiful places of Oregon that we infused into this fantasy land, and that was a great joy. We treated Wildwood the place as if it was an “Oregon's greatest hits” fantasyland. The idea of treating Oregon on the same plane as some of the great fantasy realms from my youth like Narnia and Oz and Middle Earth was crazy to even think about. It was great to be able to showcase that beauty on the big screen.

Wildwood has fingerprints from all over the Pacific Northwest in its set designs, from Crater Lake to Haystack Rock.

There’s a lot of well-deserved applause for the entirely hand-crafted approach to this film. There's a brilliant blink-and-you-miss-it moment where Prue and Mac pass by an art fair in St. Johns, and a craftsperson selling their wares has a sign at their table saying to get human-made art “before it's gone for good.” For this film, did you look to Portland’s own craft and visual arts culture for inspiration?

That's part of the way I look at the world, and it's where our community is from. That particular sign is a visual gag, but it's also something that you would see in Portland. I also think it is a meaningful statement that is relevant to the film and to our culture at large. There is a huge maker community in Portland. It's a group of people that actually celebrates handcrafted things, and that really is what's beating at the heart of the movie, that the film is a celebration of human creativity, of human innovation, of the human touch, and in a world that's often designed to be frictionless, easier, and automated. I think the human touch is something that's special and deserves to be celebrated and deserves to be preserved.

Stop motion is such a deliberate practice, where every wrinkle is quite literally sculpted. As a director, are there specific details about a place that you gravitate towards or like to use as launch points?

I think when you're approaching a story, you really want to listen to what the story wants to tell you. When I think about Kubo and the Two Strings, I go back to my own experiences when I was exposed to Japan for the first time. I was eight years old with my father, and it was a world unlike anything that I'd ever seen before. It left a huge impact on me, and those were the types of things that I was channeling. When I was making Bumblebee, I thought back to my experience of being exposed to the Transformers cartoons for the very first time. So you start putting these things through your own prism, and with Wildwood, it was very simple because it was the part of the world that I know better than anywhere. Settings, they have their own personality. Ultimately, what is driving all of these things is the theme and the emotion underneath it, and that is the thing that everything else needs to spiral out of. So, in the end, that's really the thing I think about more than anything else: what is the story trying to say, and what is the emotion that we're trying to convey?

One of the most circulated facts about the film is the 16-year timeline it took to bring it to life. How did Portland change in that time, and what did your team want to keep sight of when characterizing the city?

Portland has changed a lot in 16 years, just like the entire world has changed a lot in 16 years. I first read Colin Molloy's manuscript in 2010. The first movie that we came out with [Coraline] was in 2009, so it was only a year after that that I discovered Wildwood. Portland is a very different place now than it was then. Many, many things have happened since then, some very positive, some tragic. We moved the film into pre-production in 2019, seven years ago. I felt like at the time, we were ready to make Wildwood. Then the whole world turned upside down, and we were hit with a global pandemic the likes of which we had not seen in a century. It changed the whole world, and it changed Portland, and when I see the version of Portland on screen in Wildwood, it's the Portland that I know and the Portland that I love. My hope is that, in time, it can become that again.

“Organic shapes have their own unique grammar,” says Knight, which made the studio's most forested movie to date a tall task to animate.

What were the most challenging sets to construct and animate for Wildwood?

Nothing is easy in stop-motion. Everything is difficult in stop motion. But the things that tend to be more manageable are interiors, like a child's bedroom, but even those have so many details that have to be thought of and designed and built and manipulated. What's difficult to do is nature because organic shapes have their own unique grammar. Things like a forest are really difficult to do in stop motion, and I made the decision to set a movie that was almost entirely based in a forest. It's the materials you use, how you build these things, their shape. There's so much beauty to the natural world that's so different and so strange compared to the inorganic world, the things that we humans construct, and most of the film is set in those places. Places like when Prue and Curtis enter Wildwood for the very first time took us a long time to design what that looked like, and it is just so exquisitely gorgeous. It's so beautiful. It took set designers, set dressers, painters, artists, just so many people who built this space to be a work of art. It's something that belongs in a museum, and for a lot of the movie, that moment will pass by within a matter of seconds. What you're seeing is just extraordinary human craftsmanship and artistry on a grand scale, and it's one of the things I love so much about this movie.

How did you incorporate the wildlife and ecosystems of the Pacific Northwest into this film?

As someone who grew up in the Portland area, I have a lot of knowledge of coyotes. We have a lot of knowledge of crows. We're always next to the natural world. I grew up outside of Portland, essentially on the side of a mountain. I spent most of my childhood days wandering around the forest and climbing up trees and discovering things. The film is emotionally about one primal thing, but there are a lot of layers to the story that we're telling, and part of that is that the film is an eco-fable. That was something that we treated seriously, as it’s something that so many of my collaborators care about deeply, and it’s something I care about deeply. We wanted to try to express that in an organic way without feeling like we were emblazoning it in neon that we're making a message movie. The hope is that you want to honor these things in a way that feels authentic. There's no greater teacher to you than observation, and going out in nature, and seeing how the world was formed, independent of how we’ve tried to shape it. Fortunately, in Oregon, one of the many great gifts of the state is that in any direction you go, you will discover it. We poured so much of that into this movie.

From manuscript to big screen, Wildwood took 16 years to create.

Now that you’re on the other side of creating a movie based on where you call home, do you have any words to the wise for any creatives out there who feel daunted to tell their own stories about home?

What is the piece of advice that they leave out on the old chair-side chest? It was something like writing what you know. I don't think that necessarily means write where you're from, but you have a unique way of looking at the world based on your experiences within it. We know our own lives, unlike anyone else, but that's also the thing that we have to offer that no one else can offer. That's the one thing an artist can do that nobody else can do. They know their own experiences, they own their mind, they know their own imagination, which is different than everybody else's. It's the one thing that we can really offer—how you've lived, how you grew up, the people you grew up with, the people you've interacted with. Every place has its own unique characteristics and its own unique personality. Even within that, our experiences are different from the person next to us. The daunting thing shouldn't be how to tell a story about your home, it's how to really honor the things that you're feeling, how to honor your imagination in a way that really does it justice. Those are all things that we strive to do as artists. We have big dreams, we have big feelings, and how can we get that out in a way that it makes sense to somebody, and it makes them care?

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Russlanddeutsche in Kasachstan und der Umgang mit dem Erbe der Sowjetunion

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Kasachstan ringt mit dem Erbe der Sowjetdiktatur. Vor 85 Jahren ließ Stalin Hunderttausende Menschen deportieren. Rund 400.000 Russlanddeutsche wurden nach Kasachstan verschleppt.

Sie will Geschichte lebendig halten. Yunna Kinereish ist 22 und studiert Public Relations in der modernen Glitzer-Hauptstadt Astana. Sie bezeichnet sich selbst als "kasachische Deutsche". "Ich bin Deutsche von meiner Nationalität her. Aber ich fühle mich auch als Kasachin, weil ich die Traditionen in meiner Familie lebe und diese Mentalität habe", sagt sie der DW. Yunna Kinereish ist eine von 223.000 Kasachstan-Deutschen, die heute in der zentralasiatischen Republik Kasachstan leben.

Yunna Kinereish engagiert sich bei der "Republikanischen Vereinigung der Deutschen Kasachstans - Wiedergeburt" in der Jugendgruppe "Diamant". Die Studentin hat kürzlich am Jugendtheater als Schauspielerin das Schicksal ihrer Vorfahren auf die Bühne gebracht. Das Schicksal der Russlanddeutschen, die zunächst ins Land gelockt wurden und ab dem 28. August 1941 unter Diktator Josef Stalin aus ihren angestammten Gebieten in der Sowjetunion zu Hunderttausenden gewaltsam nach Sibirien oder Kasachstan deportiert wurden.

Mindestens 200.000 von mehr als einer Million Menschen verloren durch Aussiedlung, Hunger und Zwangsarbeit ihr Leben. Genau vor 85 Jahren begann das Leid mit einem Erlass des Präsidiums des Obersten Sowjets der UdSSR. Eine direkte Reaktion auf den Überfall des nationalsozialistischen Deutschlands auf die Sowjetunion am 22. Juni 1941.

Menschen, die nichts mit dem Terror der Hitler-Truppen zu tun hatten, wurden kollektiv mitverantwortlich gemacht, sie wurden als Spione und Kollaborateure denunziert.

"Meine Familie ist Teil dieser Geschichte, von diesen schweren Zeiten. Sie wurden vertrieben aus dem ukrainischen Dorf Kleinwärter, einer Siedlung von Deutschstämmigen in der Ukraine." Die Schicksale, die sie und ihre Freundinnen auf die Bühne bringen, berührten sie sehr, sagt sie. "Für mich ist das nicht einfach, weil ich das tief im Herzen alles empfinde. Eigentlich möchte ich weinen. Ich muss aber stark sein, wenn ich im Deutschen Haus auf der Bühne stehe."

Das modern ausgestattete "Haus der Wiedergeburt" in der Hauptstadt Astana ist das Zentrum für deutsche Kultur. Es wird überwiegend durch Fördermittel der Bundesregierung in Berlin finanziert. Hier werden Sprachkurse gegeben, wird Sozialarbeit geleistet und Erinnerungskultur gefördert. Im Zentrum stehen dabei die Themen Deportation und Repression in der Sowjetzeit.

Yunna Kinereish steht nicht nur auf der Bühne, sie dokumentierte die Geschichte auch filmisch. Im vergangenen Jahr hat sie einen Film über inhaftierte deutsche Frauen im Lager Alzhir gedreht. Es war eines der berüchtigtsten Lager des sowjetischen Gulag-Systems.

In Kasachstan entstand während der stalinistischen "Säuberungen" ab 1937 das wohl größte Lagersystem der Sowjetunion. Allein das Lager Alzhir durchliefen 18.000 Frauen. Frauen, die dort inhaftiert wurden, nur weil ihre Männer als Volksfeinde oder Verräter verurteilt worden waren. Unter ihnen waren 167 deutschstämmige Frauen, deren Schicksal die Gruppe "Diamant" im Film zeigt.

Die Gedenkstätte für das Frauenlager Alzhir liegt direkt vor den Toren der neuen Hauptstadt Astana. Die 1,7-Millionen-Einwohner-Stadt ist am Reißbrett entstanden und erst seit 1997 Hauptstadt des größten zentralasiatischen Landes geworden. Kasachstan kann man als einen autoritär regierten Präsidialstaat bezeichnen.

Auf den Straßen von Astana herrscht Dauerstau. Fußgänger sind kaum zu sehen, Fahrradfahrer gibt es nicht. Astana wächst. Überall am Stadtrand: Neubauten. Dahinter öde Steppe, bis man nach etwa einer Stunde Autofahrt das Dorf Akmol erreicht.

Bevor man das eigentliche Museum betritt, durchschreitet man den "Bogen der Trauer"; eine riesige Stahlkonstruktion, die an das Leiden der Frauen und die Zwangsarbeit erinnern soll. Im Museum selbst: persönliche Gegenstände der inhaftierten Frauen, Modelle der primitiven Unterkünfte, Originaldokumente und Fotos.

Unter den Fotos ist auch das von Gertrud Platais, einer Frau mit deutschen Wurzeln, die lange in Alzhir inhaftiert war. Platais stammte aus Niederschlesien, das heute zu Polen gehört. Die Druckerin heiratete Karl Eduard Platais und nahm die sowjetische Staatsbürgerschaft an. Während des "Großen Terrors" in der Sowjetunion wurde ihr Ehemann 1938 verhaftet. Auch Gertrud Platais wurde verurteilt; als "Familienangehörige eines Verräters der Heimat".

Sie wurde nach Alzhir überstellt und musste dort bis 1946 als Stickerin Zwangsarbeit leisten, in einer Region, wo im Sommer 40 Grad Hitze herrschen und die Temperaturen im Winter auf minus 40 Grad fallen. Es folgte eine lebenslange Verbannung in das Großlager Karaganda. Erst 1956 wurde Gertrud Platais rehabilitiert. Ein Einzelschicksal, das den Terror des sowjetischen Systems auf kasachischem Boden dokumentiert.

Das weitaus größte Lagersystem war jedoch der sogenannte Karlag (Karagandinsches Besserungslager). Ein Komplex so groß wie das deutsche Bundesland Thüringen. Der Karlag war nicht nur ein Gefangenenlager, sondern ein riesiger Wirtschafts- und Landwirtschaftskomplex, den zwischen 1931 und 1959 fast eine Million Menschen durchliefen.

Allein zwischen 1940 und 1950 sollen hier rund 10.000 Gefangene gestorben sein: durch Hunger, Kälte, Krankheiten und die Folgen der Zwangsarbeit.

Das Museum liegt mitten in der kasachischen Steppe, in der Nähe der Stadt Karaganda, rund 250 Kilometer südöstlich von Astana. Die Gedenkstätte befindet sich im ehemaligen Verwaltungsgebäude des Lagersystems. Deutsche Historiker und Fachleute runzeln ein wenig die Stirn beim Besuch, denn in diesem Gebäude wurde nicht gefoltert oder drangsaliert, hier wurde verwaltet.

Zu sehen sind nachgebaute Verhör- und Folterzellen. Untermalt wird das ganze durch melodramatische Musik, die überall aus den Lautsprechern quillt: Inszenierung statt Authentizität. Wie auch die Alzhir-Gedenkstätte ist das Karlag Museum nicht besonders gut besucht. Selbst akademisch ausgebildete junge Menschen kennen die Gedenkstätte nicht unbedingt.

"Bei vielen Menschen und Teilen der Politik gibt es in Kasachstan kaum Interesse an einer Aufarbeitung der teils grausamen Geschichte des Landes", sagt Katherine M. Erdmann. Seit rund einem Jahrzehnt beschäftigt sich die amerikanische Anthropologin und Archäologin an der Nazarbayev-Universität in Astana mit dem Gulag-System. "Wir wollten herausfinden, wo die Lager genau waren, und wie man sie heute noch erkennen kann."

Erdman und ihre Studentinnen und Studenten setzten auf moderne Technik wie Drohnenüberflüge. Aber auch Befragungen und Recherchen am Boden gehören dazu. "Die Zwangsarbeit hat in großen Landesteilen die Landschaft geformt", sagt Erdman. Neben den primitiven Unterkünften entstanden oft Betriebe. Häuser wurden errichtet, mit Ziegeln, die in Zwangsarbeit hergestellt wurden. Ihre Erkenntnisse teilen die Forscher direkt mit Nachbarn und den lokalen Verwaltungen vor Ort.

"Oft begegnet uns nur Apathie oder bloße Ablehnung", sagt Erdman. Die breite Öffentlichkeit sei an Geschichtsaufarbeitung kaum interessiert. An die unrühmliche Vergangenheit wollen viele nicht erinnert werden in einer Zeit des wirtschaftlichen und gesellschaftlichen Aufstiegs, beklagt Erdmann.

International wolle man demonstrieren, dass man sich mit den dunklen Seiten der Geschichte befasse, aber oft erlebe sie mangelndes Interesse und sogar Ablehnung. Seit einem Jahrzehnt forscht die Wissenschaftlerin in Kasachstan. Für weitere Lehraufträge fehle die staatliche Finanzierung.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Yunna Kinereish gehört zu den jungen Menschen in Kasachstan, die nicht wollen, dass die Vergangenheit in Vergessenheit gerät. Die Kasachstan-Deutsche aus Astana gibt der DW zum Abschied dies mit auf den Weg: "Meine Vorfahren haben das Gulag-System in Kasachstan überlebt. Für mich ist es deshalb wichtig, die Wurzeln nicht zu vergessen und das von unseren Vorfahren Erlebte und Erlittene weiterzugeben."

Dieser Text ist im Rahmen einer Studienfahrt der "Bundesstiftung zur Aufarbeitung der SED-Diktatur" entstanden. Die aus Steuergeldern finanzierte Stiftung widmet sich seit 1998 der Auseinandersetzung mit der kommunistischen Diktatur in Deutschland, in der DDR, und in anderen Ländern.

In Wicker, a woman and her basket show just how suffocating the institution can be.

The Real Child Protection Cases That Loosely Inspired Fjord

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Renate Reinsve and Sebastian Stan star in Fjord

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

From the Palme d’Or-winning abortion drama 4 Months, Three Weeks, and Two Days (2007), to the institutional corruption-themed Graduation (2016) and beyond, Romanian auteur Cristian Mungiu’s body of work has always concerned itself with the human cost of systemic oppression and bureaucratic faultlines. With the searing legal and familial drama Fjord, which earned Mungiu his second Palme at the 2026 Cannes Film Festival in May, the filmmaker once again brings his signature sense of slow-burning tension to the clash between individual and societal codes of morality. Except this time, he shrewdly flips the script. With the story of a religious immigrant family of seven facing an uphill battle against Norway’s child protection services (known as Barnevernet), Fjord dares to imagine a scenario where well-intentioned progressive values can backfire as severely as rigidly conservative ones if they disregard basic human principles like empathy, compassion, and respect.

Written by Mungiu, Fjord follows Mihai and Lisbet Gheorghiu, a Romanian engineer and Norwegian nurse played by Sebastian Stan and Renate Reinsve, respectively. Set in their modest ways, the couple tries to settle into their lives in Norway, having moved there after the death of Mihai’s parents to be closer to Lisbet’s family. Raising their five children with a conservative ethos, they don’t quite mesh with the liberal community that surrounds them. But their eager kids still build close friendships with the children of their progressive neighbors, Mats and Mia Halberg, a development that further underscores the two families’—and consequently, the two belief systems’—political and practical differences.

Tensions rise in town when the kids’ school raises child-abuse allegations against the Gheorghius after teachers notice some mysterious bruising on one of their kids. While the film never spells out their precise origins, Barnevernet steps in at once, separating their five kids (including a baby, still breastfeeding) from Mihai and Lisbet, placing them in foster care after the couple admits to some physical discipline that would be considered mild in Mihai’s culture, but is against the law in Norway. Claiming that they are being harshly discriminated against due to their religion and conservative beliefs,  the couple asks help from Mia, an attorney, to represent their case in the Norwegian court.

Stan and Reinsve as Mihai and Lisbet Gheorghiu Courtesy of Neon

Joining TIME on Zoom recently, Mungiu emphasizes that “the film is fictional. It’s not a reenactment associated with any one particular case.” Still, there were a number of real-life court cases that intrigued him as a storyteller with a journalism background. Chief among them was that of the Bodnariu family, who occupied international headlines for several months after they were separated from their five children in November 2015. Pentecostal parents Ruth and Marius were accused of domestic violence, with worldwide protests criticizing what dissenters called harsh and hasty steps taken by Norwegian social workers without proper evidence. In Romania, one of the indirect results of the wildly popular case went as far as a constitutional referendum in 2018 that aimed to alter the definition of a traditional family and prevent same-sex marriages. (The referendum eventually failed due to low turnout.)

“So you couldn’t avoid the Bodnariu case,” recalls Mungiu. He followed the story as it remained in the papers, and during that time and the years that followed, Mungiu began to notice other cases that displayed a similar pattern. “Whenever there was this encounter between, let's say, families coming to Scandinavia from more conservative parts of the world, there was a cultural clash.” What eventually convinced him to pursue the topic through a film was a recent-enough realization that the world somehow became more illiberal than it used to be. “I thought that through this story, I could talk about the [confrontational] state of the world today. People are not listening to the other group. I hope that the world can be a bit more rational than this.”

Upon deciding to write Fjord, Mungiu read everything he could online, moving to Norway for several extended periods to investigate the country’s child-protection laws and relevant cases on the ground. His process involved talking to several families involved with child protection cases and their relatives, as well as journalists who covered cases like the Bodnarius’, court interpreters, police officers, NGO representatives, and various different religious groups. “Finally, I talked to Barnevernet about these cases in general,” he adds. “They were willing to also give details about how things have changed.”

One key tool Mungiu didn’t have access to was the recording and transcription of the Bodnariu trial, which is among the reasons why Fjord had to be largely fictional. “Norwegian authorities said only the family could have access to the recording if they came over. But the family knew that they couldn’t come over. Because if they did, they could be arrested,” he explains. “The family was trying to get their justice in the European Court of Justice, but they first had to utilize all the legal procedures [in Norway]. And because they left Norway before having a final decision about their proceedings, they couldn't get a positive answer from the European Court of Justice.”

Vanessa Ceban, at the piano, plays eldest daughter Elia Courtesy of Neon

Mungiu draws a direct connection between Fjord and his previous film, R.M.N. “In that film, I was trying to investigate how a conservative society reacts when someone who’s different from them comes into their world. We know that they’re not very welcoming or willing to accept differences. They don't understand that there's a bigger world out there. In Fjord, I really wanted to reverse this paradigm and to understand what happens when the conservatives are the minority in a progressive, liberal society.” According to Mungiu, we might expect that type of society to be more empathetic and inclusive, treating minorities with more compassion. But through his investigations, he got the sense that whoever’s in the majority feels entitled to impose their own point of view onto others. “This is not a good example that we wish to set for the conservative part of the society,” Mungiu reflects. “We shouldn't be behaving the same way they behave.”

On these grounds, the central question of Fjord is whether anyone can be truly objective toward people whose values they don’t share. The Gheorghius’ conservatism, for example, means they oppose same-sex marriage. So can viewers both disagree with the couple on those grounds, while also feel that they are being treated unfairly by Barnevernet?   It is a challenge that is essentially at the heart of the American justice system, which asks potential jurors to set all their biases aside and only assess the case in hand through proven facts. “I noticed that saying, ‘I'm here to [only] judge if they are guilty of what they are charged with’ isn’t easy, because you are biased by your own point of view of the world. But I hope that this is what the audience is going to do. The reason why I wanted to tell this story from this neutral perspective is that it is a rare opportunity of inviting not just one bubble that you sympathize with in your regular life. The other bubble exists and dialogue is needed to make the society a little bit more peaceful again. So I took this position of a mediator, if you will.”

To accentuate the connection between Fjord’s searching themes and lean visuals, Mungiu purposefully embraced long shots and continuous takes beginning during the scripting phase, in order to fully immerse the audience in the conflicting world of the characters in a neutral fashion. Indicating that he avoids many of the mainstream filmmaking methods that he finds manipulative, “I don't use anything [distracting] within the scene,” Mungiu says. “I don't use coverage. I don't use music. I don't have funny angles. The camera doesn't move unless it follows a movement in the shot. I try to do this in a sober way, just with the actors and their emotions. I think it's a respectful way of telling the audience, ‘You are adults like we are, you are as smart as I am, so feel free to see [for yourself] what's more important in this shot, or who the main character is.’”

Similarly, he carefully scouted locations in sync with his ideas around isolation and lack of communication. “It was important for us to set this story in a small community, with two houses at the end of this fjord in a lovely but somehow curled world,” he explains. “It’s very majestic, and [it’s a location where] people will have to talk to one another eventually because there's nobody else around.”

He remembers an anecdote about the real inhabitants of the two houses he uses in the film for the Gheorghius and Halbergs, who were in fact not communicating with one another. That all changed shortly after the production convinced one of them to allow the film crew to shoot in his house. Mungiu recalls the homeowner saying, “I will let you, but my neighbor is very grumpy. He hasn’t talked to me in the 10 years that we have been neighbors, so you are going to have problems with him." But when the crew contacted the owner of the other house, they found that he was quite open-minded. “We asked him, ‘Do you have a conflict with your neighbor?’ He said, ‘No, but none of us have started a conversation, so I let him enjoy his privacy.’ At the end, they were communicating normally, and we had a very good relationship with both of them.”

No matter how neutral Mungiu tried to stay throughout his research process, there were some moments that made him face his own biases. “I went [to Norway] with this idea that I will meet a society that is not at all religious. This is the idea that we project onto the Protestant north.” As such, he was surprised to notice deeply rooted religious leanings there, especially among older people with a connection to the Norwegian church. He also learned about the younger population’s attraction to new neo-cult movements. “I watched at least a couple of documentaries about this phenomenon. People are really worried that the younger generations are [drawn to] these new cults. So I noticed that the people there are just as inclined to have religious beliefs as any other.”

With this discovery, Mungiu eventually eliminated various scenes in Fjord which would have insinuated that Norwegian people saw the world in a more rational way only because they weren’t religious. “Making decisions on rational grounds is of course a very good thing to do,” Mungiu explains. “But [there is also] this need of belonging to something bigger than just [rationality] that we all feel. Some people still long for the discomfort that there's something bigger than them. And as long as this belief places them on the right side of the world through [tolerance] and [inclusivity], I think we should be tolerant to it.”

His time in Norway also led Mungiu to learn more about the Sami people. “I didn’t know much about them before, but the Sami population sets a good [historical] example about how bigger communities and countries were not acting in an ethical way towards minorities. You can see this in the U.S. as well, [in the way] Native Americans were treated,” he adds. “And I think that we should learn from this [that] maybe this ‘we want to civilize you’ idea is not the most ethical one,” he reflects, clarifying that while everyone should obey the laws and respect the culture of the country they live in, real integration cannot happen by enforcing one’s own values onto others. “That won't change [the way] someone thinks. You will just frustrate them, [reinforcing] that there is a hypocrisy in the world.”

Mungiu cautions against the ongoing ideological war that he says is moving in the wrong direction across the globe. “I don't want to be preachy, but hopefully there's something that stays with you after you watch a film like Fjord,” he reflects. “I took the risk of talking about things that people need to listen to even if they’re not nice. Don't kill the messenger. It's time to start to have a dialogue and this is what cinema can do.”

Political Skirmishes in “Other Desert Cities” and “A Bright Room Called Day”

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

On the night that I attended the current Broadway revival of Jon Robin Baitz’s 2010 play, “Other Desert Cities,” there was a mad clapper at large, a mercenary determined to let us know whose side he was on. It was clear which actor he favored: Joe Keery, who shot to fame on the Netflix hit “Stranger Things” as the moody, mulleted Steve Harrington, a small-town doofus drafted into a teen militia. In “Other Desert Cities,” Keery plays a different sort of conscript, the quietly perceptive reality-TV producer Trip Wyeth, a louche charmer negotiating a ceasefire between his neocon parents and his sister, Brooke, who is threatening to spill the family’s secrets in the pages of this very magazine. I have no idea whether the clapper was a Keery superfan or just agreed with Trip’s critique of his family, but he wasn’t wrong on either count, with the actor delivering a career-making performance: loose, bright, and unpredictable. After his best speech—a witty stem-winder that ends with the declaration “I am California! And California is not happy!”—I was tempted to clap, too.

Unfortunately, the starry ensemble surrounding Keery felt unevenly weighted and off-kilter, like billiard balls only occasionally landing in the pocket. Baitz’s story is set in Palm Springs, in December, 2004, at the home of Polly and Lyman Wyeth, an opulent sandstone aerie with meditative views of the desert, a massive Christmas tree, and portraits of eminences like Barry Goldwater. The Wyeths are Republican grandees and L.A. royalty: Polly (Julia Louis-Dreyfus, all sparkle and venom) is a retired screenwriter, Lyman (a craggy, mournful Ed Harris) a former Hollywood cowboy. Polly, a verbal sharpshooter in a family of clay pigeons, is a dab hand at witticisms like “Sarcasm is the purview of teen-agers and homosexuals,” but if she’s a casual bigot she’s also a reinventor, a steerer of fates: a Jewish go-getter from Texas, she co-wrote frothy commercial films with her sister, then married into Wasp aristocracy with Lyman, who became a government ambassador after their pal “Ronnie” got elected President. By the time we meet the couple, however, that’s ancient history: the Iraq War is raging, and they rarely leave Palm Springs, where they’re in a right-wing bubble, barely tolerating Polly’s now estranged sister, Silda, a lefty dipsomaniac fresh out of rehab, who is played with leggy relish by Allison Janney. The plot revolves, as it inevitably does in shows like this, around a collective trauma. Here, it’s the loss of a third sibling, a radical son who, in the chaos of the seventies, blew up his life and warped the family trajectory. That’s the dark story Brooke is ready to tell the world in a manuscript she’s brought from Sag Harbor—the world’s least welcome Christmas present.

This material should feel resonant, given how many families are split right now—over MAGA, over Israel—and how much of a default confessionalism has become online. (These days, Brooke could post an incendiary tweet storm and skip the airfare.) The script is full of pungent, funny debates about power, with observations that still land. “Families get terrorized by their weakest member,” Polly argues. And Trip tells Brooke, “Isn’t it revenge enough that everyone worries about you all the time?” If the exposition creaks, the cast is sprightly, and, for a long time, it’s a blast to watch them spar, particularly Louis-Dreyfus, who scores laughs off the smallest phrases—a disgusted “amongst the Basques,” say. Together, she and Harris capture something formidable about the slice of Old Hollywood the Wyeths represent—sybaritic and hypocritical, brined in self-pity but with a fierce, glamorous quality.

The primary problem is, unfortunately, Lily Rabe, who plays Brooke in a different, blunter register than everyone else onstage. I’ve seen Rabe give beautiful, varied performances, from her dignified Hermione in this past summer’s “The Winter’s Tale” to her demon-possessed nun in “American Horror Story,” gyrating saucily to “You Don’t Own Me.” In “Other Desert Cities,” she’s all sulk and grimace. To be fair, it’s a hard role: Brooke is a solipsistic bummer, a frustrated writer who spends months in a mental hospital being tended to by her mother, then ambushes her with a score-settling tell-all. Instead of seeming righteous but flawed, Rabe’s Brooke validates Polly’s harsh judgment that she’s a reckless brat convinced her truth is the only truth. When, in the final act, a twist reframes the Wyeths’ behavior as, if not fully sympathetic, more understandable, it just made me furrow my brow. By then, it was hard to imagine Brooke’s parents opening up and easier to imagine them manipulating her—defusing, by any means necessary, their daughter’s incendiary literary device.

Even so, I had fun. I loved every costume and considered trying to dress more like a Palm Springs Republican, as Polly would want me to. And there was even, perversely, a touch of escapism to the experience. “Other Desert Cities,” so topical in 2010, now feels as if it were written in the Paleolithic era. If the Wyeths are monsters, they’re also dinosaurs, eclipsed by the nihilists who followed. There’s a wonderful moment when Harris puts on a show, mimicking the death scenes he specialized in onscreen: he buckles at phantom gunshots, then mimes shock, his torso atilt. It was his one true skill, Lyman says, modestly, with a Reaganite twinkle—he always knew how to make an exit.

Two nights later, I went to Crown Heights to catch a revival of a play that felt much more timely, despite having débuted in 1987 and being set in the nineteen-thirties. A tiny, passionate production of Tony Kushner’s “A Bright Room Called Day,” directed by Rachel Gita Karp, was being staged in a town house in the heart of Brooklyn’s so-called Commie Corridor, which helped steer Mayor Zohran Mamdani to victory. As I reclined on a sofa, sipping rosé, a cadre of Berlin bohemians—Reds, fellow- travellers, anarchists, and homosexuals; actors, socialist-realist painters, and writers—began celebrating New Year’s Eve, 1932, at a kitchen table just a few feet away. Lifting glasses of champagne, they toasted, giddily, to “health, happiness, and relative safety on this fierce and splendid night and for many years to come.” It flashed me back to nights of political hope in recent decades, and I felt sick.

“Day” is Kushner’s début—his early problem play, which premièred, in San Francisco, four years before “Angels in America.” At the time, the project felt hyperbolic, largely because of the finger-wagging interventions of Zillah, a modern-day punk anarchist who ranted about Reagan as a new Hitler. (With “Angels,” Kushner found a more fruitful comparison: Roy Cohn and the Red Scare.) Zillah’s speeches have since been reworked, most notably in a 2019 production in which a Kushner-y character called Xillah debated her, framing the story with an anxious meta-narrative. By then, the Hitler comparisons had a different feel: if Kushner had once been the boy who cried wolf, the wolf was at the door.

In 2026, the gemlike Brooklyn venue lent Kushner’s project a magical quality, turning the audience into ghosts from the future, eavesdropping on the fall of the Weimar Republic but unable to intervene. There was no meta frame, and although Zillah still chimed in, it was in smaller doses—and the truth is, we no longer needed her guidance. As the characters playfully debated the rise of Fascism, the dialogue often felt transcribed from some Brooklyn polycule. Is class the best lens for understanding Hitler’s appeal, or is daddy worship? Can anything be learned from sleeping with Nazis? When, ultimately, is it time to flee? The cast was excellent, particularly a sad, sexy Molly Rose Heller as Agnes, a movie actress clinging to her perfect Berlin rental, Jonathan Bock as her Trotskyite beau, a poignant Portal Rose as a sexual adventurer, and Aja Downing as a morally malleable starlet toying with working for the Reich.

Still, the real star was Kushner’s text, a message in a bottle. Witty, bitter, flamboyant, and emotionally grounded, the dialogue swings, delightfully, between intimate exchanges and grandiloquent soliloquies, capturing the vulnerability of idealists in the face of evil. When the Devil himself appeared, in a puff of smoke, the town-house alarm went off and the play paused. For a moment, it all seemed, surreally, to be part of the production: yet another alarm to ignore so that the show could go on. ♦

Spooky-Season Theatre to Chill You to the Bone

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When it comes to horror, I’m a wimp. But, as a theatre critic, I’m fascinated by what catches on with audiences, and this season the genre is, unusually, front and center. The flagship production is Broadway’s “Paranormal Activity” (at the August Wilson, through Feb. 28), whose popularity recently prompted an eight-week extension of its run. Cunningly adapted by Levi Holloway from the hit movie franchise, the play follows Lou and James, a young couple who have moved to London from Chicago, hoping that the change will improve Lou’s mental health. Spoiler alert: it doesn’t.

Off Broadway, Danny Robins’s “2:22: A Ghost Story” (Lucille Lortel; through Jan. 17) also features a couple beset by troubles in their new home; the wife’s claim of a nightly haunting is put to the test when dinner guests stay up with them to witness it. Jamie Armitage’s “A Ghost in Your Ear” (Minetta Lane; through Dec. 12) equips audiences with headphones at the top of the show, literally plugging them into what happens onstage: an audiobook-recording session in which the story of a sadistic father seeps into the sound booth.

If that’s not immersive enough, try Candle House Collective’s “claws,” by Evan Neiden, who specializes in remotely staged shows. In this one, you, the sole audience member, receive a phone call from a teen-ager begging for help, and it’s up to you to advise him. Still insufficiently realistic? Consider a ghost tour of Merchant’s House, a residence turned museum where supernatural phenomena have been reported since the death of its last occupant, in 1933. When the building closed to the public during COVID, researchers investigated. Now one of them leads a 9:30 P.M. tour, in which he shares findings that suggest—wait for it—paranormal activity.—Dan Stahl

The singer-songwriter Yebba spent much of her initial breakout in mourning. A soloist in the classic soul and gospel traditions, she possesses a commanding and hypnotic voice that feels unignorable. But just as a performance of her original song “My Mind” was going viral, in 2016, her mother died. The elegant, sumptuous 2021 début she released in the aftermath, “Dawn,” dealt in processing that loss, rather than developing her personal style. This March, Yebba returned with the album “Jean,” and with a new directive: more reined-in, inward-looking music that is just as vulnerable yet focussed less on the power of her voice than on its character.—Sheldon Pearce (Brooklyn Paramount; Nov. 2-3.)

About a decade ago, Oona Doherty, an Irish choreographer of rare and risk-taking honesty, made a name for herself with danced portraits of Belfast lads. Reacting to sudden fame, she swerved in other directions, but she has since swerved back with “Specky Clark.” A dance with dialogue, fuelled by the punkish sound of the Irish folk band Lankum, it takes the story of her great-great-grandfather working in an abattoir as a child and pushes it into Lynchian fantasy horror. On Samhain, the Gaelic festival during which boundaries between the living and the dead blur, he follows a pig into the spirit world. “Specky Clark” comes to the Irish Arts Center in time for Halloween.—Brian Seibert (Oct. 25-Nov. 15.)

American Ballet Theatre’s fall season is short and sweet, with three mixed bills plus a gala (Oct. 29). The latter includes both Frederick Ashton’s poetic male solo “Dance of the Blessed Spirits”—danced by the equally poetic Daniel Camargo—and Michel Fokine’s rococo “Le Spectre de la Rose.” In one program, Ashton’s undeniably magical “The Dream” (based on Shakespeare) will be performed alongside a new sextet, “Trio Élégiaque,” produced by a young dancer in the company, Brady Farrar. Another program is anchored by Natalia Makarova’s suite from the nineteenth-century Spanish-themed ballet “Paquita,” a tour de force of classical dance. And yet another offers a glimpse of Kyle Abraham’s stylish ensemble work “Mercurial Son,” in which the men of the company, in particular, have a chance to shine.—Marina Harss (David H. Koch; Oct. 21-31.)

“The Curious Case of Benjamin Button” is a stunner—a gorgeously performed, gloriously staged, philosophically perverse fairy-tale musical that left me swooning. You may know the premise even if you haven’t read F. Scott Fitzgerald’s short story: a baby is born as an elderly man and ages backward; along the way, he struggles, has worldly adventures, and falls in love. Andrew Durand, with his handsome crab-apple features, makes a perfectly quizzical Benjamin; Kelsee Kimmel is stirring as his true love, who shares his age just once. The harmony-singing, instrument-playing ensemble is terrific, and the show’s strong score, by Darren Clark and Jethro Compton (who also wrote the book and directed), has a touch of “Hamilton” and a streak of “Come from Away.” Everything clicks: even the moon, swinging from a green net above the clever nautical set, stole the show.—Emily Nussbaum (Public Theatre; through Dec. 6.)

“The Shape of the Rock That’s Hurling Towards the Sea,” from 2022.

A terrible weight hangs over “Burning in the Eyes of the Maker,” an exhibition of recent paintings by Celeste Dupuy-Spencer. The heaviness comes from the fact that the artist died in April, at age forty-six, but it’s also embedded in the paintings on view—in the bursts of fire and swirling floods that mark them. Dupuy-Spencer had a reputation for clear-eyed renderings of both the tenderness and the brutality of our world; here, darker forces prevail. Mythical beings hover over apocalyptic scenes crowded with tiny, ghostly figures, which seem to emerge from the paint. The monumental four-panel piece “The Shape of the Rock That’s Hurling Towards the Sea” (2022) is a parable in which only the rich seem to survive. But there’s one saving grace: the yellows, oranges, and reds of Dupuy-Spencer’s fires are achingly beautiful.—Jillian Steinhauer (Jeffrey Deitch; through Oct. 31.)

The historical drama “Fatherland,” written and directed by Paweł Pawlikowski, zeroes in on an intriguing literary byway: the elderly, Nobel Prize-winning German novelist Thomas Mann’s trip to Germany in 1949, sixteen years after his self-exile upon Hitler taking power. The tale is energized by the thoughtful performances of Hanns Zischler, as Mann, and Sandra Hüller, as the author’s daughter Erika, who accompanies him while reviving conflicts from her own past. But the story, set amid Cold War tensions, and involving Mann’s vain belief in the importance of his visit to reconciling East and West, along with his nostalgic dream of a politics-free realm of art, is told with restrained images and a terse script that leave little room for dramatic complexity.—Richard Brody (In limited release.)

Michael Schulman on downtown entertainers making grand returns.

1. The sui-generis musical “Hedwig and the Angry Inch” originated as a gender-flouting, genre-busting phenomenon at the Jane Street Theatre, in 1998, performed by John Cameron Mitchell as the titular bewigged East German rocker. Mitchell wrote, directed, and starred in a movie version in 2001. (The songs are by Stephen Trask.) To celebrate the film’s twenty-fifth anniversary, Mitchell is touring the country with 4K screenings, followed by Q. & A.s and acoustic performances. The tour hits the Beacon Theatre on Nov. 16. Wigs welcome.

2. The dearly departed Hulu sitcom “Difficult People” ran 2015-17, starring the comedians Julie Klausner (its creator) and Billy Eichner as snarky show-biz aspirants. The series was thick with pop-culture barbs, including ahead-of-the-curve digs at Kevin Spacey. Klausner and Alex Scordelis wrote an unproduced screenplay, “Difficult People: The Movie,” which she and other members of the original cast—including Eichner, Andrea Martin, James Urbaniak, and Gabourey Sidibe—will read at The Town Hall on Nov. 3, along with such special guests as Paul Walter Hauser, Cecily Strong, and Michael Urie.

3. The drag king Murray Hill has the look of a mustachioed Atlantic City lounge lizard and the shticky comic delivery of Don Rickles. In his moving new memoir, “Showbiz! My Life as a Middle-Aged Man,” Hill recounts his abusive childhood; his self-acceptance as a trans man; his breakout in New York’s downtown cabaret scene in the nineteen-nineties and then the burlesque revival of the two-thousands; the fire that destroyed his apartment on his fiftieth birthday; and his role as the bon vivant Fred Rococo on the HBO series “Somebody Somewhere.”

“She Made Me Do It”: a famed equestrian instructor shot his most annoying student.

Parents disagreed on whether to vaccinate their children. Then the kids got sick.

In the biotech world, you can’t see everything

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A reporter’s unsettling takeaway from You Can See Everything.

Earlier this week, I attended a screening of You Can See Everything, the buzzy new documentary about the disgraced former biotech CEO Elizabeth Holmes.

I went in not really knowing what to expect. I’d avoided spoilers (and won’t be giving anything away here, either!). I was wondering what new information this film could reveal, given how familiar many of us are with the Theranos story thanks to years of media coverage, the best-selling book Bad Blood, and the Hulu series The Dropout, starring Amanda Seyfried.

Like many others who have seen the film, I came away feeling unsettled. But not just about Holmes’s fraud and the somewhat disturbing interactions between the subjects. The film reignited a concern I’ve had about covering science for most of my career: Despite our best efforts, we never can actually see everything.

In case you’re rusty on the details, Elizabeth Holmes dropped out of Stanford University in 2003 to run Theranos, a diagnostics company she founded, when she was just 19. She went on to claim that her company had invented a way to run over 100 diagnostic tests on just a few drops of blood.

Holmes raised hundreds of millions of dollars for Theranos, which was valued at $9 billion in 2014. Forbes named her the youngest self-made female billionaire—she was worth $4.5 billion when she was just 30 years old.

Things started to unravel in 2015, when an investigation by the Wall Street Journal raised concerns by former Theranos employees that the tests didn’t work. Some also said that the company’s device had not been tested properly, and that blood samples had been analyzed on machines from other companies instead. MIT Technology Review named it one of the biggest technology failures of 2015.

Forbes had revised Holmes’s worth to $0 by 2016. That year, a damning government report said that the company’s lab was struggling with basic procedures and gave patients results even when they knew they could be wrong.

In 2018, Theranos, Holmes, and former company president Ramesh Balwani were charged with “massive fraud.” In 2022, she was sentenced to a little over 11 years in prison for defrauding investors. She began serving her sentence in May 2023.

It was around a month before this that filmmakers Nathan Fielder, Lance Oppenheim, and their colleagues began documenting Holmes and her partner, Billy Evans, at home with their two young children. I’m not giving anything away when I say it’s an extremely intimate portrayal—at least of how the two adults interact with Fielder, who is also a central character in the film.

As you might know if you’ve seen the trailer, there are plenty of super close-ups. I felt as if I spent most of the film staring straight into the faces of those people. And almost the whole way through, they all seemed to be trying to figure each other out. Holmes is known for her dishonesty, but she invited cameras into her home in an apparent bid to reframe the narrative and position herself as an honest person with an unusual mind. Will anyone believe her?

I couldn’t help feeling some empathy for Holmes, a mother we see preparing to leave her two kids for a lengthy prison sentence. And even though I have long known the facts, Holmes is clearly an effective manipulator. You can see her plant the seeds of doubt in Fielder’s mind. At times, I felt she was convincing me, sitting in my cinema seat. Maybe she really didn’t mean to lie?

But then I remembered how other journalists felt after being taken in by her claims. “I blew it,” Fortune’s Roger Parloff reflected in 2015, a year after profiling Holmes and her company. “I’m pretty sure she lied to me,” STAT’s Matthew Herper wrote recently, having interviewed Holmes in 2015. Even the former employee turned whistleblower Tyler Shultz, who knew the product didn’t work, said that he could “have a five-minute conversation with her and walk away feeling like my work was changing the world,” he said in 2022.

I didn’t cover Theranos in the 2010s. If I’d interviewed Holmes, would I have fallen for it all?

One of the early red flags in the Theranos case was the lack of published, peer-reviewed data. Much of the company’s work was classified as a “trade secret.” Scientists tend to publish their work in journals—a slow process that involves having the work evaluated by often anonymous experts in the field, who provide feedback, highlight questionable conclusions, and often ask for clarification. Peer review is meant to add rigor to the publication process. You should be able to trust published scientific papers.

Scientists aren’t perfect. Some will exaggerate their results or inflate their importance. Some manipulate data to serve a hypothesis. Sometimes these infractions are caught during peer review, but not always. There are plenty of examples of scientific findings that could never be replicated by other labs and ended up collapsing under the weight of real evidence. Many papers ultimately end up with corrections. Some are withdrawn—10,000 research papers were retracted in 2023.

At any rate, there’s been a shift to sharing research in other ways. Today, many scientists opt to publish their work at preprint servers like arXiv, medRxiv, and bioRxiv before they submit them to established journals. Those outlets bypass peer review. So do company press releases, social media posts, and many conference presentations. And I get a lot of my stories from conversations and interviews.

I’ll admit I’ve looked at a scientific finding and thought: A scientist has just said they’ve done that. How can I be sure they really have?

I like to think that I hold myself to high journalistic standards here, as does everyone at MIT Technology Review. I would never publish a story about some new scientific development without doing my own form of peer review, and asking scientists—ideally people I know and trust—what they think of a new claim.

But the film is a stark reminder of how easy it can be to fall for an appealing narrative. While most scientists are, I hope, pretty honest, and Holmes is certainly an outlier, we should all continue to interrogate the claims being fed to us. If you’ve seen the film, let me know what you think!

This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here.
