What Is Nobody Wants This’ Sasha-Morgan Endgame?

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Justine Lupe and Timothy Simons in Nobody Wants This Season 3

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

This article discusses the full third season of Nobody Wants This.

Joanne and Noah are going to make it. Two seasons of so many highs and lows, will-theys and won’t-theys, makeups and breakups had gotten a bit repetitive. So it comes as a relief that Season 3 of the Netflix romantic comedy Nobody Wants This all but guarantees us Kristen Bell’s formerly secular sex podcaster and Adam Brody’s hot rabbi will live happily ever after. With Joanne taking conversion classes, the couple checks off commitment milestones in quick succession: cohabitation, then engagement, and, in the finale, an unplanned exchange of wedding vows after the bride emerges, officially Jewish, from an ocean mikvah. It’s a lovely, duly earned culmination of their interfaith courtship. But it raises the question of where the show can go from here.

The season’s other main storyline—and sole substantive source of conflict—suggests that creator Erin Foster and her co-showrunners, Jenni Konner and Bruce Eric Kaplan, are staking its future on the relationship between Joanne’s erratic, underachieving sister, Morgan (Justine Lupe), and Noah’s eccentric, underachieving brother, Sasha (Timothy Simons). Morgan is fresh off her latest ill-advised romance, an ethically dubious relationship with her ex-therapist (Arian Moayed) that imploded at their engagement party. Sasha is separating from his wife, Jackie Tohn’s Esther. (This is her choice, probably because writers remain sensitive following complaints about the portrayal of Jewish women in the first season and wanted to avoid making hot, blonde Morgan the cause of the split.) And so the best buddies, who’ve been flirting since Season 1, are finally single at the same time. But is it actually a good idea for the show to bring them together, as it briefly does in the second half of this season? I’m of two minds about that.

Justine Lupe and Timothy Simons in Nobody Wants This Season 3 Netflix

What the Morgan-Sasha romance has going for it is the unlikely, off-kilter yet undeniable chemistry between Lupe and Simons, which has somehow surpassed even the electric connection of Bell and Brody’s soulmates. (Let’s take a second to appreciate the versatility of Simons, whose other great performance was as Veep’s toxic West Wing staffer Jonah, a character who would never be attractive to anyone.) Morgan is a brash, blunt, benignly self-absorbed icon in her own mind who also, in her darker moments, fears she’ll never get her life together. Awkward, self-deprecating Sasha is genuinely dazzled by her. For a guy who sacrificed his youth to marriage and fatherhood, she represents all the fun he never got to have. And because they’ve never seemed like viable partners for each other, they have a better rapport than either could manage on a date with someone who’s more their type. “Act the way you act with me,” Morgan advises Sasha as he apprehensively delves into the apps. “You should pretend that you’re talking to me every time you talk to anyone, because I bring out the most charming side of you.” She’s conceited, as usual, but she also happens to be right.

The most refreshing aspect of their friendship is their mutual honesty. So it was clever of Foster, Konner, and Kaplan to make that the driver of their storyline this season. When Sasha fills in for Joanne on the sisters’ podcast, his banter with Morgan is, of course, gold. He explains how he has used the Let Them theory to make peace with professional setbacks—then she calls B.S., and goes on to improvise her own Make Them theory, a pure expression of her aggressive approach to getting what she wants. The episode goes viral. Morgan starts to see a future for herself as a self-help author. Sasha is there to encourage her to dig deeper when her early attempts at writing fall flat. And she can be counted on to inform him that his “lucky shirt” is not exactly the chick magnet he imagines it to be. Yet they also frequently build each other up.

Each faces more disappointment as the season goes on. Morgan blows a meeting with a publisher (Poorna Jagannathan, in her third of four high-profile TV role this year. Hey, I’m not mad about it). Sasha tries to force a connection with a woman who gets confused when he mentions the social contract. So they’re both in a tender place when Morgan impulsively invites him to accompany her to New York, where she’s been booked to talk Make Them with Andy Cohen on Watch What Happens Live. The season’s seventh episode is almost entirely devoted to the trip. That it is such a pleasure to, er, watch what happens between them is a testament to the power of great characters to reinvigorate old genre tropes. From the shopping montage where Sasha buys Morgan a dress, Pretty Woman style, to her earnest speech as they walk the High Line, to the false starts that end with them in bed, familiar rom-com beats feel exciting because of the affection we have for these two. Morgan’s epiphany that, “When I was younger, I always fell for people I wanted to be seen with. And now I think I just wanna be with someone who sees me,” could be a Carrie Bradshaw koan. It hits, though, because it’s so true to her character arc.

Timothy Simons and Justine Lupe in Nobody Wants This Season 3 Katrina Marchinowski—Netflix

But what’s the endgame for Sasha and Morgan? Presumably in order to save some material for Season 4, Nobody Wants This pulls them apart just hours after their hookup. Sasha awakens to a note that says Morgan has hopped on an earlier flight home. There, she crashes out in bed, resigned to the impossibility of a long-term relationship with her future brother-in-law. When it dawns on Joanne that her sister is serious about Sasha, she gives Morgan her blessing. Yet by the time Morgan tracks him down and delivers another big monologue (“I don’t wanna half-ass you”), he’s decided he can’t endure her avoidance so soon after Esther left him. Then he sleeps with Esther, who’s dating a rich guy a full generation her senior (Steven Weber), laying the groundwork for an absolutely disastrous encounter between Joanne’s and Noah’s families at the mikvah.

No end to the season could have been more apt than Joanne and Noah pledging their eternal love while, in a gorgeous beachfront rental just steps away, their parents and siblings tear each other apart. And, again, the scenes Sasha and Morgan share have become some of the most exhilarating of the show. But Nobody Wants This has always been better with isolated situations and screwball dialogue than it is with big-picture plot. With that in mind, I worry it’s written itself into another corner with this relationship. A Season 4 love triangle, with Sasha forced to choose between Esther and Morgan, could only lead back to the same old referendum on Jewish women. If the two Jewish brothers did both end up with the two gentile sisters, that would make its own, somewhat discouraging statement on the latter issue.

Even if you can get past those implications, it’s hard to imagine Morgan and Sasha’s long-term romance arc going anywhere worthwhile, narratively speaking. We’ve already seen the culture clash, the parental pushback, the conversion drama. Are we going to do the same thing all over again, with the same abrasive mother-in-law, brief breakups, and tearful reunions—except with lower stakes because Sasha isn’t a rabbi? I’m not saying nobody wants this. I just need to be convinced.

“You Can See Everything” and the Nathan Fielder Effect

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sign up to receive our weekly cultural-recommendations newsletter.

Nathan Fielder’s onscreen persona—flat, literal-minded, and seemingly baffled by ordinary social behavior—has made him one of our most distinctive cultural figures. On his Comedy Central series “Nathan for You,” his approach collided with the eagerness of ordinary people to be agreeable, and, on the HBO series “The Rehearsal,” it became a kind of social science, as Fielder staged elaborate simulations of real life. Fielder’s latest project, “You Can See Everything,” a documentary he made with the director Lance Oppenheim, trains that deadpan gaze on Elizabeth Holmes, the disgraced founder of Theranos, who invited the filmmakers into her home in the weeks before she reported to prison for defrauding investors. On this episode of Critics at Large, Vinson Cunningham, Naomi Fry, and Alexandra Schwartz discuss the film and the strange mirroring between its two central figures: Fielder, performing an unnerving bluntness, and Holmes, wide-eyed and doll-like, who seems to remember almost nothing and insists she did nothing wrong. The hosts also consider what the movie says about the documentary form itself, and whether it can, or should, get at the truth. And with “You Can See Everything” earning fifteen million dollars in its opening weekend, a remarkable figure for a documentary, they ask what it says about us as viewers that we’re so caught in Fielder’s web. Schwartz says it speaks to “our very human need to trust people while knowing that no one is really fundamentally trustworthy, but wanting everyone to be.” As she puts it, “We’re all kind of on trial here, too.”

In the funny and sexy 'Wicker,' the perfect man is society's worst nightmare

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

See more from the L.A. Times in Google Search. Set us as preferred

Reason No. 1 to watch “Wicker” is the natural intrigue of seeing Alexander Skarsgård molded out of twigs: face, arms, pecs and abs — and yes, there’s also a shot of that.

Reason No. 2 is that the married filmmaking team of Alex Huston Fischer and Eleanor Wilson have made a mischievous, crowd-pleasing folktale with several sweet beats of romance. Their wicker man isn’t an object of punishment, like the one that made Nicolas Cage flail around screaming about bees. His creator, an unnamed basket-weaver (Peter Dinklage), braided him as a mate for a lonely virgin, a fisherwoman (Olivia Colman) who stomps around her rustic village smelling like sushi and tossing her head back in an Eliza Doolittle guffaw.

In public, the fisherwoman acts tough. But alone in her candlelit cottage, she’s visibly weary of her joyless routine. The liveliest noise in her spinster hovel is the sound she makes using the chamber pot. Once married, however, she wakes up the whole town screaming with delight; her wicker spouse is the handy type who breaks the bed at night and fixes it in the morning. His competence is a major turn-on.

Fischer and Wilson’s bawdy fable is predominately about how this flammable hunk makes the rest of the villagers burn up with jealousy. He’s not like the other men — he’s better. The time period is vaguely medieval and the accents British, save for Skarsgård, who speaks in a flat, neutral exhale, the sound of breeze riffling through straw. Meanwhile, Dinklage’s pizzicato mutterings always sound like he’s about to burst into song.

Based on “The Wicker Husband,” a 10-page short story by Ursula Wills-Jones, “Wicker” is pitching itself as kooky smut, which it is, for about 15 worthwhile minutes. Romantasy is a hot-selling genre. Forget Fabio: People are reading bodice-rippers about sexy dinosaurs, gargoyles, aliens, orcs and even trees. (Those curious to explore beyond mammalian biology should check out Chuck Tingle, the author of “I’m Gay for My Living Billionaire Jet Plane.”)

That audience will want more scenes than they get of Skarsgård swooping Colman to bed as the sound design adds an array of naughty creaks. “Wicker’s” louder point is societal. It’s “Frankenstein” if the rabble wanted to be (or be with) the monster — and a parable with plenty to say about today.

Representing petty humanity are several couples each named for the husbands’ jobs: the Tailors, the Bakers, the Innkeepers and the Bottlewashers. Elizabeth Debicki plays the queen bee, a bully born into high status as the doctor’s daughter, whose icy lavender gowns contrast crisply against Colman’s hand-knitted rags. Clever and ambitious, Debicki’s snob is itching for an excuse to leave this hamlet for the city. Being stuck, she instead bends the residents to her will. As Hedda Gabler warned, bored women are time bombs whose agitation puts everyone at risk.

“Wicker” is brisk, visual storytelling boosted by Sofi Marshall’s clever editing and Lol Crawley’s assured camerawork. Smartly, Fischer and Wilson build out this community with its own strange customs, big and small, each executed without explanation, question or complaint. At weddings, brides proudly wear a heavy matrimonial collar, an accessory more often associated with the stockade, while grooms strap carrots to their crotches and waggle them at goats.

Nearly all of their rituals are rooted in female submission, expressed without words to keep the film from feeling like a lecture. The script prefers to show horrors without dwelling on them. When the perverted old butcher claims a beautiful young bride, the poor girl barely makes a peep. But we glimpse despair under her smile — and one of the next times we see her, she’s chained up in a cellar.

Our three Sundance correspondents, Amy Nicholson, Mark Olsen and Joshua Rothkopf, pick their favorites from this year’s lineup and reflect on memories of Park City.

Our hearts go out to the wives, even when their actions don’t deserve it. “Wicker” also spends a fair amount of time with the husbands to show how much pressure they feel at home too, light as their burdens often seem. The innkeeper is annoyed that his wife wants more conversation. The newlywed bottlewasher is alarmed that his bride wants him to fix the roof. The wicker man’s superiority makes all the villagers feel ashamed. “Wicker” is empathetic to their insecurities, yet it has no tolerance for how moral hypocrites ignore the opportunity for self-improvement in favor of scorching everything to the ground.

An orphan and an outcast, Colman’s fisherwoman wasn’t raised to fit in. Note how she dances, ignoring the regimented steps to nuzzle sweetly into her husband’s rib cage. On her character’s wedding day, Colman traipses into the church like it’s a prank, then nervously straightens up as she realizes this ceremony is serious. The most touching beats in the film are simply scenes of a woman who’s always fended for herself experiencing kindness. Love changes her, not all at once but gradually, like a rescue dog. And it makes her feel vulnerable too.

Colman nimbly balances right on the line between humor and pathos. Still, even she can’t leap over the fact that the second half of the film skips several emotional beats. The fisherwoman’s love story is “Wicker’s” pulse, but the movie is really about the town and it sidelines her character to skewer the place. Frustratingly, by the end, we’re barely inside her head or heart at all.

Skarsgård’s wicker man is tall, blond and handsome on the outside, but hollow by design. He rarely shares what he’s thinking, although there’s one lovely image of him gazing at the sun through his woven hand, perhaps quietly contemplating his own existence. Lately, Skarsgård seems to be doing the same, gravitating toward roles that, at their core, interrogate the impact his looks have on other people. (See also the marvelous “Pillion,” in which his silent biker dominates a cowed lover.)

The special effects team has done terrific work re-creating his body down to the creature’s fringed lashes. More importantly, they’ve kept Skarsgård’s sad blue eyes and the actor puts more soul into them than the script gives his character. “I was made for you,” he says simply. The theater swoons.

Rated: R, for sexual content, language and brief full nudityRunning time: 1 hour, 45 minutesPlaying: Opens Friday, Oct. 23 in wide release

“Slow Horses,” Apple TV’s Best Show, Retains Its Pungent Relevance

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

What does a spy story smell like? The genre, oft beloved by its fans for its cerebral chill, tends to evoke the same stock scents: the burnt rubber of a car chase, the lingering gun smoke after a firefight. “Slow Horses,” Apple TV’s darkly comic espionage drama about a division of MI5 where the screwups and sluggards get sent, takes care to develop a scent profile like few shows ever do; its base notes include old socks, unrefrigerated takeout, and farts. The source of all these stenches is Jackson Lamb (Gary Oldman), a stringy-haired Cold War veteran who oversees the agency’s castoffs and wears his hygienic failure as a badge of pride; his foulness is both a middle finger to the service’s establishment and a useful costume that lulls his adversaries into underestimating him. Sure, everyone wants to be the handsome Martini sipper with the snazzy doodads and the gleaming douche-mobile, but it’s probably easier to get spycraft done as an aging shuffler with an unkempt beard and a rotation of ill-fitting shirts—the kind of guy no one takes a second look at.

Since its première, in 2022, “Slow Horses,” which just aired its sixth-season finale and will return for at least two more seasons, has become something not unlike Lamb: a treasure to those in the know, and a nonentity to outsiders. The show boasts nearly two dozen Emmy nominations, and a few wins, but has never reached the breakout status of fellow Apple TV series like “Ted Lasso” or “Severance,” despite being, in my estimation, the streaming site’s best offering by a mile. While most dramas this long in the tooth trend toward decline, the series’ many innovations, in a frequently stodgy genre, continue to pay off. The show has felt only more relevant in its past two seasons, as it tackles head on the larger question of what espionage means in a post-imperial Britain. The enemies that Lamb’s team is battling are often revealed not to be foreign agents or domestic terrorists, as they initially appear, but venal bureaucrats within the government who incite political chaos for the sake of personal gain. As is currently the case in the U.S., the debasement of the country is being led by the state itself.

But “Slow Horses”—closely adapted from Mick Herron’s long-running book series—is, foremost, a fast-paced, immodestly budgeted spy romp, with Lamb’s brigade of pariahs, known within MI5 as Slough House, looking into one intrigue each season. (If it didn’t smell bad enough inside, Slough House’s unassuming office, located three miles from the agency’s starkly sleek headquarters, in central London, can only be entered by passing an overfilled dumpster.) The filth around Lamb is visceral, but deceptive: his fiefdom, whose independence he jealously guards, is probably the branch of MI5 least touched by the agency’s general lawlessness and institutional rot, which abides by the careerist ethos of “Cover your arse.” Lamb, who was once floated for the service’s top job, treats his current position as a kind of penance for past sins, both for himself and for his underlings. His lacerating wit spares no member of his staff—a mix of true incompetents, off-putting savants, ticking time bombs, and onetime offenders prematurely consigned to professional purgatory. The hope for rehabilitation spurs on the show’s protagonist, River Cartwright (Jack Lowden), to reckless heroics. The grandson of a legendary spook (Jonathan Pryce), River, whose early antics led to the simulated deaths of more than a hundred civilians in a training exercise gone sideways, bristles at being “sat around like a spare part” at Slough House. After a lifetime of being fed stories about British espionage’s glory days by his grandfather, River can’t help wanting to rejoin the agency proper, despite enjoying the camaraderie and protection that he never found within MI5’s standard ranks.

Like “Widow’s Bay” and “Better Call Saul,” “Slow Horses” is a showcase for character actors, with a greater focus on character flaws and relationships than on conventional likability. Lamb’s tussles with his frosty boss, Diana Taverner (Kristin Scott Thomas), who spends much of the series as MI5’s aggrieved second-in-command, amount to an eternal game between two chessmasters, and their scenes to a master class by a pair of acting powerhouses. To be fair, Diana is a far worse player than Lamb—in the first season, one of the moles she has embedded in Slough House inadvertently kills another—but she has a seemingly infinite number of pawns at her disposal. Lamb, for his part, counts among his dented pieces a compulsive gambler (Kadiff Kirwan) who places bets on his phone mid-mission, a recovering alcoholic (Saskia Reeves) whose sobriety Lamb regularly belittles, and a raw-egg-drinking hacker (Christopher Chung), who may be saved from inceldom only by a honey trap set up by Libyan militants. Chung’s Roddy Ho is so obnoxious—he inanely boasts of possessing the “finest mind and finest body” at MI5—that he somehow comes back around to being charming, thanks, largely, to the actor himself, who has perfected the tightrope act of conveying the character’s comical delusions and the earnestness with which he believes them. The ensemble is so consistently stellar, in fact, that watching the show often feels like witnessing the next generation of U.K.-based scene stealers just before their breakouts.

But “Slow Horses” isn’t precious with its cast, either; it derives much of its stakes from its willingness to kill off, or otherwise dispense with, at least one major character every season. The show replaces each one with a fresh fuckup, who rarely has trouble adopting Slough House’s culture of picking at one another’s wounds until everyone’s a little too exposed for comfort. It’s to the credit of the original head writer, Will Smith (who left after the fifth season), that these arrivals introduce new dynamics to the office without unbalancing the series. But the deaths and departures do add up. Over the years, the series has seen River’s mounting pessimism curdle into a hardness toward his own teammates, and one formerly committed agent (Rosalind Eleazar), whose career-ruining mistake was to lose a tail, succumbs to opportunism when she finally realizes that there’s no meritocratic escape from Slough House. Beneath the spy plots, then, is a fond portrait of a group of people held in perpetual disgrace for their shortcomings, and thus forced to find individual paths to redemption.

The series has proved even more compelling as an expression of national humiliation. Herron, who put out the first of the “Slough House” novels in 2010 and was subsequently dropped by his publisher for poor sales, has attributed his books’ late-coming success to Brexit, which became a touchstone of the fecklessness of the governing class. (“The country’s misfortune was my good luck,” he told the Guardian.) In the past two years, the show has darkened alongside the U.K.’s political mood. The premise of the first season—the apparent kidnapping of a young Muslim man by nativist extremists, which turns out to be a false-flag operation orchestrated by Diana to stage an easy publicity win for the agency—is practically quaint compared with the swampy backdrop of the sixth, in which Westminster élites fend off the influence of Russian money in London with one hand while grasping for a payout with the other.

The show hasn’t lost its escapist thrills to its gloomier mood; the latest season features a pair of warmly grounded assassins on a mission to root out every “slow horse,” as well as the elaborate destruction of the kind of beach house that would otherwise be the setting for a Nicole Kidman murder mystery. But there’s as much suspense in the clipped exchanges within the MI5 offices, where espionage has become just another source of plunder and political favors. The show’s deepened cynicism—reflected in its desaturated palette, which renders some scenes practically monochromatic and bathes others in a sickly green tinge—has given the series a meaningful renewal, as Lamb’s world comes to feel less like fiction and more like a mirror. In an increasingly complicated world, those entrusted with power seem less able—or willing—than ever to take on the threats that loom. Perhaps no contemporary series better captures the current moment’s despair over this decay from within, yet “Slow Horses” is just as quick to remind us that there are still ways to fight back from the bottom. ♦

In 1871, this nightmarish ‘Creeping Baby Doll’ was all the rage

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Published Oct 21, 2026 1:13 PM EDT

Already looking for children’s toys for the holidays? Are you also a big Halloween fan? Well, do we have a  gift for you! Allow us to introduce the present no child could ever love (unless maybe they were possessed by a demon or a big fan of The Nightmare Before Christmas): The Creeping Baby Doll!

This nightmarish 1871 creation is everything you don’t want in a children’s toy. It’s noisy. It moves—seemingly propelled by painted plaster baby arms and legs. And it’s way creepy.

The mechanical baby doll has two legs, two arms, and a head all made of painted plaster (which is undoubtedly a bit chipped after more than 150 years). These plaster bits are really the only thing that actually look somewhat like a baby. The rest of the doll’s body is made up of brass clockwork. Some sort of dress or outfit would’ve likely covered its brass body back in the day.

Both the arms and legs are attached to gears that make the plaster limbs move so it looks like the baby doll is crawling (hence, the name—though we would argue there’s perhaps a reason they went with “creeping” instead of “crawling”). The entirety of the toy moves using two toothed wheels hidden under the baby’s body. A flat piece of wood covers the baby’s back to hide the mechanical instruments that make up the baby’s insides.

New York City-based inventor George Pemberton Clarke developed the horrific toy as an improvement to his colleague’s own creeping baby doll. That’s right, there were two! His associate, inventor Robert J. Clay, had filed the patent for his own creeping baby doll on March 14, 1871. Clarke later filed his patent on August 29th of that same year. They really thought this toy would be a hit apparently.

To be fair, there’s a long history of inventors trying to mimic human movement in machines. As far back as ancient Greece, there are stories of human mechanical models dumbfounding awestruck audiences. In the ninth century, Persian alchemist Jabir ibn Hayyan created moving statues. In the 10th century, Chinese engineer Yan Shi (nicknamed the Artificer) created life-size, human-like, moving figurines to the delight of King Mu of Zhou.

Mechanical automata reached their heyday during the Enlightenment and into the Victorian age. Automated sailing ships decorated tables, mechanical birds sang from gilded boxes, and silver swans floated down peaceful streams.

But even when you take into account the long history of automata, it is hard to look at Clarke’s Creeping Baby Doll and not think it’s a horror movie prop. In fact, it kind of was.

According to a Boing Boing, the antique toy actually inspired a very obscure 1972 horror film called Miriam: The Wind-Up Killer Doll. The film follows a group of friends who find a vintage crawling baby doll at a garage sale. But, in true horror movie fashion, an evil force possesses the baby doll, and the demonic doll starts killing off the friends one by one. (We didn’t say it was an original scary movie.)

That said, maybe we don’t actually recommend getting your favorite child Clarke’s Creeping Baby Doll. That decision may come back to haunt you.

After 'Bojack Horseman' came her midlife reset: polyamory fails, panic and pure joy

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

I Can't Stop Thinking About Horses and Sex

By Lisa HanawaltDrawn and Quarterly: 250 pages, $25If you buy books linked on our site, The Times may earn a commission from Bookshop.org, whose fees support independent bookstores.

While growing up in Palo Alto, it was not uncommon for animator Lisa Hanawalt to pretend she was a horse. This game of make-believe was a way for Hanawalt to escape her “burdensome and awkward” body, as she writes in her candid new illustrated memoir, “I Can’t Stop Thinking About Horses and Sex.” “But when I was a horse, I was powerful, graceful, and gorgeous.”

Although Hanawalt might be an unapologetic horse girl, she really hates a horse pun. So when it came time to choose a title for the book, she decided to keep it literal. “This is just the most true version of what this book actually is,” Hanawalt says about the utterly confessional nature of her book’s title, which groups two seemingly unrelated subjects that her mind wanders to when she’s bored. “They’re the two most mentally engaging things I can imagine,” she writes as a self-described “anxious lil freak.”

Sitting in her Los Angeles home, the designer of such adult animated touchstones as Netflix’s “BoJack Horseman,” “Long Story Short,” and her personal creation, “Tuca & Bertie,” is drinking from a mug of tea as we chat over Zoom. When I ask what people’s responses have been so far to her book’s cheeky title, she says, “They just laugh. A lot of them are just like, ‘Well, I have to read that.’”

In many ways, “I Can’t Stop” represents a culmination of Hanawalt’s shtick for truth-telling via colorful anthropomorphized characters. The 43-year-old’s sketches are famously loose, bright and unrefined. Topics like weird body stuff, sex and horniness are never off the table. A typical drawing might feature an animal-human hybrid (say, a bird head on a human body) doing something utterly mundane while Hanawalt’s inner monologue appears in thought bubbles to reveal an intensely personal thought most people would never say out loud, let alone publish in a book.

“They all have personal things in them, some more overtly than others,” Hanawalt says about her particular oeuvre, which includes 2013’s “My Dirty Dumb Eyes,” 2016’s “Hot Dog Taste Test,” 2018’s “Coyote Doggirl” and 2020’s archival collection “I Want You,” as well as countless contributions over the years to publications like the New York Times, McSweeney’s, Vanity Fair and the now-defunct women’s humor blog the Hairpin.

“I think [‘I Can’t Stop Thinking About Horses and Sex’] fits in with ‘My Dirty Dumb Eyes’ and ‘Hot Dog Taste Test’ in that it’s another kind of mishmash book, and it all goes together in my mind,” Hanawalt adds of her nonlinear storytelling, which in this book intersperses memories of couples therapy and egg-freezing with drawings of a sloth dancing with a bear. Or a deep dive on the filmography of Kevin Costner.

Hanawalt has never shied away from getting personal, but there’s no question that “I Can’t Stop” takes her disarming and revelatory style to a new level. “I think it just needed that extra layer of me going through a midlife crisis and breaking up with my partner and going through all this weird stuff. It’s a weird combo with the horse stuff because my love of horses is so pure.”

If Hanawalt ever felt embarrassment at being a horse girl (and she certainly has — as she got older, Hanawalt eased off the horse games out of fear of being bullied), that shame has since evaporated. About eight years ago, as she faced a combination of career burnout and gut-health issues, Hanawalt started taking riding lessons in an attempt at self-care. “There’s something about riding that clears my head and eases my physical pain, for a moment,” she writes in a comic titled “How I Got a Horse.” When her then-partner (comedian and podcaster Adam Conover) noted how relaxed Hanawalt seemed when she’d return home from riding, he urged her to consider buying a horse of her own.

Creator Raphael Bob-Waksberg and supervising producer Lisa Hanawalt discuss how the show time-jumps between moments from the 1950s to 2020s, including the COVID-19 pandemic.

So Hanawalt reactivated her dormant Facebook account and began scrolling through local horse sale groups. That’s where she discovered Juniper (“Juni” for short), a 2006 Norwegian Fjord Mare. “I bought her somewhat impulsively,” Hanawalt says. “It was a huge lifestyle shift. Having a horse is often very stressful because it’s like a pet you have to rent an apartment for. It’s expensive, and they injure themselves ... But then, being with her, I’m present in a way that I never am in the rest of my life. It just feels nice to care for another creature, human or animal.”

Spending time with Juni and a cast of new friends at the stables was physically and emotionally therapeutic for Hanawalt, particularly as new issues began to sneak up on her. For instance, she began to wonder if she still wanted to remain child-free. Simultaneously, her long-term relationship of 14 years was beginning to come apart. She describes in the book how she and Conover made a Hail Mary attempt at ethical nonmonogamy — an experience she’s glad to have had, even if Hanawalt jokes today about how she’s “a true polyamory fail.” She adds, “I don’t think it’s for me at all, and I never did. But I was willing to give it a go [as a] last-ditch effort to save something I really cared about. I think that’s the case for a lot of people.”

Hanawalt admits how anxious she was to talk about her breakup in the book — so much so that she considered avoiding it altogether. “I was dancing around it, and then I was like, ‘I don’t need to explain every little part of it,’” she says. Hanawalt even solicited creative feedback from Conover, who responded by playing her bits of a new stand-up special, which showcased his perspective of their breakup (that special will be the forthcoming “Big Divorce Energy,” out Oct. 23 on YouTube).

“It’s really nice that we still have that kind of relationship,” Hanawalt says of her ongoing connection and friendship with Conover. “Because I really value his opinion and input. He’s just a really smart, creative person, and that was always a strong point in our relationship. I also just wanted him to be OK with how I was representing us. I’m still very protective of that.”

The latter half of “I Can’t Stop” finds Hanawalt processing the end of her relationship, dipping a toe back into the dating world, and exploring fertility options such as egg-freezing. Despite its heavy subject matter — one especially tough passage looks back at a sexual assault Hanawalt experienced when she was an adolescent — “I Can’t Stop” is packed with absurdist doodles that keep the mood light without seeming dismissive. For example, as she describes how Juni behaves when she’s in heat, Hanawalt considers her own overwhelming “livestock mode,” which once included a hyper-fixation on Kevin Costner. This is all laid out in a section titled “Costner Watch,” where Hanawalt decides to consume every film the actor ever made.

“Yeah, I was losing my mind,” Hanawalt laughs at “Costner Watch.” “I don’t know. It was a cope, in hindsight.”

As she prepares to celebrate the release of “I Can’t Stop” with a six-date book tour, Hanawalt can’t say for sure if she’s answered any existential midlife questions. She jokes that her eggs are “still on ice.” She’s working on several new animation projects, though isn’t sure at this point which, if any, will move forward. (It probably also doesn’t help that the animation industry is currently experiencing a severe downturn, with record-high unemployment among even the most seasoned creators. “It’s a weird time,” she says.) To round things out, Hanawalt says she’s “selling hats” and even working with a perfume maker to eventually release a “horsey-themed” fragrance.

When the Nickelodeon animated series premiered Oct. 7, 1996, its offbeat, lyrical and sometimes painfully honest portrayal of childhood in the city set the show apart.

Hanawalt also has no clue what being a self-proclaimed horse girl even means in the year 2026. The other horse people she hangs out with at the barn, where Juni lives, come from “every possible income level and political belief, and they’re all kind of shoved together,” Hanawalt tells me. “It’s kind of like ‘Lord of the Flies.’

“It’s just so fun to have a community with people and be able to trust them,” Hanawalt says of the friendships she’s made through caring for Juni. “We help each other. Just to be able to go out on the trail and talk about blow jobs or whatever with women I truly connect to … They’re some of the coolest people I’ve ever met in my whole life.”

All that is to say, Hanawalt has grown more comfortable in not quite knowing what’s next. “You’re never fully happy or fulfilled,” she says. “And that’s part of being alive.”

Catch Lisa Hanawalt live Oct. 22 at 7 p.m. at Skylight Books in conversation with Priyanka Mattoo. 1818 N. Vermont Ave., Los Angeles.

Brodsky is an L.A. culture and music writer. Her biography of Stevie Nicks, “Lessons & Lace,” published by Penguin Random House, is out now.

Inside the "To Catch a Predator" election's ad wars

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Republicans and Democrats are flooding the midterm airwaves with explosive TV ads accusing their opponents of protecting pedophiles and child sexual abusers.

Why it matters: The advertising blitz underscores the lingering power of the Jeffrey Epstein scandal, which has fueled distrust of powerful figures and pushed allegations of child sexual abuse to the forefront of national politics.

  • The controversy over the Trump administration's handling of the Epstein files — including allegations that officials sought to protect powerful figures by not releasing all of the files — has kept the issue in the headlines.
  • The midterm ads that have followed have been bipartisan and are playing out in state and federal races.

By the numbers: Since Sept. 1, candidates and outside groups have aired at least $70 million in TV ads mentioning pedophilia, Epstein and allegations related to child sexual abuse, according to AdImpact.

  • Of the 118 ads identified, 63 were sponsored by Democrats, 52 by Republicans and three by independents.

Driving the news: The issue has become a flashpoint in the competitive Senate race in Kansas, where Democrats unveiled three ads Tuesday attacking Republican Sen. Roger Marshall over his initial vote against a measure to force the Justice Department to release additional Epstein files.

  • Meanwhile, the conservative Club for Growth has been airing an ad accusing pastor Adam Hamilton, Marshall's Democratic opponent, of mishandling sexual abuse allegations involving a church camp volunteer.
  • The allegations date to 2005, when church leaders initially investigated accusations involving boys at the camp rather than immediately contacting police. Hamilton has released a commercial in which he denies a cover-up.
  • The Club for Growth's ad closely resembles promotional materials for the new movie "Primetime," which is inspired by the former NBC TV series "To Catch a Predator."

In Texas, Democrats backing Senate candidate James Talarico have spent more than $8 million on ads attacking his opponent, state Attorney General Ken Paxton (R), over his office's initial offer of a plea deal calling for just one day in jail for a man accused of sexually abusing a child.

  • Paxton says he agreed to the deal to spare a child abuse victim from having to testify in court a second time.

In North Carolina, meanwhile, Democrats have aired four ads accusing Republican Senate candidate Michael Whatley of appointing a convicted sex offender to a state GOP committee. Whatley has denied hiring the man.

  • "It's an issue that goes to character and judgment," said John Anzalone, a veteran Democratic pollster who's advising Whatley's Democratic opponent, former Gov. Roy Cooper.

Behind the scenes: Private GOP polling in Kansas, provided to Axios, illustrates why strategists believe the attacks can resonate.

  • After hearing allegations about Hamilton's handling of the church camp incident, 69% of Kansas voters surveyed said they were less likely to support him, strategists found.

State of play: Republicans' embrace of the attacks marks a striking turn for a party that spent months on the defensive over the Trump administration's resistance to releasing additional Epstein files.

  • Some GOP strategists see the attacks as a way to put Democrats on defense as Republicans struggle to sell voters on Trump's agenda.
  • Democrats have spent much of the campaign hammering Republicans over high prices. But with voters' views on the economy largely set, attack ads are increasingly focused on issues of character and judgment.

What they're saying: "Epstein reinforced the suspicion that powerful people protect each other and operate under different rules," Republican pollster Chris Wilson told Axios.

  • "Allegations involving children are especially visceral, and [the collapse of trust in institutions] gives them added resonance."

“Fatherland” Cleans Up the Rough Edges of History

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Paweł Pawlikowski’s “Fatherland” offers an amiable eighty-two minutes of condensed history, centered on the septuagenarian novelist Thomas Mann’s trip, in the summer of 1949, to Germany—West and East—in order to receive each country’s Goethe Prize on the occasion of that great predecessor’s bicentenary. If Pawlikowski’s aim was to portray Mann (played by Hanns Zischler) as a terminal bore and a pompous ass, he succeeded brilliantly, even if he failed to suggest the reasons for his own animosity. Mann, who has been living in Los Angeles since 1941, is accompanied on the journey by his daughter Erika (Sandra Hüller), who serves as his assistant, even editing his speeches. And he does gives speeches. He gives an impromptu one at a press conference at their first stop, Goethe’s native Frankfurt; he gives a carefully crafted one at the formal prize ceremony there, and he gives another at the East German ceremony in Weimar, Goethe’s longtime home, overtly under Soviet dominion. What’s more, he gives speeches, terse but fusty, in private, to Erika.

For the most part, Mann struts around looking iconic, bearing an air of absurd vanity in his belief that, as an eminent anti-Nazi exile and a Nobel Prize winner who left Germany, in 1933, soon after Hitler came to power, his supra-ideological presence in both Germanies, at a time of Cold War tension, could indeed matter. But Mann most of all strikes an air of formality even in private, even when, in Frankfurt, he gets the grim news of the death of his son Klaus (August Diehl)—Erika’s brother and confidant, who is also a noted author—from a drug overdose. (His death was ruled a suicide.) Most pompous of all are the silences; the most conspicuous of them hollows out one of the major early scenes, when father and daughter, newly arrived in West Germany and travelling by car to Frankfurt, pass through streets of destruction, a country ravaged by war—and remain silent. Yes, awestruck and horrified, no doubt; still, I wondered throughout why silence served Pawlikowski as an optimal mode of expression.

Throughout my viewing, something felt off—in style and in substance—and the first concrete diagnosis came through in my colleague Anthony Lane’s recent New Yorker Profile of Pawlikowski, which I had deliberately delayed reading until after seeing the movie. (History may have no spoilers, but drama does.) That in-depth portrait mentions a fundamental change the filmmaker wrought upon Mann’s real-life 1949 journey: the author was, in fact, accompanied by his wife, Katia, who, in “Fatherland,” is said to be too weak to travel. The character of Erika, a writer and intellectual with her own range of uneasy connections to Germany—she’ll encounter an ex-husband and an ex-lover there—is a screenwriter’s convenience, a walking plot-production machine. Behind closed doors, however, the switch from a spouse to a child, from Mann the husband to Mann the father, is the switch from the intimate to the casual, from uninhibited speech to guardedness.

It took no more than minutes of curiosity to find that the fulcrum of “Fatherland,” the death of Klaus —the first character seen in the movie—occurred not during the Germany trip but some months before it. As for the trip itself, it was far more tumultuous, far more infuriating to Mann, and far more unflattering toward West Germany than the movie lets on. Upon his return to the United States, Mann wrote, for the Times, that, in West Germany, “they live by the slogan: ‘Everything was better under Hitler!’ By virtue of the experiences they claim to have had, they declare triumphantly that democracy has been tried and found wanting. Democracy to them means the Occupation Powers and all who ‘collaborate’ with them.” This isn’t the elegant stuffiness of “Fatherland”; it conjures a Fassbinder-esque film—outspoken, insolent, crude, loud, and brazen. Mann may not have shared his testimony, his vision, his voice with Erika during the tour, but he might have with Katia.

To share that voice in “Fatherland,” Pawlikowski would have to imagine it. But his economy of expression also suggests that the film, which is never at risk of spilling out beyond the confines of its scripted pointedness, depends on an economy of imagination. Its contrivances are less like conveniences or abridgments than like deceptions: the facts and the tone are in service to a style that itself has little room for variety or range. “Fatherland” is a muted, muffled quiet-box of understatement, twisting history less for dramatic effect than for affectlessness. Pawlikowski has developed a felt-lined aesthetic—of black-and-white images, in the traditional nearly square screen dimensions, that leave space around the characters—that bespeaks observational detachment and comes off as culture personified. This forced reserve and mannered dignity create a hermetic atmosphere of refinement (from behind the camera) and of elimination (in front of it). It is a cinema of prefabricated meaning and predetermined moods, in which images limit the very kinds of events that can be depicted.

Here, Pawlikowski further rarefies the style that he developed in “Ida” (2013) and advanced in “Cold War” (2018), but not to advantage. I found myself thinking of other filmmakers who rarefy: Christopher Munch, whose 1991 feature, “The Hours and Times,” about the 1963 Barcelona jaunt of John Lennon and Brian Epstein, has the decency to stick to these characters and not replace Epstein with, say, George Martin; or Hong Sangsoo, whose radical reduction of space and span in his new movie, “The Day She Returns,” creates great cinematic leeway for the uninhibited voice of its actress-protagonist; or Robert Bresson, whose severe restraint served artistically as both microscope and microphone, to experience infinitesimal gestures on a cosmic scale of moment and morality.

In “Fatherland,” such microscopy is reversed. Pawlikowski’s camera reduces the figures and voices, in order to fit them into his own rigidly molded spaces. The characters are petrified like specimens, expressing and embodying only what fits into the film’s design, with no potential for inconvenient loose ends for viewers to follow outside the frame’s borders. It’s a sieve too fine for anything as inconvenient as bodies or as coarse as discourse to get through—an aesthetic of purification, disincarnation, signification, as if the mere fact of keeping voices down and holding thoughts in were itself a mark of urbanity, conferring that cultural virtue upon a dignified audience. The movie sanitizes the past and removes its tang, its pungency, leaving a craving for contact with nature, with raw experience, with incongruities and accidents and anything but what fits, in lieu of such synthetic sophistication. ♦

Gifts for Cats (and Their Owners) That Are Worth Knocking Off the Table

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Cats: what would we do without them? To the cat agnostics, not to mention the cat haters, I say: don’t answer that! (Also, what’s wrong with you?) But to the cat lovers, my intended interlocutors here, I say: no need to answer, either. We both know. A catless world is a terrible thing to contemplate. No lap snuggles? No headbutts? No slow blinks? No raising of the rump to welcome a wealth of scritches? No occasional, inexplicable biting? We’d truly be lost without our fuzzy, innocent but knowing friends.

A catless world would also be devoid of a whole slew of objects and accoutrements, gadgets and doodads—some meant for cats and some for the humans obsessed with them—that turn a house into a home, or, in some cases, a living room into a giant obstacle course. There are two things I can’t resist: any item that might improve or enrich my two cats’ lives in some way (even if that perceived improvement is a pathetic fallacy that exists purely in my own mind); and any item that captures, for me, the half-sweet, half-tart character of the feline species. If there’s a special cat fanatic in your life (or a special cat!), why not treat them, this holiday season, to one of the below?

It’s never too early for the cat-loving parent to Stockholm syndrome their kids into becoming cat lovers, too, a process that might begin with stuffing the proverbial stocking with a nice cat-centric book. My daughter is now past the age of picture books, but one of her favorites, and mine, used to be “Rich Cat, Poor Cat” ($29), by the magnificent Bernard Waber (best known for his Lyle the Crocodile series), in which a scraggly street cat named Scat finds a home. Other picture books we never tired of were Jenni Desmond’s “Red Cat, Blue Cat” ($14), about two feline frenemies; “No Kiss for Mother” ($21), by Tomi Ungerer, in which a young kit refuses to play nice; and Judith Kerr’s Mog the Cat series ($36 for eight of the books). For the slightly older child, I’d suggest either Esther Averill’s fantastic “Jenny and the Cat Club” collection ($22), about the shy black cat Jenny Linsky and her community of cat friends in nineteen-forties Greenwich Village, or Ursula K. Le Guin’s “Catwings” series ($28), about a litter of flying cats. Finally, a book for adults that kids will also enjoy leafing through is “The Encyclopedia of Movie Cats” ($50), from A24.

The best gift for an actual cat is almost undoubtedly the gift of play. I love watching my cats go nuts for catnip, and these knitted cigarette-shaped catnip toys ($15 for three), for the bad-boy feline about town, are super cute. (For a healthier-looking playtime option, these felted vegetable toys, for $6, are also terrific.) Feather wands ($9) can help the rotund cat get her jumps in, but if she prefers a more brainteasing kind of exercise this tri-tier ball tower ($12) will keep her occupied. Finally, a clicker toy ($13) is my own cats’ personal favorite. Hours of fun following that little red dot . . . if only all of us were so easily amused.

Let’s face it, neither a cat tree nor a scratcher is likely to stop your cat from shedding on your furniture, or destroying it (and I have the totally clawed-down Design Within Reach sofa to prove it). Still, it’s nice to make them feel like they have their own little domain, and if this domain is aesthetically pleasing, so much the better. This rustic pearwood-and-sisal cat tree-cum-scratcher (£90) will make one’s cat look like Robinson Crusoe sleeping in a tree; this fun cherry-shaped cat tree ($136) is very Claes Oldenburg; and this mid-century-modern scratching chair ($48) will class up any joint. A plush window perch ($23) will allow a bored house cat a view of the outdoors. (I always think of this kind of contraption as a public service, since there’s nothing that makes my heart beat faster than spotting an indoor cat lounging on one while walking down the street.) And, perhaps most useful, this techy litter robot ($599) will keep one’s home odor-free.

I know, I know. Feline-themed clothing and paraphernalia can easily veer corny or “crazy cat lady,” but there’s some cool stuff out there that avoids these pitfalls. I, for one, always enjoy a good vintage Kliban shirt ($48). This nineteen-seventies Danish cat tapestry ($408) is giving Ingmar Bergman’s “Scenes from a Marriage” décor (in a good way), and drinking coffee out of this William Steig New Yorker-cover cat mug ($24) could turn a terrible morning into a good one. The cat bus from Miyazaki’s “My Neighbor Totoro” always gives me a warm, happy feeling, so falling asleep in a room illuminated by this night light ($57) seems very soothing. Another famous cartoon cat that I’m obsessed with is, of course, Garfield, and this vintage ceramic figurine ($35) takes me straight back to 1981.

There’s never enough support for the people and organizations that are helping to save cats from the mean streets of America’s cities. I live in Brooklyn, and I adopted my extremely soft black-and-white cat, Gully, from Bushwick Street Cats, a nonprofit doing fantastic work with stray cats in the area. (They also helped neuter my other cat, the grumpy but loving Gingy.) Two other great organizations near me are Flatbush Cats and Brooklyn Animal Action. I can’t imagine a nicer gift than making a donation in someone’s name to one of these charities, or the cat charity of your choice, wherever you live.

Believe it or not, I love dogs, too (though maybe not as passionately as I love cats), so I was delighted to take my editor’s note and add a few choice finds for our canine friends. Since my knowledge of dogs is much less intimate, however, I solicited the assistance of two friends. Susan Korn, who owns both an accessories brand called Susan Alexandra and a gentle senior chihuahua named Pigeon, makes fabulous hand-beaded leashes ($155) and collars ($98). Pigeon, meanwhile, recommends this dog shampoo ($98) and this human-grade dog food (a basic plan starts at roughly $1 per meal). My friend Claire Jarvis has recently found herself the owner of a large, Marmaduke-like setter named Frankie, who’s been enjoying this wand toy ($32) and this harness ($40). He’ll also “walk on water” for these freeze-dried elk treats ($30). ♦

Texas women on death row walk the road to ‘Redemption’ with a community of nuns

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

See more from the L.A. Times in Google Search. Set us as preferred

Redemption: Faith, Justice, and Sisterhood on Death Row

By Lawrence WrightKnopf: 272 pages; $35If you buy books linked on our site, The Times may earn a commission from Bookshop.org, whose fees support independent bookstores.

New Yorker staff writer Lawrence Wright’s latest nonfiction narrative, “Redemption,” begins with an intriguing, real-life premise: an unlikely series of encounters between seven female convicts on Texas’ death row and a convent of nuns.

A Catholic prison deacon, Ronnie Lastovica, orchestrated the meetings because he saw commonalities between the two groups. Both were leading unconventional, isolated lives and relied on faith for solace, even joy.

The results of these encounters were surprising. “They ministered to us,” the initially reluctant nuns told Lastovica. The benefits were mutual. Before the arrival of the Sisters of Mary Morning Star, the death row inmates had been plagued by petty infighting. The nuns modeled how to create a sustaining, supportive community.

The Pulitzer Prize-winning Wright (“The Looming Tower,” “Going Clear”) gained extraordinary access to the Gatesville, Texas, prison, its residents and the visiting nuns, based near Waco, for a 2025 New Yorker article. But in expanding that lengthy article into a book, he sometimes loses the thread, indulging in digressions that detract from his study of transformation and redemption.

The digressions include the author’s own struggles with religious faith, references to the siege of the Branch Davidians at Waco and the mass suicide of Jim Jones’ followers at Jonestown, and an exploration of the case of a Methodist minister accused of strangling his wife. Often we don’t know quite where Wright’s narrative is going, or why.

To illuminate the plight of the condemned women, Wright explores not just their gruesome crimes but their horrific backstories. Most were themselves victims of repeated violence and sexual abuse. They succumbed to (sometimes inherited) drug addiction and often lived in poverty. One, Brittany Holberg, supported herself and her child as a sex worker.

After such ordeals, the women seem lucky to be alive. Even with execution dates looming, their death row confinement offers a relatively peaceful oasis. Free from drugs and violence, and with treatment for mental illness, they discover spirituality and their creative capacities.

One important aspect of “Redemption” is Wright’s investigation of the women’s culpability and the inadequate lawyering they received. “I have lost faith,” he writes, “in the presumption that Texas, or any state, has a system of justice that is sufficiently reliable, fair, or just to assume the power to take a life.”

Too frequently, he finds, evidence of the trauma and abuse that contributed to the crimes never made it into the record. If it had, it might at the least have affected sentencing. Even more disturbing is the possibility that one or more of the women was wrongfully convicted.

Melissa Lucio presents the most troubling example. Her confession to murdering her 2-year-old daughter was coerced, and the district attorney who prosecuted her case was later convicted of bribery, extortion and other crimes. A 2020 documentary, Sabrina Van Tassel’s “The State of Texas vs. Melissa,” made the case for her innocence. The original trial judge agreed, several jurors recanted their guilty verdict, and Texas politicians rallied to her defense. But Lucio, after receiving a stay of execution, remains on death row.

Another narrative strand involves the unusual history of the Sisters of Mary Morning Star. The founders of this contemplative order, Wright discovers, split from the Community of St. John, in France, which had been dominated by priests and afflicted by widespread sexual abuse.

Those circumstances suggest an unexpected connection to the death row inmates. But the breakaway nuns always denied that they had been abuse victims. And when Wright profiles the sisters involved in the prison meetings, he finds women who had grown up in “safe and predictable” circumstances, “buffered by love.”

Wandering further afield, Wright recounts the case of Walker Railey, a Methodist minister accused of strangling his wife and leaving her in a vegetative state. Six years after the assault, Railey was tried and found not guilty. Wright had attended Railey’s church; he reported on the crime for Texas Monthly and interviewed Railey, whom he believed to be guilty. The verdict, Wright says, shattered his belief in both the judicial system and religion.

In a chapter titled “Jesus in the Dock,” Wright analogizes the legal forces marshaled against Jesus to the highly imperfect Texas criminal justice system. Jesus’ crucifixion, in Wright’s telling, becomes yet another instance of justice gone awry.

For all its narrative quirks, “Redemption” does function as what Wright calls “an appeal to conscience.” In its pages, he asks a series of penetrating questions: Does faith retain value even if the stories underlying it are human inventions? How can Christians, who both grieve and celebrate the sacrifice of Jesus, support a flawed justice system that inevitably executes innocents?

Wright also interrogates the boundary between severe mental illness and evil, and wonders whether, even in extreme cases, redemption might be possible. If these death row inmates are utterly changed — no longer dangerous, and hopeful of contributing to society — what purpose does it serve for the state to execute them? That urgent question is the book’s ultimate contribution to the fraught death-penalty debate.

Klein, a cultural reporter and critic in Philadelphia, has been a three-time finalist for the National Book Critics Circle’s Nona Balakian Citation for Excellence in Reviewing.

In dieser Jane Austen-Welt schimmelt es [premium]

📅 Oct 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Aus ihrer Neuverfilmung von »Sinn und Sinnlichkeit« hat die britische »Blue Jean«-Regisseurin Georgia Oakley den Glamour verbannt: Auf Norland Park schimmelt es, im Zentrum steht nicht die große Liebe, sondern die Beziehung zweier Schwestern.

Ihr Langfilmdebüt „Blue Jean“ wurde vor vier Jahren mit viel Aufmerksamkeit und zahlreichen Auszeichnungen bedacht. Fiel es Ihnen danach leicht zu entscheiden, in welche Richtung Sie der nächste Karriereschritt führen wird?

Georgia Oakley: Mir hat mal jemand gesagt, dass eine Karriere das ist, was passiert, während man Pläne für andere Filme macht. Da ist etwas dran. Schon vor den Dreharbeiten zu „Blue Jean“ hatte ich damit begonnen, auch an anderen Ideen zu arbeiten und die habe ich dann im Anschluss wieder aufgegriffen. Zusätzlich habe ich angefangen, neue Projekte zu entwickeln. Ich hatte eine relativ klare Vorstellung davon, welche Geschichte ich als Nächstes erzählen wollte. Doch dann landete das Angebot für „Sinn und Sinnlichkeit“ dank der Produktionsfirma in meinem Schoß, als eine Art Querschläger, der meine Pläne auf den Kopf gestellt hat. Ich hatte nicht unbedingt vorgehabt, mir einen Film dieser Größenordnung vorzunehmen. Aber das Drehbuch von Diana Reid gefiel mir einfach zu gut.

Für das Werk von Jane Austen hege ich schon lange eine große Faszination. Nicht nur für ihre Sprache und Themen, sondern auch dafür, wie ihre Arbeiten im Laufe der Zeit immer wieder neu betrachtet und umgesetzt wurden, ohne je ihren Reiz einzubüßen. Als jemand, der selbst englische Literatur studiert hat, war es durchaus ein Traum von mir, mir einmal eine ihrer Geschichten vorzunehmen. Und ich liebe die Geschichte der beiden Schwestern in „Sinn und Sinnlichkeit“. Deswegen konnte ich mir die Gelegenheit, für eine Neuverfilmung meinen Hut in den Ring zu werfen, einfach nicht entgehen lassen.

Wie erklären Sie sich die ungebrochene Begeisterung, mit der Austens Romane bis heute rezipiert werden?

Ich habe Austens Arbeit immer als unglaublich subversiv empfunden. Ihre Sprache und ihre Geschichten gelten ja als leichtfüßig, witzig und romantisch. Aber in den Themen, die sie verhandelt, ist sie immer auch enorm politisch und kritisiert die gesellschaftspolitischen Umstände ihrer Zeit. „Sinn und Sinnlichkeit“ ist ein hervorragendes Beispiel, es geht um den Platz der Frau in der Gesellschaft, um Erbschaftsgesetze und die Institution der Ehe.

Als ich das Drehbuch und dann auch den Roman noch einmal gelesen habe, habe ich eine Version dieser Geschichte erkannt, die ich so noch nicht gesehen hatte. Da gab es durchaus noch Dinge zu entdecken und Elemente, mit denen man die klassischen Erwartungen an einen Austen-Film unterwandern konnte. Bei vielen Adaptionen fühle ich mich – anders als in den Büchern selbst – irgendwie auf Armeslänge gehalten. So als würde mir die Menschlichkeit der Figuren fremd bleiben, weil sie von der Geschichte überdeckt werden. Oder auch von einer gewissen visuellen Umsetzung. Das wollte ich unbedingt anders machen.

Sie haben einmal davon gesprochen, dass sich eine gewisse Glamourisierung in britischen Historienfilmen breitgemacht hat, die Sie stört.

Die gibt es nicht nur in Filmen. Denken Sie an all die prächtigen, mit viel Geld und Sorgfalt gepflegten Landhäuser, die es in ganz England gibt und die jedes Jahr zahllose Besucherinnen und Besucher aus der ganzen Welt anlocken. Die sind eigentlich das mit Millionen von Pfund unterstützte Produkt einer Denkmal-Fetischisierung, die mit der Realität wenig zu tun hat. Weder der historischen noch der heutigen.

Auf unterschiedlichste Weise, aber ein gutes Beispiel ist der Landsitz zu Beginn der Geschichte, also Norland Park. Mir war es wichtig, ein Haus zu finden, das eben nicht einer großen Stiftung oder so gehört, sondern das wirklich noch von einer Familie bewohnt und betrieben wird. Dem man die damit einhergehenden finanziellen Nöte ansieht. In dem es hier und da schimmelt und die Teppiche seit Jahrzehnten nicht erneuert wurden. Um 1800 war es in solchen Anwesen kalt und dunkel, und mir war es wichtig, dass man das spürt. Für glattpolierten Glamour hatte ich in den Kulissen oder Kostümen meiner Version von „Sinn und Sinnlichkeit“ keinen Platz, denn ich habe nach etwas Wahrhaftigerem gesucht.

Sie haben bereits das Subversive an Austens Arbeit erwähnt. Sehen Sie als lesbische Filmemacherin auch eine Queerness darin?

Ich hatte kaum mit der Arbeit begonnen, als jemand zu mir sagte: „Du weißt schon, dass das Austens queerster Roman ist, oder?“ Das war durchaus nicht an mir vorbeigegangen, auch weil ich einmal einen tollen, literaturwissenschaftlichen Essay mit dem Titel „Jane Austen and the Masturbating Girl“ gelesen hatte. Da ging es unter anderem darum, dass Marianne Dashwood im Grunde so etwas wie die frühe Version einer queeren Figur ist. Alles, wofür sie steht und was sie in Sachen Emotionen und Leidenschaft ausdrückt, unterwandert das damals herrschende Frauenbild. Sie passt in keine der vorgesehenen Schubladen, ist viel weniger definierbar oder binär, als es Frauen damals eigentlich gestattet war. Da kann man schon – genau wie übrigens mit Blick auf Austens eigene Biografie, die nie geheiratet und stets mit ihrer Schwester zusammengelebt hat – von einer inhärenten Queerness im weiteren Sinne sprechen, finde ich. Den Status quo abzulehnen ist für mich jedenfalls queer, und all das schwingt in meiner Begeisterung mit. Selbst wenn es im Film eine untergeordnete Rolle spielt.

Ihr Fokus im Film liegt auf jeden Fall auch weniger auf den romantischen Liebesbeziehungen, ohne dass Sie natürlich darauf hätten verzichten können…

Stimmt, und ich glaube, das war das Erste, worauf ich bei der Drehbuchlektüre angesprungen bin. Im Zentrum stehen klar die beiden Schwestern, was meiner Lesart des Textes komplett entspricht. Die Beziehung von Elinor und Marianne ist es, die diesen Roman von anderen Austen-Geschichten abhebt. So wie Diana das in ihrem Skript umgesetzt hatte, folgt sie durchaus einem klassischen RomCom-Bogen. Nur dass die beiden Figuren, die sich ineinander verlieben, dann getrennt werden und schließlich wieder zusammenfinden, eben die Schwestern sind. Das fand ich aufregend. Die Männer sind durchaus mehr als schmückendes Beiwerk, aber das Herz der Geschichte und die komplexeste Paardynamik ist die von Elinor und Marianne. Weshalb den richtigen Schlusspunkt für den Film zu finden auch die größte Schwierigkeit war.

Georgia Oakley (38) wuchs in Oxfordshire auf. Sie studierte englische Literatur in Newcastle. Ihr Langfilmdebüt „Blue Jean“ (2022) gewann u. a. den Publikumspreis in Venedig. „Sinn und Sinnlichkeit“ ist ihr erster Film dieser Größenordnung.

Beethoven ließ Napoleon und Prometheus tanzen

📅 Oct 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Die Dritte Symphonie war ursprünglich Napoleon gewidmet. Doch führen uns einige Ballett-Schritte direkt in die Mythologie.

Beethoven selbst hat seine Dritte Symphonie, die „Eroica“, Napoleon Bonaparte gewidmet, diese Widmung aber – so will es die Legende – erzürnt wieder ausgekratzt, als er erfuhr, dass der Feldherr sich zum Kaiser gekrönt hatte. Spätere Forschungen haben ergeben, dass diese Symphonie viel eher mit dem antiken Mythos von Prometheus zu tun hat, der den Menschen das Feuer gebracht hat. Ob das Werk, in dessen Zentrum ein imposanter Trauermarsch steht, wirklich so „heroisch“ ist wie der Titel verheißt, wird immer wieder diskutiert. Auf diese Frage versuchen auch die bedeutendsten Interpreten Antworten zu geben. Jede Zeit hat ihren eigenen symphonischen Heldenmythos. In diesem Podcast beleuchtet Wilhelm Sinkovicz Beethovens Werk, das zuvor komponierte Ballett „Die Geschöpfe des Prometheus“ und zwei außergewöhnliche Aufnahmen der Symphonie.

"Presse"-Musikkritiker Wilhelm Sinkovicz präsentiert Lieblingsaufnahmen aus seiner SINKOTHEK und lädt uns ein, mit ihm einzudringen in die Geheimnisse von Mozart, Bach, Beethoven und anderen. Ein Podcast für Kenner und Neulinge.

Jeden zweiten Samstag auf der Webseite der "Presse" und überall, wo es Podcasts gibt.

Alternierend mit dem „Musiksalon“ erscheint der Podcast „Klassik für Taktlose“, in dem Katrin Nussmayr und Wilhelm Sinkovicz gemeinsam die Welt der klassischen Musik ergründen: für musikalische Einsteiger und Klassik-Freunde, die’s ein wenig genauer wissen wollen.

Abby Martin on the Climate Cost of US Empire

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The filmmaker discusses military pollution, her reporting in Hawai’i, and why the climate and antiwar movements belong together.

What would climate politics look like if it put the military at the center of the conversation? In her documentary Earth’s Greatest Enemy, Abby Martin examines the environmental costs of US power, from its dependence on fossil fuels to the contamination left behind at military bases.

Those costs reach service members and their families as well as the communities around them. At Red Hill in Oʻahu, fuel releases in 2021 contaminated drinking water supplied by the Navy. At Camp Lejeune in North Carolina, another contamination exposed personnel to chemicals associated with increased risks of cancer.

Shilpi Chhotray: Why do you call the US military “Earth’s greatest enemy”?

Abby Martin: The film covers not just emissions but the maintenance of the military arsenal coupled with legacy contamination and the further militarization and expansion of the military, and what that actually does to our drinking water, to our air. Seeing the ecocidal destruction in Iran and Gaza, it becomes really clear what’s going on.

SC: You’ve said we have to look at the military as the institution keeping fossil fuel infrastructure in place through brutality and violence. Can you speak to that?

AM: The climate community has rightfully looked at fossil fuel infrastructure. But those companies have a military that works on their behalf. The US military is basically the enforcement arm of global capitalism. The military has always been a force for extractive industries to maintain its own force around the world.

National security has always explicitly been about fossil fuels and securing and maintaining access to those fossil fuels. When you look at all of the bases around the world, where are they? They’re where the main arteries of capital flow. It’s controlling the supply chain and the arteries of capital. That expanse necessitates the continuous growth and pillaging and plundering of all the resources on Earth, just to maintain itself.

SC: Most of this film was made under the Biden administration. What keeps this level of violence in place no matter who’s in power?

AM: The film is important because it’s a time capsule of the Biden administration. It exemplifies the status quo and what has been deemed normal by the ruling class, by the global elites. That’s really important and instructive because it has gotten so bad.

Trump has taken it to such a new level, but we have to go back and realize Trump is not an anomaly. Trump is an extension of the system that incubated this. He’s a natural consequence of what has been normalized and allowed. When we don’t hold criminals to account, when we let this impunity reign, coupled with the wanton environmental destruction, this is what we get.

SC: What do you wish people understood about the cost of war for the people sent to carry it out?

AM: I see it firsthand. Everyone in the military is a victim of this machine. They’re a victim of propaganda too. We are all being harmed by this. But what it’s doing to service members, it’s killing them too.

SC: If military dominance relies on fossil fuels, what does that mean for the possibility of a real energy transition?

AM: We can’t have an energy transition with a global military empire. It’s not going to be allowed. You see people in the film telling me that point-blank.

The problem is you can’t green a global military empire that essentially runs on oil. That is oil, oil, oil. You even heard major generals tell me it’s a pipe dream. We need oil.

SC: Let’s talk about Hawai’i. What happened at Red Hill in Oʻahu?

AM: [Jet fuel] seeped into the drinking water wells used by military families. These antiquated, rotting storage tanks sat right above Oʻahu’s main aquifer. The military decided to build storage tanks of oil right on top of it. Why?

The Red Hill disaster is one part of what the military has done to Hawai’i. A lot of Americans have no idea that Hawai’i was a sovereign country that had its own government up until the late 1800s.

SC: You got access to RIMPAC, the Rim of the Pacific naval exercise. What did you witness there?

AM: The scale and scope of these exercises are massive. They sink a ship. They love doing that. They will destroy a ship and let it sink to the bottom of the ocean. They do this as the grand finale, I guess, at the end of the exercises.

They use Hawai’i as not only a dumping ground but as a playground for themselves. They have a good time. They treat the island as expendable. They’re blowing stuff up, patting themselves on the back, pumping fists.

SC: How the hell are you getting into RIMPAC and these industry and military events? A quick Google search would show you’re Abby Martin, the anti-imperialist.

AM: There is a contradictory narrative throughout the film. On one hand, the military is this very secretive institution. They don’t grant you any access. But on the other hand, they’re dumb. They don’t Google you.

They don’t do their research. I’m applying to get into these spaces and I’m like, I’m Abigail Martin. They’re like, “Come on in, Sis.” I thought I was going to be beating down other journalists at RIMPAC because it’s such a huge event.

I lived in DC for several years. I know how it works. It’s access, access, access. You don’t want to step on the toes of not being able to get back into these spaces. So you play the game. I go into these spaces knowing that I will never go back. You have one shot to get it right.

I’m not special. I do the bare minimum of what a journalist should do. My question is, where’s everyone else?

SC: Where are the pressure points? What are some tangible things you want to see?

AM: There are incremental steps that can be taken now. There’s the big vision of what ultimately needs to happen to have a habitable planet that we can all survive on. And there are things that need to happen right now that are completely feasible and that are actually being proposed.

The military budget continues to grow and grow and grow. It needs to be completely cut in half right now.

There’s been so much movement with the encampments and the demand for accountability of what weapons systems and contractors are invested in universities.

Banding together, building big tents and broad coalitions and stopping these things in their tracks. That’s possible. There’s so much that we can do, but I think we have to decommission this global military empire and we have to force accountability mechanisms to make the remediation happen. We have to force the military to clean up their mess.

SC: I keep thinking about the 1960s and ’70s and the rise of anti-imperialism at the height of the Vietnam War. What will it take to see our society rise up in that way?

AM: We’re in the infancy stages of organizing and mass education. I feel like we’re getting back to that foundational understanding that capitalism is driving a lot of this. That’s why Gaza has been such a fundamental moment of awakening for so many people, understanding the mechanism and the structure of why these things are happening.

We have to be anti-imperialists. We have to be internationalists, and we have to have solidarity with all oppressed people in the Global South and understand how their oppression is our oppression. It’s all intrinsically tied together.

As an advocacy journalist, I create media to agitate. I try to use propaganda in a way that’s slapping you across the face with the truth, but not to paralyze you, to empower you. Education, action, community, building organizations that have the understanding of what we’re talking about, because we can’t make a dent in the environmental space if we don’t address the elephant in the room.

We’re not going to fight them militarily. We have to speak in the language that they speak: capital flow, withholding our labor, strikes, understanding how labor is essential. During Occupy Wall Street, that shut down the ports. It was the most beautiful day of my life because I saw the power of all of these people coming together and I understood how it could be stopped.

We can’t ever let this system prevent us from imagining something different, from realizing that we have the power and the agency to do something else. This movie is just one tool in the arsenal to give us a fighting chance.

Shilpi Chhotray is the host of A People’s Climate, a new podcast from Counterstream Media and The Nation. New episodes every Saturday, starting September 27.
