„Mal sehen, wie weit ich die Zither pushen kann“ [premium]

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Fabian Schumann spielt ein Instrument, das in Vergessenheit geraten ist: Zither – akustisch und elektronisch. Am Samstag (17.10.) ist er in Salzburg bei „Jazz & The City Sounds“ zu hören.

Die Liebe von Fabian Schumann zur Zither hat früh begonnen. Schon als Fünfjähriger fing er Feuer und nervte seine Eltern so lange, bis sie ihm erlaubten, statt wie üblich Gitarre, Flöte oder Klavier zu lernen, doch Unterricht in Zither zu nehmen. Aber selbst als die Eltern endlich zustimmten, musste er sich noch gedulden.

Sein Lehrer befand ihn als zu jung und begann erst mit dem Unterricht, als Fabian sechs Jahre alt war. „Man muss warten, bis die Fingerknochen fest genug sind“, sagt Schumann im Rückblick. Mittlerweile ist er 28 Jahre, hat an der Universität Mozarteum Volksmusik studiert und unterrichtet Musik an einer Mittelschule in Zell am See. Vor einigen Monaten hat er unter dem Titel „Strom“ sein erstes Album mit eigener Musik für akustische und E-Zither veröffentlicht. Wie das klingt, kann man am Samstag bei „Jazz & The City sounds“ in Salzburg hören.

Mit der Zither verbinden die meisten Menschen die berühmte Melodie aus dem Film „Der dritte Mann“ oder traditionelle Volksmusik. Bei Schumann ist das doppelt exotisch: Er kommt aus einer Familie, der zwar die musikalische Frühförderung ihrer Kinder ein Anliegen war, in der Musik aber sonst kaum eine Rolle spielte. Und er stammt aus Kassel – eine Region, in der die Zither ebenso wenig daheim ist wie die alpenländische Volksmusik. Dass er sich auf die Zither als Wunschinstrument kaprizierte, habe vor allem mit seinem Lehrer Axel Rüdiger zu tun gehabt, erzählt er. Dieser hat in der musikalischen Früherziehung einfache Kinder- und alte Volkslieder auf der Zither vorgespielt. „Er war so charismatisch, dass ich seinetwegen unbedingt Zither lernen wollte.“

Zehn Jahre unterrichtete Rüdiger ihn in Kassel, dann fuhr Schumann noch als Gymnasiast einmal im Monat nach München, um im Rahmen eines Jung-Studiums das Instrument weiter zu erlernen. Nach der Matura verschlug es ihn schließlich nach Salzburg, wo er an der Universität Mozarteum Volksmusik studierte. „Es ist eine eigene Welt, die ich da entdecken durfte und die mich unglaublich fasziniert“, erzählt Schumann.

Zu Beginn war er viel bei Stammtischen und Veranstaltungen unterwegs und tauchte tief in eine Szene ein, die ihm als gebürtigem Deutschen anfangs durchaus mit einiger Skepsis begegnete. Schumann ist voller Bewunderung für die Virtuosität, mit der junge und ältere Zitherspieler ihr Instrument beherrschen. „Ich musste mir mein Standing erst erarbeiten“, sagt er im Rückblick. Wenn er auf seinem Instrument spielte und zeigte, dass es ihm ernst mit der Zuwendung zur Volksmusik ist, wuchs auch der Respekt. Er ist begeistert, dass viele junge Menschen in Salzburg die Traditionen aufgreifen und die Volksmusik ins Heute tragen. „Die Szene ist jung, bunt und spannend“, sagt Schumann.

Seine musikalische Reise führte ihn von der traditionellen Volksmusik stark in den Jazz. Er tritt regelmäßig als Solokünstler und mit einem von ihm gegründeten Trio auf und sorgt dafür, dass die Zither in Salzburg mittlerweile einen gewissen Coolness-Faktor erreicht hat. Erst kürzlich hielt er im Salzburger Rockhouse einen Workshop für E-Zither und E-Hackbrett, der gut besucht war. Die vielfältigen Möglichkeiten der E-Zither auszuloten, macht dem Musiker Spaß. „Sie ist nicht nur lauter als die akustische Zither, sie ist für mich ein ganz anderes Instrument“, beschreibt Schumann seine Faszination. Inspiriert von E-Gitarren, ließ er sich bei einem Instrumentenbauer eine E-Zither bauen. „Damit kann man die ganze Rock- und Popliteratur auf der Zither umsetzen“, schwärmt er von den Möglichkeiten.

Seine Kompositionen entstehen meist abends, wenn er auf seinem Instrument Stimmungen nachspürt und sich meditativ in Klangwelten versenkt und improvisiert. Die Sachen, die ihm bei diesen musikalischen Expeditionen gelingen, schreibt er später auf und arbeitet weiter daran. Einige dieser Kompositionen finden sich auf „Strom“. Das Album sei „ein Stimmungsbarometer der letzten drei, vier Jahre“, erzählt Schumann. Er träumt von einer musikalischen Karriere – und einem Revival seines Instruments: „Mal sehen, wie weit ich die Zither pushen kann.“

Fabian Schumann (28) ist Musiker und Musikpädagoge. Der gebürtige Deutsche lebt und arbeitet in Salzburg, wo er Volksmusik und Zither studiert hat. Sein erstes Album „Strom“ holt die Zither aus ihrer traditionellen Nische in ein von Jazz und Improvisation inspiriertes Genre.

Doris Dörrie und "Frau Winkler": Sterbehilfe per Roboter?

📅 Oct 15, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Während die Welt über die Gefahren Künstlicher Intelligenz diskutiert, lotet die Filmemacherin Doris Dörrie aus, wie humanoide Roboter unseren Umgang mit Alter, Pflege und selbstbestimmtem Sterben verändern könnten.

Hamburg in naher Zukunft: Statt Paketboten bringen Roboter die Pakete, im Café fährt ein sprechender Servierwagen zwischen den Tischen umher, auf den Straßen begegnet man humanoiden Robotern, die Hunde ausführen; oder auch umgekehrt: Menschen, die einen Roboterhund spazieren führen. Ansonsten wirkt diese Welt erstaunlich normal.

In dieser Szenerie beginnt Doris Dörries neuer Film "Frau Winkler verlässt das Haus". Im Mittelpunkt steht eine ältere Dame, die nach einem Sturz nicht mehr alleine zurecht kommt. Doch ihre einzige Tochter kann die Pflege nicht dauerhaft übernehmen. Die Lösung scheint schnell gefunden: ein Pflegeroboter mit eingebautem EOL-Programm. EOL steht für "End of Life Mode", sprich: Der Roboter begleitet nicht nur durch den Alltag, sondern leistet unter bestimmten Voraussetzungen auch Sterbehilfe, nämlich wenn der Mensch dies selbst wünscht und medizinische Kriterien erfüllt sind.

Doris Dörrie greift in ihrem Film mit großer Leichtigkeit gleiche mehrere gesellschaftliche Schwergewichte auf: Es geht unter anderem um die Herausforderungen, vor denen alternde Gesellschaften wie beispielsweise Deutschland, Japan oder auch China stehen, es geht um das Thema selbstbestimmtes Sterben und eine Mutter-Tochter-Beziehung, die die Schuldfrage zwischen den Generationen stellt. Es sind Themen von universeller Relevanz, auch wenn die Handlung in Deutschland verankert ist.

"Ich schreibe das, was mich selbst bewegt und interessiert", sagt Doris Dörrie im Interview mit der DW. Dörrie zählt zu den bekanntesten deutschen Filmregisseurinnen der Gegenwart. Der Durchbruch gelang ihr 1985 mit der Komödie "Männer"; später machte sie unter anderem mit Filmen wie "Kirschblüten – Hanami" auch international auf sich aufmerksam.

Sterbehilfe gehört zu den komplexesten ethischen und gesellschaftlichen Fragen unserer Zeit. Entsprechend unterschiedlich fallen die Regelungen weltweit aus: Während einige Länder aktive Sterbehilfe unter bestimmten Voraussetzungen erlauben, ist in anderen jede Form der Beihilfe zum Sterben verboten und strafbar.

Doris Dörrie stellt mit ihrem Film die Frage: Was passiert, wenn eine KI beginnt auszurechnen, wie kostenintensiv die Pflege eines alten Menschen ist und ob sich diese Belastung für eine Gesellschaft noch "lohnt"? Der Grundgedanke an sich ist nicht neu, sagt Dörrie, und verweist auf die japanische Legende von Narayama, die auch im Film Erwähnung findet: Der Überlieferung zufolge war es die Aufgabe der ältesten Kinder, ihre betagten Eltern auf einen Berg zu bringen, um sie dort sterben zu lassen, weil die Nahrung nicht für alle ausreichte. "Und in der Diskussion befinden wir uns ja auch schon zum Teil: Können wir uns das überhaupt noch leisten, dass die Alten immer älter werden?"

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In Dörries Film darf der alte Mensch zwar ab einem bestimmten Gesundheitszustand selbst entscheiden, die Entscheidung aber dann auch nicht mehr zurücknehmen. Doch was genau heißt hier selbst entscheiden? "Das ist schon vertrackt", sagt Dörrie, denn die Frage sei ja, inwieweit man zu dieser Entscheidung auch gedrängt werde, "weil es dann nicht nur ökonomisch sinnvoller ist, sondern weil man auch für die Nächsten, also hier die Tochter, keine Belastung mehr ist?"

Der Film wirft diese Fragen teilweise indirekt und mit viel schwarzem Humor auf, etwa wenn die Pflegeberaterin von Frau Winkler das EOL-Feature als attraktive und vor allem unkomplizierte Zusatzoption anpreist, so dass man selbst gar nicht weiter tun müsse.

Doris Dörrie geht es nicht darum, die KI zu verteufeln. Ganz im Gegenteil. Im Film schafft es der Pflegeroboter, dem Frau Winkler den Namen "Pawlowitsch" gibt, dass die alte Dame wieder ein Stück Lebenslust gewinnt und tatsächlich das Haus verlässt - daher auch der Titel des Films.

"Wenn man durch Deutschland fährt, sieht man überall diese kleinen Einfamilienhäuschen, und in jedem zweiten Häuschen, wenn man sich die Statistik anschaut, lebt ein alter Mensch allein." Wenn KI hier helfen könne, warum nicht, sagt die Filmemacherin. Sie wolle genau diese Ambivalenz der Dinge untersuchen. "Die Dinge in Schwarz-Weiß einzuteilen, wäre sowieso nicht interessant und auch nicht richtig, glaube ich."

Sie selbst und das Filmteam hätten zudem beim Dreh erstaunt festgestellt, wie schnell man als Mensch eine Beziehung zu einem Roboter aufbauen könne. Sie erklärt sich das mit dem menschlichen Gehirn. "Wir halten alles, was sich bewegt und spricht, für ein Lebewesen. Wir können nicht anders." Und genau da müsse man hinschauen, so Dörrie, wenn man mit einer KI kommuniziere. "Wie sehr sind wir uns jedes Mal bewusst, dass wir mit einer Maschine reden und nicht mit einem Lebewesen? Das sind alles Dinge, die müssen wir jetzt erst lernen."

Schaut man sich die Entwicklung von KI an, zeigt sich, dass die Grenzen zwischen Mensch und Maschine immer mehr zu verschwimmen scheinen. Und genau deshalb ist es wichtig, darüber zu reden, so Doris Dörrie. Problematisch werde es ihrer Ansicht nach dort, "wo wir uns nicht mehr distanzieren und wo wir keine eigenen Gedanken mehr fassen". Als Menschen müssen wir uns die Frage stellen, so Dörrie, wer wir sind und was uns wirklich ausmacht. "Ich glaube, dass es einen großen Unterschied zwischen dem Roboter und uns gibt, und das ist das Spielen." Der Roboter könne ein Spiel vielleicht initiieren, "aber das Spielen an sich ist doch etwas zutiefst Menschliches, weil es erst mal sinn- und zweckfrei ist".

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Neben diesen philosophischen Fragen, was das Menschsein ausmache, stellt die Filmemacherin im DW-Gespräch aber auch ganz konkret die Forderung nach KI-Regulierung in den Raum. "Wir haben auch über Atomwaffen irgendwann international geredet. Wir haben über alle möglichen Dinge in der Vergangenheit nicht nur geredet, sondern es auch geschafft, Lösungen zu finden." Und das müsse nun dringend angegangen werden. "Und ich glaube, dass wir uns da auch sehr beeilen müssen."

Aria Vega will bring ‘Caribbean maximalism’ to L.A. — and soon, the Latin Grammys

📅 Oct 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

See more from the L.A. Times in Google Search. Set us as preferred

One word that could describe Aria Vega’s whirlwind 2026? Chévere.

The Colombian expression for “awesome” is also the name of the fast-rising star’s global smash hit. Released in January, the breezy tropical swing of “Chévere” — and the rest of her sun-kissed singles — embodies the spirit of Colombia’s Caribbean coast where she hails from.

Ahead of the Latin Grammys on Nov. 12, where Vega will be in the running for the best new artist award, Vega is taking what she calls her “Costeñita Core” pop movement on the road, with her first-ever U.S. tour.

“Looking back at all the dreams I’ve accomplished and made come true, it’s all blessings,” a wide-grinning Vega says over Zoom from Miami.

“I’ve been crying tears of joy with my family, my friends and my combo chévere, as I like to call my fandom. Every artist wishes and dreams of a Latin Grammy, and for me, just to be one of the nominees is like, ‘Yo, I’m a winner!’”

Vega was born Mariana Padilla Pérez in Barranquilla, where she lived the lifestyle of the costeños — or the people of Colombia’s Caribbean coastal region. She has been steadily chasing her dreams of superstardom since competing on “Idol Colombia” in 2014; but Vega finally started making waves last year when she integrated more costeño culture, rhythms and slang into her mellowed, Spanglish pop flow.

“Chévere” exploded in popularity after fellow Colombian singer Ryan Castro jumped on the remix in February. Since then, Vega has pushed “Costeñita Core” further into the mainstream with songs like the alluring “Cule Poco (Sirena_Besarico_Costeña),” and the sultry “Compa Coleto (Uy_Como),” offering unhurried takes on champeta and other Afro-Caribbean rhythms.

Vega just kicked off her U.S. Cariavales Tour, which includes a stop at the Roxy in Los Angeles on Oct. 13. Just a week before, she was handpicked by her pop foremother and icon from Barranquilla — Shakira — to perform as part of her 12-show residency in Madrid.

In an interview with The Times, Aria Vega opened up about her Latin Grammy nomination, representing costeños and getting a co-sign from Shakira.

How did the success of the song “Chévere” change your life?I didn’t expect to be on [so many] airplanes and flights. Right now, I’m starting to feel the weight of that. I’ve seen what it’s like to live on the road. The remix happened after Ryan [Castro] sent me a DM on Instagram saying, “‘Chévere’ is a banger!’” I was really excited to even get that message from him, because I’m a fan of his music. That was a really cool moment.

What can you tell us about your upcoming debut album? It’s Afro-Caribbean-inspired music. Right now, the album is really long. I have recorded more than 30 songs for it. I’m working on cutting it down to hopefully 15 songs that will touch your heart and soul ... I want to make people vibe with my songs, feel cool, and feel chévere. That’s my goal with this album [I call] “Costeñita Core.”

What does “Costeñita Core” mean to you?It’s an energy that you either vibe with or you don’t. It’s like our food, our slang and experimental fashion. Caribbean maximalism and shifting [mainstream] culture. Overall, it’s about making bold choices and being rebellious.

How do you feel about representing costeño culture through your music and visuals?It’s a big responsibility. I feel really happy and excited when I see comments that say things like, “It’s an honor for you to represent us, and we love what you’re doing.” I get emotional because I don’t always get that response … There’s also people that say, “I feel that I’m not represented by her in any way.” It’s a mixture of feelings. What can I say? I’m doing this with a lot of love for Barranquilla, Colombia and Colombia’s Caribbean coast. I want to be a voice of the Caribbean coast.

What did it mean for you when Shakira selected you to perform for her residency in Madrid?As soon as my team told me about this opportunity with Shakira, I was going crazy because she’s so inspiring. She’s also from Barranquilla. Her music has been a part of [my] life. It has been like a companion for me. Since I was like 5 years old, there’s always been a Shakira song that I was vibing with. I’m really excited even about the possibility of meeting her.

How do you feel about performing in L.A. for the first time on your U.S. tour?I’ve been to Los Angeles before in meetings with my agents and stuff like that, and it felt like a movie. It’s Hollywood vibes and I’ve never felt that energy in my life. It’s really special getting to perform all my songs that are not just in Spanish, but in Costeñol [the costeño dialect of Spanish] at the Roxy. I’m going to wear outfits with cultural designs and perform traditional dances that have been part of my life, ever since I was literally in my mom’s belly. Los Angeles is a coastal city too, so there’s something that connects us.

What are your thoughts about being nominated in the category of best new artist at this year’s Latin Grammys?Everyone says it’s a really important category, because you can’t be nominated twice. You can only be nominated once in your career. Hopefully I’ll go to Las Vegas for that Latin Grammy and I’ll take it over to la costa.

Edgar Barrera leads the nominations with a total of 10 nods, followed by Karol G, Rosalía, Ca7riel & Paco Amoroso and Jorge Drexler, who each received seven nominations.

What will you do if you win the award?Maybe I’ll do a concert at home. I want to do something that feels honest and authentic, because that’s what led me to being nominated. I dream of becoming a bearer of my culture. What better way to do it than being recognized in the eyes of the Latin Recording Academy?

What was the inspiration for your latest single “Compa Coleto (Uy_Como)”?My muse is always my tiburón, my shark. It’s no secret that I’m in love. It just makes sense to talk about him in these really loving and cool songs. It’s a song with flirtatious vibes. When you understand the song’s lyrics, it just makes sense to flirt that way and have fun with it.

Who do you want to collaborate with in the future?I would love to collaborate with Shakira and Beéle. I feel they do a lot for Barranquilla as well. I would love to collaborate with Bomba Estéreo from Santa Marta. Maybe I’ll do vallenato music with Silvestre Dangond. That would be really cool. Felipe Peláez has an amazing voice. It would be cool to bring these sounds of our own to the world.

Yuzu Kosho Edamame 柚子こしょう枝豆

📅 Sep 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This Yuzu Kosho Edamame coats buttery, sweet edamame pods in a ponzu and yuzu kosho marinade. It takes three ingredients and a few minutes of hands-on prep to turn plain edamame into a citrusy snack with a slow-building kick of spice. If you loved my Spicy Edamame recipe, this could become your new favorite.

This post may contain affiliate links. Please read my disclosure policy.

Yuzu kosho edamame is a chilled appetizer or snack made by marinating edamame in ponzu and yuzu kosho (柚子胡椒). Yuzu kosho is a fermented condiment of yuzu zest, chilies, and sea salt. It adds a gentle, tangy heat that clings to the pods and seasons the beans inside.

After making homemade yuzu kosho for the first time with green yuzu from my friend’s tree, I couldn’t stop thinking of new ways to use it. This Yuzu Kosho Edamame was inspired by a small izakaya in my hometown, Yokohama. They served it with fresh seasonal edamame, and it was simple yet so addicting.

I don’t have access to fresh edamame here in the U.S., but my homemade yuzu kosho gives frozen edamame pods a fresh, bright flavor. We love this spicy appetizer for casual get-togethers.

If you love yuzu kosho, try my homemade Yuzu Kosho, Hamachi Teriyaki with Yuzu Kosho, and Miso Soup with Yuzu Kosho next!

For this recipe, you’ll need unshelled edamame pods, yuzu kosho, and ponzu.

A Bicycle Journey Across Brazil to Find a Vanishing Savanna

📅 Aug 13, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In “Here There Was Cerrado” — the Second-Place Winner of the 2026 Yale Environment 360 Film Contest — Natascha Otoya shows how industrial farms are plowing up the Brazilian savanna, changing the flow of water, and skewing human memory of the landscape.

In 2025, environmental historian Natascha Otoya traveled for a week through Brazil’s Cerrado region, pedaling more than 200 miles through western Bahia and Tocantins. Originally spanning close to 800,000 square miles, the Cerrado is the most biodiverse savanna on the planet and Brazil’s second largest biome. But in the last 40 years, more than half of the Cerrado has been converted to cattle pastures and to monocultures of corn and soybeans, most of which will be exported for animal feed.

Otoya’s expedition, part of her postdoctoral research at the Universidade Evangélica de Goiás Environmental History Laboratory of the Cerrado, focused on the collection of data and on empirical experience. The slower pace of the bicycle, she said, “opens space for interactions with local people, which can unearth stories, leads, details, and perspectives that might otherwise have been missed.”

Filming as she pedaled along endless fields of row crops sustained by center pivot irrigation, she reached the edge of the agrarian plateau to gaze down upon an intact section of the Cerrado — a thriving and complex mosaic of grasslands, wooded savannas, and gallery forests that support a vast array of plant species, mammals, birds, amphibians, and reptiles.

Otoya made the film to raise awareness of the Cerrado and how it is changing. “I believe environmental advocacy and public policy should be informed by the work of environmental historians,” she said, noting that documentary films can be more accessible to the public than more scholarly works.

About the Filmmaker: Natascha de Vasconcellos Otoya is an environmental historian whose work explores the intersection of geology, ecology, and human energy pursuits. Her scholarship has been published in leading journals, such as Environmental Humanities, and has been supported by multiple grants, including from the American Society for Environmental History, the Medical Humanities program at Georgetown University, and the Brazilian Ministry of Education.

About the Contest: Now in its 13th season, the Yale Environment 360 Film Contest honors the year’s best environmental documentary shorts from around the world. This year we received 596 submissions from more than 80 countries across six continents, with the winners selected by the editors of Yale Environment 360.

ABC investigations boss leaves as Four Corners probe continues

📅 Aug 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jo Puccini is leaving the ABC after more than 25 years. (Supplied)

Award-winning ABC investigations boss Jo Puccini is leaving the public broadcaster after a 25-year career.

She had oversight of Four Corners and Background Briefing, which recently removed three programs by reporter Mahmood Fazal over accuracy concerns.

The ABC Ombudsman's investigation into Fazal's reporting continues.

The ABC's head of investigations and current affairs, Jo Puccini, is leaving the public broadcaster, the ABC has announced.

In an email to ABC staff, acting news director Donna Field said Puccini had shaped significant public interest journalism, leading to numerous Walkley awards.

"We wish Jo every success in whatever comes next," she said.

The announcement comes alongside a deepening investigation into the work of sacked Four Corners reporter Mahmood Fazal. The long-running current affairs program falls under Puccini's oversight.

Puccini joined the ABC in 2000 from the Seven Network, working at Media Watch as a researcher, before moving to Four Corners, where she won three Walkley Awards.

She held senior roles at Media Watch, Lateline, 7.30, the National Reporting Team and ABC Investigations.

Puccini said it was time for a new chapter in her career.

"I'm proud of the journalism I've helped produce and the impact it's had on helping our audience understand the issues and events that affect them," she said.

"It's been an adventure and a privilege to work at the national broadcaster, an institution where I've made lifelong friends. It's now time for a new chapter in my career.

"I'll be watching and cheering you all on to great success and important journalism that will continue to benefit the Australian public."

Three stories by Mahmood Fazal have been withdrawn by the ABC. (ABC News)

Three stories by Fazal have been withdrawn by the public broadcaster in recent weeks over concerns about their accuracy.

Other senior managers at the broadcaster at the time of Fazal's reporting have already left the broadcaster for unrelated reasons, including Four Corners executive producer Mathew Carney, news director Justin Stevens and managing director David Anderson.

Once a member of the Mongols bikie club, Fazal switched careers, becoming an award-winning journalist specialising in crime, violence, imprisonment and terrorism.

He was terminated after an ABC investigation into his appearance on a podcast with a former underworld figure.

In recent weeks, the two Four Corners episodes Cocaine Nation and Meth Highway were removed by the ABC due to accuracy concerns, along with a Background Briefing episode titled "Remy was 12 when she became a drug dealer".

Further concerns over the accuracy of reporting by sacked Four Corners reporter Mahmood Fazal are raised on Media Watch, including that a hire car was used to mislead viewers in the profile of a purported cocaine dealer.

On Monday, Media Watch reported on claims that "Remy", who purported to be a cocaine dealer in the Background Briefing and a Four Corners episode, was self-styled Sydney rapper Charissa Habkouk, who said the core of her story was invented.

Ms Habkouk told Media Watch she considered Remy "an acting role and a favour to Mahmood".

"All of that … is a complete fable … there's no validity to [it] … [Fazal] knew I could play the part and look the part," she said.

Media Watch also alleged the Mercedes-Benz that "Remy" and Fazal drove around in the Four Corners episode was hired by the ABC and not owned by "Remy" as the program implied.

In a response to Media Watch, Fazal rejected Ms Habkouk's claims and said he stood by the "integrity of my reporting and the editorial processes that underpinned both programs".

"I reject the suggestion that I fabricated, staged or knowingly published information that I believed to be false," Fazal said in a statement.

The ABC has previously said Fazal's episodes were removed "following concerns identified about the veracity of sources used in the three programs".

"The ABC Ombudsman is already conducting an independent review of whether the Four Corners programs complied with the ABC's editorial standards," it said in a statement.

Puccini's departure comes on the same day the ABC has apologised for a Race Around the World episode containing remarks about mining magnate Gina Rinehart, despite the ABC Ombudsman finding it did not breach editorial standards.

The episode featured contestant Kate McGuinness describing a fictional scenario that involved a violent attack on Mrs Rinehart.

The ABC apologises for a Race Around the World episode containing remarks about mining magnate Gina Rinehart AO.

Managing director Hugh Marks said the segment fell short of community expectations.

"While the Ombudsman has found that the film did not breach the ABC's editorial policies, the ABC acknowledges the episode has fallen short of community expectations," Mr Marks said in a statement.

"The ABC apologises to Mrs Rinehart and to anyone else affected or upset by the publication of the film."

Mrs Rinehart's company, Hancock Prospecting, wrote to the ABC Board on July 23 calling on the ABC to immediately remove the material, publicly apologise and investigate how the material came to be published, with full accountability for those responsible, a company statement said.

McGuinness released a statement through the ABC last week, apologising to anyone she offended and saying the sense of humour featured in her films on Race Around the World was "farcical".

"In no way was it my intention to promote violence," she said.

"My writing is cartoonish and always satirical.

"Unfortunately, jokes do not land with everyone. Its purpose was never to be taken literally."

Who Gets to Decide the Fate of a Pristine Alaskan Rainforest?

📅 Aug 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In “Land Less” — the Third-Place Winner of the 2026 Yale Environment 360 Film Contest — filmmaker Alexi Liotti follows the activists fighting a bill that would transfer thousands of acres of Alaskan rainforest to five Native corporations, opening the land to logging.

More than half a century ago, the U.S. Congress transferred 44 million acres of public lands in Alaska to for-profit Native corporations, which have been free to exploit those lands, including by logging them. Last year, the state’s congressional delegation introduced a new bill, the Alaska Native Landless Equity Act, which would transfer an additional 115,000 acres from the Tongass National Forest — the world’s largest temperate rainforest — to five newly formed Native corporations. Those lands contain 80,000 acres of ancient forest, including 60,000 roadless acres. Should the bill pass, many environmental activists fear much of that land will be clearcut.

Last summer, British Columbia-based filmmaker Alexi Liotti followed one of those activists, Joshua Wright, as he traveled through southeast Alaska, using satellite imagery to document pristine and highly biodiverse areas at risk of being logged under the Landless Act. In Juneau, Wright met with Native Tlingit matriarch and Tongass defender Wanda Culp, who decried the proposed legislation and likened the logging of old-growth forest to “slicing up our culture.”

As Liotti filmed, he says his focus shifted from the Landless Act itself to questioning who gets to take responsibility for and ultimately decide the future of public lands. “I hope people come away seeing places like the Tongass not as lines on a map,” he says, “but as living landscapes whose future depends on the choices we make today.”

About the Filmmaker: Alexi Liotti is a Canadian documentary filmmaker whose work explores the relationship between people, place, and nature. His debut feature documentary, Rematriation, received the Best Environmental Film award at the Vancouver International Mountain Film Festival. His films have been supported by ORTOVOX, Arc’teryx, The North Face, Red Bull, and the CBC.

About the Contest: Now in its 13th season, the Yale Environment 360 Film Contest honors the year’s best environmental documentary shorts from around the world. This year we received 596 submissions from more than 80 countries across six continents, with the winners selected by the editors of Yale Environment 360.

How to Embody a Primal Lifestyle

📅 Jun 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

If I stopped you on the street today and offered you a million dollars to improve your health or fitness, you’d probably immediately think diet and exercise. Of course you’d be right—but only to a degree. Those two factors are fundamental to health, fitness, vitality, and longevity. However, if you only focused on diet and exercise, you wouldn’t reach your full potential. Not by a long shot.

As important as food and movement are, they are only two of the inputs that affect gene expression. There is a bigger picture to consider, another pillar encompassed within the Primal Blueprint—one that is often overlooked even by individuals who have made a sincere commitment to building healthy habits and taking care of their bodies for the long haul. These are the Primal Blueprint Lifestyle Laws.

If I had to nominate the Primal Blueprint Law that is most neglected in the modern world—and it would be hard to pick just one—it would probably be sleep. At least with diet and exercise, most people pay lip service to how important they are, recognizing that they should care about them even if their day-to-day choices say otherwise. Yet how many times have you seen uber-successful people boasting about how little sleep they get on a nightly basis?

Too many folks glorify an “I’ll sleep when I’m dead” mentality, ignoring the fact that too sleep is, ironically, pushing them toward an early grave.

Our genes expect and need consistent, restful sleep, and plenty of it. Our ancestors got lots of sleep. Even after the discovery of fire, it wasn’t as if they stayed up all night partying. From sunset to sunrise it was safer to huddle together and rest. Long days of hunting and gathering and otherwise working hard for every bite of food also required sufficient time to repair and recover. Studies of modern hunter-gatherers suggest it wasn’t necessarily always an uninterrupted nine or ten hours, either. It’s likely that they slept together as families or as small tribes, keeping a watch out for predators, breastfeeding the baby or just dozing in and out throughout the night.

Of course, a restorative afternoon nap was also available when the urge hit, with no guilt about what else they really should have been doing. Our modern tendency toward poor quality, insufficient sleep has catastrophic consequences for physical, cognitive, and emotional health. Yet better sleep is almost always within reach with some simple (and sometimes not-so-easy) behavior modifications.

(Primal Law #7 is Play. You can read about it in the Primal Movement post.)

Cavemen weren’t really men (or women) who lived their lives in caves all the time. Most of the day, they were in the great outdoors pursuing their various survival tasks. Regular exposure to sun provided lots of vitamin D, an all-important vitamin (really more like a hormone) which they could not easily obtain from food and which their bodies could not manufacture without direct sunlight.

Fast forward to today, and most people spend almost all their time inside—at home, at work, in the car or subway, at the grocery store, mall, movie theaters, bowling alleys, and on and on. Even most of exercise is now done inside gyms, dance studios, or garages. The net result is that an alarming proportion of the population does not get enough sun exposure. Couple that with widespread public health messages about avoiding the sun and using sunscreen every time you go out, and vitamin D deficiency is now widespread.

Not to mention, we are missing out on the other benefits of being in nature. While it’s never a good idea to burn, I think the far greater danger to most people is too little sun, not too much. Respect basic sun safety practices, but embrace the sun as the life-giving force that it is.

You’re doing all these other things to live a long, healthy life—eating right, exercising, getting plenty of sleep, nurturing your social relationships—but it can all be cut short in the blink of an eye with one silly, preventable misstep. That’s why Primal Law #9 reminds you to exercise appropriate caution when navigating this dangerous world.

In our ancestors’ time, this mostly meant avoiding trauma that could lead to immediate death or serious injury that would prevent them from being active, participating members of their hunter-gatherer clan. Today it still means essentially the same thing, except the dangers we face are a far cry from the rogue tiger or poisonous mushroom that might have taken out Grok.

This doesn’t mean wrap yourself in bubble wrap and never take any risks whatsoever. Rather, this law exhorts you to use that highly evolved human brain of yours to calculate risk and take wise precautions as needed.

Speaking of your big brain, the final Primal Law reminds you to use it. Use it to problem solve. Use it to create. Use it to imagine. Use it to dream big dreams and make them come true. Use it to reminisce about the past. Use it to learn from past mistakes. Use it to create the wild and wonderful life that is your birthright as a human.

Mark’s Daily Apple is still under construction. My team and I are actively working to repost the archives. Check back regularly to find posts that are new to you and revisit old favorites. Looking for a specific post? Let us know here.

You can help more people find us by spreading the word:

* This blog reflects my personal views and opinions and isn’t intended as medical advice, but I hope it will be informative and inspiring as you pursue a healthy, fulfilling life.

Ocean Acidification Investigation

📅 Jun 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The ocean covers about 71% of the Earth’s surface and holds about 97% of our planet’s total water. Despite the vastness of our oceans, they are being massively impacted by human activity. Microplastics have been found at the bottom of the Mariana Trench, fish populations are declining due to overfishing, and the oceans are warming and rising.

Burning coal for energy, driving cars, and other activities that use fossil fuels release carbon dioxide into the atmosphere. The oceans absorb about a quarter of the carbon dioxide released by human activity, which might sound like a good thing, but even though the oceans are vast, the huge amount of carbon dioxide they’ve absorbed has caused their pH to drop, a process called acidification.

Our oceans have become about 30% more acidic over the last 200 years, a change faster than anything known over the last 50 million years!

Increasing ocean acidity is expected to have huge impacts on marine life. As with any change, some organisms will survive and maybe even thrive, some struggle to adapt, and some will not survive. The effects are wide ranging, and it’s not just lost biodiversity, but a potential threat to food supplies for people.

As seawater gets more acidic, several things happen.

You can use an egg and vinegar to demonstrate visually how sea creatures can lose their protective shells in increasingly acidic waters.

One simple way to demonstrate the effect of ocean acidification on the shells of marine life is to place an egg in vinegar and watch as the vinegar dissolves the shell.

Eggshells are made from calcium carbonate ( CaCO3 ), which is the same substance corals, clams, oysters, and other marine animals use to build their skeletons and shells.

Acidic ocean water contains carbonic acid, but we’re going to use another weak acid, which you probably have in your kitchen, acetic acid ( vinegar ).

When you place an egg in vinegar, the acid breaks down the calcium carbonate, releasing carbon dioxide gas ( you’ll see little bubbles being released around the shell ). The same corrosion happens to the shells of marine organisms, but much more slowly. Vinegar has a pH of around 3, and the ocean around 8.1. The acidic ocean weakens shells over time, making them thinner and more fragile.

You might be thinking that if the ocean has a pH of 8.1, it’s not acidic, but even though pH 7 is neutral and anything above that is basic, the ocean is more acidic than it was in previous years. Acidification doesn’t mean the ocean is turning into acid, but rather that it is becoming more acidic, and even a very small change in pH can substantially affect marine life.

Vinegar – White vinegar is good, as you can see the egg better, but malt vinegar also works.

Carefully place the egg in the container.

Pour vinegar into the container until the egg is completely covered.

Leave for about 24 hours, then remove the egg and rinse it carefully under running water. You should be able to rub away some of the shell.

To remove the shell completely, place the egg back in the vinegar for another 24 hours.

Once you’ve removed all the eggshell, you should be able to gently bounce the shell-less egg.

Vinegar contains acetic acid, which reacts with the calcium carbonate in the egg shell to form calcium ions, water and carbon dioxide.

The dissolving of the eggshell shows how much impact ocean acidification can have on marine life.

Don’t Visit Ko Lipe

📅 Feb 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

After 19 years, I finally went back to Ko Lipe, the Thai island I spent close to a month on in 2006. Back then, it was one of those super off-the-beaten-path destinations that few but the most intrepid travelers visited, where electricity only ran a few hours a day, basic bungalows right on the beach cost something like $2 USD, and there really was a last boat for the season.

There was much to do here but that was the point. You hung out on the beach, read a book, went snorkeling, went back to the beach, drank beers at the one beach bar on the island, rotated meals between the five restaurants there, and went to bed early.

It was paradise – and a place a lot of people got stuck. Days easily turned into weeks here.

If you asked me what the highlight of all my travels was, I would be the time I spent on Ko Lipe. I made incredible friends, lounged around, got to know the locals, learned a bit of Thai, and, overall, lived that idyllic backpacker life we all dream about.

Over the years, I’ve avoided going back to Ko Lipe because the memory of my time there is so strong that I didn’t want to ruin it. Any re-visit would simply be trying to recreated a magic that couldn’t be recreated because the people that it special wouldn’t be there. I’d be chasing travel ghosts. And, since I know my sleepy paradise has been developed greatly over the years, I was also too afraid seeing that would make me sad.

Tourism in Thailand tends toward the unsustainable. No island really develops in a good way. It’s all build, build, build.

And I didn’t want to see my Ko Lipe like that.

But as I was planning my recent trip through Southeast Asia, returning to Ko Lipe made sense. I was heading down the Indian Ocean side of Thailand on my way into Malaysia and I’d pass by it.

And, since I was looking for a lively place for New Year’s Eve, it seemed liked the best choice. I knew there would be travelers there and there were no other nearby islands that would work, especially since Ko Lipe has a boat to Langkawi, which was my next stop.

And I’m sad to report that Ko Lipe took the Ko Phi Phi model of tourism and is now extremely overdeveloped.

Most of the island is now paved over, the old dirt footpaths having become concrete for the cars and construction trucks. Swaths of palm trees are now the sites of high-end resorts with pools (on an island with no natural water supply). Construction of more resorts continues at a fast pace. The coral around the island is dying, a victim of all the boats, anchors, pollution, and overfishing. Beaches are now lined with boats, their exhaust spilling into the ocean, leaving a shiny film you can see as you swim. And the restaurants cater to tourists looking for bad Western food, not great Thai cuisine.

The island’s boom has displaced many locals, who were forced to sell to mainland developers, and much of the island’s workforce is now from the mainland. They see little of the benefits this tourism boom.

So lies Ko Lipe, another victim of Thailand’s all too common overdevelopment and exploitation of limited resources.

I met lots of people there who loved the island. If it’s your first time, I can see why you would love it. After all, the area is postcard perfect, the water is perfectly an azure blue, the sand is a beautiful white, and since you’re surrounded by a national park, a lot of tours take you to some secluded islands.

And, in comparison to Ko Phi Phi, Krabi, or Phuket, it is less developed so I can’t fault someone stepping here for the first time going “wow!”

But, as I reflect on the island and its overdevelopment, I have come to the same conclusion I have about Ko Phi Phi: people shouldn’t visit.

I’m not against growth, but I’m against this kind of growth. It’s not sustainably managed and going there will only tax the island’s limited resources even further. You can’t put the genie back in the bottle and no local is going to say “sure, I’ll stay broke so you can an idealized vision of the world.”

And, with so many other islands to visit that are well managed (Ko Lanta, Ko Jum, and Ko Mook, to name three nearby), I think you should skip Ko Lipe.

A visit there will only make things worse.

It pains me to say that, because it was such a beautiful place, and my original visit had a huge impact on my life. But if we’re going to be good stewards and travelers, sometimes you just have to say enough is enough.

And Ko Lipe is a place where enough is enough.

Go somewhere else that is better managed.

Riding elephants in Thailand went away when consumers became more conscious of it. Eco lodges got big because of consumers. Overtourism is talked about by consumers as much as it is by locals.

Maybe if enough people start to do something, Ko Lipe will change.

But, at the very least, by not going you are at least no contributing to the problem.

Sprites on the Web

📅 Feb 23, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In 2015, back when Twitter was still Twitter, their dev team had a problem.

In those early days, tweets could be “favourited” by clicking a little “⭐” icon. The product team wanted to migrate to “liking” tweets, Facebook-style, with a “❤️”. As part of this update, their designers created this lovely animation:

This looks super nice, but there’s kind of a lot going on in there; by my count, there are 16 separate elements all animating at the same time (14 particles, the popping circle, the heart). Twitter’s web app needed to run on very low-end mobile devices, so it wasn’t feasible to create this procedurally using DOM nodes. Instead, they decided to borrow a technique from video games: sprites.

The basic idea with a sprite is that we create a single image that contains each individual frame of an animation in a long strip. Then, we display each frame for a fraction of a second, like a roll of film sliding through an oldschool film projector:

This technique has been around for decades; I vaguely remember learning how to do this with background-position in the early 2000s. Fortunately, modern CSS has given us some great new options for implementing sprites!

In this blog post, I’ll show you the best way I’ve found to work with sprites in CSS and share some of the use cases I’ve discovered. We’ll also talk about some of the trade-offs, to see when we shouldn’t use sprites.

Your operating system has the “Reduce motion” option enabled. I generally do my best to respect this preference on this blog, but for this post in particular, there are several motion-heavy animations that have not been disabled. These animations are necessary in order to understand the animation technique.

If you have a motion sensitivity, please exercise caution with this blog post!

First thing’s first, we need an asset! Let’s use a gold trophy sprite I created a few years ago:

To produce the illusion that the fire is flickering, I drew five different versions of the blue flames. These frames are stacked side-by-side in a single image known as a “spritesheet”:

Here’s the fundamental strategy: we’ll create an tag and calculate its size based on one of these frames. We can then use object-fit and object-position to control which part of the sprite is currently visible, flipping through each frame using a CSS keyframe animation.

This full image has a native resolution of 2000px × 800px, and contains 5 frames. This means that each frame is 400px × 800px. In order for this image to look sharp on high-resolution displays, we’ll want to cut this size in half, so our final image will be 200px × 400px.

By default, tags will try to squeeze the entire image content into the DOM node’s area, meaning we’ll wind up seeing all 5 trophies, crammed together:

This happens because of the “object-fit” CSS property(opens in new tab). This property controls what happens when there’s a mismatch between the size of the underlying image and the size of the element.

The default value is fill, which tries to ensure that the entire image is visible, even if it has to be squashed. Let’s switch to cover:

Now we’re getting somewhere! cover will scale the underlying image so that it covers the entire area of the node. As a result, we wind up seeing 1/5th of the total image.

Next, we can use the object-position property to control which part of the underlying image is shown:

If you’re familiar with the SVG format, what we’re doing here is conceptually similar to modifying the viewBox to control which part of the image is displayed. In this case, the tag is a 200×400 window into our trophy sprite, and we can slide the underlying image data around using the object-position property.

We’re almost there, but there’s one final wrinkle we need to iron out: the animation. How do we set this up so that we flip between each trophy variant?

Let’s try adding a looping keyframe animation:

The problem is that we’re sliding the image smoothly, rather than moving in discrete steps. For this technique to work, we need to display each of the 5 frames for an equal amount of time.

We could do this in JavaScript with setInterval(), but there’s an obscure CSS timing function we can use for this instead: steps.

The core idea with steps is that instead of transitioning smoothly using a Bézier curve, the value jumps between a specified number of midpoints. A staircase, instead of a ramp. This’ll be clearer with a visualization:

The steps timing function allows us to split the total progression into discrete values. In this case, we’re specifying 5 steps, and the animation will spend 1/5th of the total duration on each step.

We call the steps function with the number of total steps and the “step position”. We’ll unpack that in a bit, but first, here’s a complete implementation of our trophy sprite, sliding the image data within the node using object-position:

You might be wondering why we went through all of this trouble. Couldn’t we accomplish the same goal with an ?

The main benefit of this approach over an animated GIF is that we have a lot more control. We can change how fast the animation runs by tweaking animation-duration. We can also start/stop the animation at precisely the right time using animation-play-state. GIFs don’t have a pause button, and they tend to be a bit inconsistent in terms of their timing.

Additionally, this approach tends to be more performant, especially when optimized. In the real component, I’ve plucked the flickering blue flames into their own separate spritesheet and layered them behind a static gold trophy. Both images use the modern .avif image format. The combined images are under 30kb, while a .gif would be over 100kb (and limited to just 256 colors!).

To be fair, there are plenty of circumstances where an animated GIF will work just fine. But once you start experimenting with sprites, I think you’ll agree that it opens up a lot of interesting new doors. I share a more advanced use case a bit later on.

In the playground above, you might’ve noticed something a bit odd:

The steps() function takes two arguments. The first argument is the number of steps, which is pretty self-explanatory. But what on earth is jump-none?

The second argument is the “step position”, and it has a default value of jump-end. In this mode, steps() will exclude the final value from its discrete values. For example, if our keyframe definition goes from 0% to 100% and we set steps(5), the levels will be 0%, 20%, 40%, 60%, and 80%. It will never actually reach 100%.

Here’s a playground that showcases this clearly:

Our fill keyframe goes from width: 0% to width: 100%, but the .bar element never gets beyond 80% width!

I found this quite perplexing at first, but I realized that this behaviour makes much more sense for non-looping animations:

Over the course of this 2-second animation, the bar’s width grows from 0% to 80%. When the animation expires, right at the 2-second mark, the final value from our keyframe definition (width: 100%) is applied.

So, by default, steps() has a “step position” of jump-end, causing it to jump to the final value at the very end of the animation. Without the jump, our bar would become full-width at the 1.6 second mark, which would feel premature in a lot of situations.

When it comes to looping animations like our trophy sprite, however, we don’t want to do any jumping. We don’t want to land on the final frame right as the animation expires, we want to include that final frame as one of the 5 discrete values that we flip between. And we can do that by specifying steps(5, jump-none).

Unfortunately, the steps() function doesn’t handle interrupts gracefully. This isn’t a problem for our gold trophy animation, since it runs indefinitely, but it can cause problems in other circumstances.

For example, Karey Higuera was using sprites to create a cool lever interaction. It works great when clicking slowly, but if the transition is interrupted, things get a bit funky:

This happens because CSS transitions are reversed when interrupted, which changes the discrete values used in steps(). It’s beyond the scope of this blog post, but you can read more about the interrupt behaviour in the CSSWG spec(opens in new tab).

The solution Karey found was to use a linear timing function with the new round() function, which rounds a value to the nearest specified increment. You can read more about this approach in Karey’s post:

Now that we’ve covered the basics of this technique, let’s talk about when we should actually use it. And, just as importantly, when we shouldn’t.

I mentioned at the start that the Twitter development team chose to use a sprite-based approach in part due to performance considerations*Source: I met one of Twitter’s devs back at a conference in 2016, and he told me. I think this was valid back in 2015, but I would push back against this in 2026. Devices have gotten much faster and browsers have gotten much more optimized in the years since; even the lowest-end devices ought to be able to handle 14 particles animating at the same time without breaking a sweat. And when we use a sprite for something like this, we lose some of the magic.

In my upcoming course, Whimsical Animations(opens in new tab), we build the following “Like” button. Try clicking it a few times:

The lovely thing about this approach is that it’s a bit different every time you click on it. The particles are being procedurally generated using trigonometry and randomness. By contrast, Twitter’s “Like” button is exactly the same every time you click it. It’s like we’re replaying the same video, over and over and over. 😬

So, when should we use sprites? I think the main use case is for things that, well, look like sprites! In addition to the gold trophy example, here’s another example from a generative art project(opens in new tab) I released years ago:

This little cat wanders onto the screen after a while. If you hover over her, she’ll encourage you to follow me on Bluesky(opens in new tab).

It’s a very silly example, but I think it really showcases how much more powerful sprites can be, compared to animated GIFs. We can make it so much more dynamic. For example, if you don’t interact with her for a while, she falls asleep:

While this technique is seldomly used on the web these days, it’s used all the time in video games. There’s an enormous number of spritesheets available online. You can use this technique to have a little Sonic or Mega Man run across your site!*JOSH W COMEAU ASSUMES NO LIABILITY FOR ANY COPYRIGHT INFRINGEMENT CAUSED BY YOUR USE OF ANY INTELLECTUAL PROPERTY OWNED BY COMPANIES OR INDIVIDUALS INCLUDING BUT NOT LIMITED TO NINTENDO®, SEGA®, OR CAPCOM®.

Comment cuisiner le chou-fleur ?

📅 Feb 11, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Chou de Chypre, fleur de Bretagne… il en aura connu des noms au fil des siècles, le chou-fleur ! Originaire du Sud-Est de l'Europe et du Moyen Orient, il revient à la mode en France au XVIIe siècle sous le règne de Louis XIV… Découvrez nos astuces pour le rendre savoureux, digeste et original avec les idées de la cheffe Justine Piluso.

Contenu supprimé de votre liste de lecture

Contenu ajouté à votre liste de lecture dans l'espace Mon compte

Contenu ajouté à votre liste de lecture dans l'espace Mon compte

Pour ajouter ce contenu à votre liste de lecture, vous devez être connecté(e)

Cliquez ici pour ajouter Régal à vos sources préférées sur Google.

Mis à jour le 11 févr. 2026

Écrit par Eve-Marie Zizza-Lalu (Directrice de la rédaction de Régal) et La rédaction de Régal et mis à jour par Pauline Guillet (Auteure culinaire)

Avec notre expert Justine Piluso (Cheffe de cuisine)

De la famille des brassicacées, comme le chou pommé ou le brocoli, le chou-fleur se décline en 726 variétés… Saviez-vous qu’il avait longtemps été vert, orange ou rose avant de devenir blanc à la suite de croisements ? Revenues au goût du jour, ces versions colorées ont une saveur plus délicate que leur cousin blanc. Attention, elles sont aussi plus chères. La Bretagne est la terre de prédilection pour la culture du chou-fleur (80 % de la production française), où il est appelé fleur de Bretagne.

L'histoire du chou-fleur est un peu particulière, car s'il était très apprécié dans l'Antiquité par les Grecs et les Romains, le Moyen Age le fait passer dans l'oubli, et il devient un légume du pauvre. Il aura donc fallu attendre le Roi Soleil et son botaniste La Quintinie pour que le chou retrouve sa place sur les tables de la cour. L'une des favorites de Louis XV, la comtesse du Barry a d'ailleurs donné son nom à une célèbre recette de velouté à base de chou-fleur.

C'est la pleine saison du chou-fleur de septembre à avril. Choisissez des bouquets blancs, serrés et non flétris. Il existe trois présentations de chou-fleur dans le commerce :

• le chou-fleur couronné (les feuilles enveloppantes sont légèrement épointées. C'est la présentation la plus courante)

• le chou-fleur demi-couronné ou tête de moine (les feuilles enveloppantes sont coupées à leur moitié, la panne du chou-fleur apparaît à moitié)

• le chou-fleur effeuillé (dépourvu entièrement de ses feuilles, à l'exception des 4 à 5 feuilles du coeur, commercialisé sous film).

Le chou-fleur se conserve quelques jours cru dans le bac à légumes du réfrigérateur. Une fois cuit, il faut le consommer rapidement. Il se congèle après avoir été blanchi 3 min dans l'eau bouillante ; il est préférable de congeler les bouquets bien égouttés et détachés les uns des autres, ce qui permet de prélever juste la quantité nécessaire.

Plus le chou-fleur reste croquant, plus il est digeste ! Vérifiez toujours la cuisson avec la pointe d’un couteau ; la lame doit s’enfoncer sans résistance, mais le chou-fleur doit rester ferme.

Comptez 8 à 15 min selon la taille des morceaux.

Départ eau froide (pour un chou-fleur plus croquant) ou eau bouillante (pour une texture plus tendre) au choix. Ensuite, comptez 8 à 15 min de cuisson à partir de l'ébullition, en fonction de la taille des morceaux. Puis égouttez et rincez le chou-fleur à l'eau froide pour stopper sa cuisson.

Pendant la cuisson à l'eau, le chou-fleur dégage une forte odeur qui peut vite devenir désagréable. Voici quelques conseils pour y remédier :

Comptez environ 30 min à 180 degrés. Pour le rendre plus digeste, vous pouvez le faire blanchir 3 à 5 min dans de l’eau bouillante avant la cuisson au four.

Adieu les mauvais souvenirs de cantine. Justine Piluso réhabilite ce légume aux multiples facettes en puisant dans tous les répertoires, d'Ottolenghi à Antonin Carême. Le chou-fleur nouveau est arrivé !

Tout ce qui est blanc va bien avec le chou-fleur : le yaourt, l'ail, les oignons, le fromage, les amandes, et même le chocolat blanc ! Il n'y a pas de frontière sucré-salé avec le chou-fleur. D'ailleurs David Gallienne avait réalisé un dessert chou-fleur mangue-coco-passion pour la finale de “Top Chef” en 2020. J'aime bien l'association avec la vanille et la noisette.

Je le marie également à toutes sortes d'épices, piments, noix ou racines : curry, safran, cumin, noix de muscade, gingembre, curcuma…

Pour moi, c'est un légume qui matche avec les poissons, les fruits de mer, beaucoup moins avec la viande. Sa douceur appelle aussi la saveur umami que l'on retrouve dans les fromages à pâte pressée, l'anchois, le chorizo et, bien sûr, le caviar…

Vous pouvez le déguster cru, comme la plupart des crudités en le trempant dans une sauce aux herbes. Cuit, il accompagnera la viande et le poisson et trouvera sa place dans vos gratins et même grillé. Il est aussi délicieux dans les sautés de légumes, les quiches, ou les soufflés. Le chou-fleur mixé finement et pressé, mélangé avec un œuf, du parmesan et de la poudre d'amande fait aussi une excellente pâte à pizza sans gluten…

Tout se mange dans le chou-fleur ! Ne jetez pas le pied, il sera parfait dans une soupe. Vous pouvez aussi tailler le chou-fleur entier, transversalement comme un steak, pour en faire un burger végétal. Les feuilles, quant à elles, peuvent être émincées, sautées au wok, préparées en pesto, glissées dans la soupe…

Faites le plein d'idées recettes et d'astuces dans notre newsletter !

When is the Earth closest to the sun?

📅 Jan 02, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Did you know that the Earth is about 3 million miles closer to the sun in winter than in summer?

This year (2026), Earth is closest to the Sun on Saturday, 3rd January. This specific point in the Earth’s orbit is called perihelion.

The point in Earth’s orbit at which the Sun is farthest from Earth is called aphelion.

In January, the Northern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun, which means sunlight hits the ground at a shallower angle than when the Earth is tilted towards the Sun. The shallow angle means that the sun’s heat is spread out as it travels through the atmosphere, making the weather colder.

The Southern Hemisphere is tilted towards the Sun in January, which is why it experiences summer during perihelion in the Northern Hemisphere.

Fun Fact: The Earth moves faster when it’s closest to the Sun (because of gravity), so winter in the Northern Hemisphere is about 5 days shorter than summer!

Read about Kepler’s Second Law to learn more about this!

Elf Basketball STEM Challenge

📅 Nov 28, 2025  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This fun elf-themed basketball STEM challenge is a great hands-on science activity for learning about transfer of energy. The idea is to build a lolly stick catapult and a basketball net for an elf!

You could set this up as a ready made activity and encourage children to experiment using different projectiles on the catapult. Small pom pom balls and ping pong balls work well! Or, another idea is to leave instructions and materials out so children can build their own creations!

If you want to make the activity more structured, use my FREE instructions and template at the bottom of the post.

Follow my easy lolly stick catapult instructions or watch the video below.

Build a mini basketball hoop. Use our design for inspiration.

Try pushing the catapult arm all the way down, then halfway, to see how that affects the ball’s flight.

Experiment with a table tennis ball, pom poms or even marshmallows.

When the catapult arm is pushed down, it stores potential energy, which is transferred to the ball as kinetic energy when the arm is released.

As the ball flies higher, it starts to slow down. The ball’s kinetic energy is temporarily converted to potential energy as it gains height. As the ball starts to fall, it speeds up again, and the stored potential energy is turned back into kinetic energy. Gravity acting on the ball gives it a curved path through the air.

The amount of stored energy – the further the catapult arm is pushed down, the more elastic potential energy is stored, so the ball flies further.

The angle of the launch – for maximum horizontal distance of flight, the launch angle should be about 45 degrees.

The weight of the ball—lighter projectiles usually fly further than heavier balls when the same amount of force is used, as less force is needed to accelerate them.

Flexibility of the sticks and elastic bands – more flexible materials can store more energy.

This free pack includes a simple one-page worksheet and a more detailed three-page design and evaluation pack.

If you like this idea, take a look at my 24 days of FREE elf activity ideas!!

How do fireworks fly?

📅 Nov 06, 2025  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Have you ever wondered how fireworks fly? The science behind the flight of a firework is the same as that of a rocket, and a fantastic demonstration of Newton’s Third Law of Motion.

For every action there is an equal and opposite reaction.

Just like a rocket, a firework needs a force to overcome gravity. The force is provided by chemicals at the bottom of the firework. When the firework is lit, the chemicals combust, producing a large amount of hot gas inside the firework. The hot gas escapes downwards through a small hole at the bottom of the firework.

This strong downward force creates an equal and opposite upwards force, propelling the firework into the sky.

Thrust (upward force ) – this is the upward force created by the expulsion of hot gas from the firework.

Weight ( downward force ) – this is the force of gravity pulling the firework down to Earth. Weight increases with the mass of the firework.

Drag ( downward force ) – this is the force of air resistance acting in the opposite direction to the motion of the rocket. Air resistance increases with speed.

Resultant Force – for a firework to fly upwards, the thrust must be greater than the weight and drag force together.

RESULTANT FORCE = thrust – ( weight + drag )

The forces acting on a firework change as it travels through the air. The weight decreases as the fuel is used. When the firework is at its maximum speed, the drag force is greatest. When the thrust has been used up, the resultant force becomes downwards as the firework slows down and starts to fall.

One of the easiest ways to demonstrate how fireworks fly and Newton’s Third Law is to set up a rocket made from a film canister or a small effervescent vitamin tablet tube.

Adult supervision is needed for this activity.

Now you know how fireworks fly, discover the chemistry behind their colourful display.

What is a Harvest Moon?

📅 Oct 03, 2025  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A Harvest Moon is what we call the full moon that occurs close to the first day of autumn ( the autumn equinox ).

In 2025, people will be able to see a Harvest Moon on the evening of October 6th. This moon will also be a supermoon, meaning it will be extra bright and big!

Supermoons occur when the moon is at perigee (its closest point to Earth), so it appears bigger and brighter than usual.

The Harvest Moon is named for its bright light, which helped farmers work later into the evening than usual.

A Harvest Moon appears orange when it’s near the horizon, as its light travels through a greater portion of the Earth’s atmosphere.

Harvest moons are sometimes called the Corn Moon or the Barley Moon.

The Harvest Moon can fall in either September or October, depending on when the full moon is closest to the autumn equinox.

Learn about the phases of the moon with moon phase cupcakes!
